# DuckDB — technique cards (155)

Each card is exactly 7 lines. Fetch one with: grep -A 6 "^### duckdb-NNN " duckdb.md

### duckdb-001 — Replace hashing with direct addressing when the key domain is small and dense
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB · Also: libuv, memcached
Principle: If keys are integers in a known small range, use `key - min` as an array index (or a bit index) instead of hashing. Positions replace stored keys, collisions cannot happen, and membership becomes one bit test.
Why it is faster: Hashing, probing, collision chains and key comparison all disappear. Only an offset subtraction and an array load remain. The key does not need to be stored because the slot position implies it, so the table is smaller and denser. The trade-off is memory proportional to the range rather than to the count, so it needs a cap (DuckDB uses 1M build keys, later 8 MiB). Deciding at runtime from the actual min/max, rather than from estimated statistics, makes the fast path fire far more often.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: count by HTTP status, enum or small tenant id with a fixed array, not a `Map`. Store permission sets as bitmasks. • Embedded: index lookup tables by small ids. Use gperf for command and keyword tables in flash. • Native / systems: lexers with perfect-hash keyword tables. Bitsets for small-id membership. • Data / databases: joins to dimension tables keyed by surrogate integers. Histogram buckets by day-of-year.
Watch out: sparse or wide ranges waste memory and cache, so check density and cap the size. Range arithmetic can overflow (DuckDB fixed one such bug in 0f8960ac31). Keep a hash-table fallback.
Seen in DuckDB: 6bc0e1e94d 2020-11-30 Perfect aggregate hash table: group keys with small ranges (known from statistics) map to `(value - min + 1) << shift`, OR-ed across columns. There are no stored groups, only a `group_is_set` array. Follow-ups: 215e7337ac (parallel), 01ab346f73 (early-out in the decision), 9612703695 (configurable max bits). • e8a99670ff 2021-09-07 Perfect hash join for unique integer build keys in `[min,max]`: columns are stored as arrays indexed by key. When the build is dense and every probe key matches, the probe chunk is referenced with zero copies. 1da8d5466e caps the range at 1,000,000. • 74f23629c9 2023-07-24 `fixed_size_map`: an array plus an occupancy bitmask replaces `unordered_map` for small partition indices. 6b317bdfd4 raises the threshold from 64 to 256. • 493905d55b 2023-11-07 Jaccard similarity on characters: a `std::map<char,…>` becomes a 256-bit bitset, so intersection and union are AND/OR plus popcount. • 1ef91baeae 2024-11-28 The perfect hash join is chosen at runtime from the observed build min/max instead of statically. 27885730de skips recomputing min/max when it is already known. • 988d97f315 2026-11-12 Perfect hashing inside the normal hash join, built from actual bounds, with membership bitmaps for SEMI/ANTI/MARK joins and an 8 MiB cap: TPC-DS 4.4% (ARM) and 6.2% (x86) faster. e1dffb0cc8 adds a bitmap path for RIGHT_SEMI/RIGHT_ANTI (Q69 8.3% faster). • 77fa3dffeb 2026-11-10 A generated perfect hash (gperf) for static `date_part` specifier strings replaces serial string comparisons: 0.0587 s to 0.0249 s. • Also seen in libuv (libuv-001): 1282d64868 2012-08-23 The own epoll/kqueue backends use `loop->watchers[fd]` for event dispatch in every backend, grown by `maybe_resize()`/`next_power_of_two()`. `loop->nfds` counts live entries so `uv__io_poll` returns at once when nothing is watched. • 2256be01b0 2018-05-28 `uv__fd_exists()` answers "already registered?" in O(1) with `(unsigned) fd < loop->nwatchers && loop->watchers[fd] != NULL`. It is used by `uv_poll_init`, `uv_pipe_open` and `uv_tcp_open` to return `EEXIST` rather than assert. • c9406ba0e3 2020-12-28 `watchers[fd] != w` checks in `uv_poll_start()`/`uv__io_stop()` make rapid fd reuse safe using the same table. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-007): 70c1b5f61b 2014-01-14 `conns = calloc(max_fds, sizeof(conn *))`; `conn_new` reuses `conns[sfd]` if present. `conn_from_freelist`/`conn_add_to_freelist` and their lock were removed, which "simplifies management of connection descriptors by eliminating use of the freelist".

### duckdb-002 — Turn repeated linear searches into indexed lookups
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB · Also: Aeron, Kafka
Principle: If the same collection is searched over and over, build the index once: a hash set, an inverse map, or binary search over data that is already sorted.
Why it is faster: A search loop over n items called n times is O(n²). One O(n) index build turns every later call into O(1) or O(log n). The pattern often hides in API surfaces that callers invoke per element. It pays off only when lookups repeat; a single lookup is cheaper as a scan.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: enum reverse lookups, de-duplicating in-flight requests by key, uniqueness checks in registries. • Embedded: a sorted array with `bsearch` instead of a tree or a linear scan. • Native / systems: `std::lower_bound` or `bisect` over sorted vectors. Use `try_emplace` instead of `find` plus `insert`. • Data / databases: client drivers that look up metadata per column or per parameter.
Watch out: the index must be invalidated when the collection changes. For tiny collections (fewer than about 8 items) a linear scan is faster than hashing.
Seen in DuckDB: 5ec320e013 2023-11-13 Faster ATTACH: the "is this path already attached?" check walked every attached database. It now uses a case-insensitive set of paths under a small lock. • cd72163fe8 2025-08-11 Find-then-insert double lookups become `emplace` or stored iterators. • 0761d5cc7a 2025-12-24 `duckdb_parameter_name` scanned a name-to-index map for every index. Drivers that call it per parameter made binding O(n²). It now memoizes an index-to-name inverse map. • b90abc1298 2026-02-25 Update-tuple arrays are already sorted, so `FetchRowValidity` uses binary search instead of a linear loop. • Also seen in Aeron to compact, delete-orphaned and catalog initialisation. • b3a85c3312 2024-06-06 and 093276d287 2024-08-13 Replication and the archive client ask the publication for `availableWindow()`/`positionLimit()` instead of linearly searching the counters table by type and registration id inside a wait loop. • c9567be4d3 2024-11-27 `RecordingLog.invalidateEntry(int index)` takes the cache index from callers that are already iterating, instead of walking the cache backwards by key. • Also seen in Kafka (kafka-001): 841d2d1a26 2015-12-15 Streams adds `activeTasksByPartition` and `standbyTasksByPartition`, so each record is routed with one `get(partition)` instead of nested loops over all tasks and their partitions. • 2ef6ee2338 2018-03-29 The controller's `Map[TopicPartition, Seq[Int]]` becomes `Map[String, Map[Int, Seq[Int]]]`, so per-topic operations (topic deletion events) touch only that topic instead of scanning every partition. • 145cad752d 2019-01-07 `ReplicaManager.fetchMessages` replaced a per-partition `fetchInfos.collectFirst { case (tp, v) if tp == topicPartition }` (O(n²)) with a lookup in `logReadResultMap`. • The response is now indexed once as topicId → partition → result. Rare read, initialize and delete paths deliberately keep the scan.

### duckdb-003 — Hunt down hidden quadratic and exponential behavior
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Backtracking matchers, repeated insert-in-the-middle, recursive rewriters and pairwise graph scans are fine on small inputs and catastrophic on large ones. Replace them with linear algorithms: greedy iteration, memoization, or count-then-build.
Why it is faster: These bugs do not show up in average-case benchmarks. They surface as one query that takes 30 seconds instead of a millisecond. Linear rewrites change the complexity class, so the gain grows without bound with input size. Packrat memoization trades memory for a guarantee against exponential backtracking.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: glob and ACL wildcard matching, ReDoS-prone regexes, ORM builders with huge IN lists. • Embedded: iterative matchers also avoid stack overflow on small stacks. • Native / systems: Babel or ESLint AST transforms, packrat PEG parsers, string builders that splice. • Data / databases: planners with many joins or unions, binding of very wide tables.
Watch out: test with adversarial and very large inputs, not just typical ones. Memoization tables can themselves grow large.
Seen in DuckDB: b5a6179327 2026-04-14 GLOB went from a recursive backtracking matcher to the classic greedy iterative algorithm with a single backtrack point. A pathological pattern dropped from 30+ s to 0.001 s. • a51c47e018 2026-05-18 `ExpressionRewriter` became bottom-up and iterative: a post-order stack restarts only the rewritten subtree. Nested macro rewriting is about 4x faster. • fc2148bb2c 2026-07-07 The PEG parser memoizes by (matcher_id, token_idx), which removes exponential backtracking. 3c3387f12b precomputes list groups and leading choices (FIRST sets) to skip impossible branches: 20.7% fewer instructions on TPC-DS. Earlier slices: e1d3f90a7d, 59e5d54714, 1b4c3e54ba. • 469860216f 2026-10-19 Planning had quadratic behavior from column-binding replacement graphs. It went from seconds or minutes to milliseconds. 7ee6e53e91 plans UNION ALL of many subqueries in linear time. • a0f16b1dba 2026-08-25 `StringUtil::Replace` moved the tail on every in-place replace, which is quadratic. It now counts matches, reserves once and appends linearly. 3dc6a1a971: LPAD/RPAD compute the pad geometry once and copy whole pad chunks: `lpad(x,200,'0')` 1.114 s to 0.126 s.

### duckdb-004 — Choose the sort algorithm by key shape, size and presortedness
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: No single sort wins everywhere. Radix sort is best for short fixed-width keys, insertion sort for tiny buckets, branchless pdqsort for tie-heavy or long keys, and run detection for presorted input. A k-way merge moves payload only once.
Why it is faster: Radix sort does no comparisons and runs in O(n·k), but per-bucket histogram overhead dominates on small buckets, and long shared prefixes force many passes. Comparison sorts with branchless partitioning avoid mispredictions on random data. Detecting existing order (vergesort) turns near-sorted input into nearly linear work. Cascaded 2-way merges copy payload log(k) times; a k-way merge copies it once and can be parallelized by splitting output ranges (Merge Path). Comparisons that skip bytes already known to be equal do less work per compare.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: sort fixed-width ids or timestamps with radix sort in typed arrays. Detect already-sorted feeds (TimSort-style) before sorting. • Embedded: insertion sort under about 24 elements, and an explicit stack instead of recursion. • Native / systems: Rust `sort_unstable` (pdqsort) for strings, LSD radix sort for `u32` keys, loser-tree k-way merge in external sorts. • Data / databases: LCP-aware string sorts, and parallel merges split by Merge Path.
Watch out: radix sort needs fixed-width, byte-comparable keys and extra memory. Thresholds such as 24 rows or 4 bytes are machine-dependent, so benchmark them.
Seen in DuckDB: f8b5667395 2020-07-03 Quicksort becomes iterative (an explicit queue, so no stack overflow) with an early-out when every element in a range is equal, which avoids the quadratic case. • b41ec9da35 2021-11-01 Hybrid radix sort: `count <= 24` goes to insertion sort, keys of 4 bytes or fewer to LSD, longer keys to MSD, which recurses only on buckets of more than 24 rows. fb8099deb7 removes the 32-byte key limit. b261647d7c. • 900ee89604 2021-05-27 Full ORDER BY parallelism with Merge Path: each task binary-searches its diagonal and merges an independent, equal-sized slice. • 94bb773716 2022-09-24 When any sort key is VARCHAR, the sort uses `pdqsort_branchless` over row entries, because radix sort on a fixed prefix degrades when many rows share that prefix. • f48f601d8c 2024-05-08 Insertion sort compares only from `col_offset` over `sorting_size`, the part of the key not yet resolved, instead of the full key. • 47599043f0 2025-05-28 New sorting implementation: k-way merge with Merge Path, static fixed-width keys so ska_sort (radix), vergesort (presorted runs) and pdqsort apply, and a LIMIT that can stop the merge early. Ascending 1B UBIGINT 15.3 s to 1.48 s, random 1B 17.6 s to 6.5 s, TPC-H SF100 `l_comment` 300+ s to 35 s, 8-thread scaling 6.5x vs 3.5x. • c9698a3f49 2026-10-19 Heap string comparisons start after the inline prefix that already compared equal: about 30% faster for ~100-byte keys with a ~50-byte common prefix. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-012): fbac1272b89 2004-03-17 Equal keys in btree builds are ordered by TID, avoiding degenerate behavior on many duplicates. • a3f0b3d68f9 2006-03-21 The port qsort replaced an insertion-sort switch that "was in fact a big loss" with a presorted check. • 19c60ad69a9 2020-09-16 `compactify_tuples()` replaced a qsort of item pointers with a presorted check plus a temp-buffer copy that writes tuples back in order, so the next call is likely to hit the fast path; it was prominent in VACUUM and recovery profiles. • ef3c3cf6d02 2026-02-14 Tuplesort radix-sorts pass-by-value keys after normalization, falling back to comparison sort for ties and small inputs; ORDER BY was up to 2x faster on high-cardinality input, and the earlier specialized quicksorts were removed. • f6bd9f0fe25 2026-04-01 Radix sort skips byte positions that are constant across the input, such as the common upper zero bytes of integers. • 6f5ad00ab76 2026-04-07 GIN entry extraction removes NULLs before a templated sort and dedup, so the comparator stays simple.

### duckdb-005 — Encode composite keys into one byte-comparable string
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Transform each ordering column (null flag, sign-flipped big-endian integers, string prefixes, direction) so that comparing the concatenated bytes with `memcmp` gives the correct multi-column order.
Why it is faster: Per-type, per-column comparator dispatch disappears. A compare becomes one `memcmp`, radix sort can work directly on the bytes, and one generic structure (sort, heap, boundary detector) handles every type. The cost is an up-front encoding pass and wider keys. Long strings still need a tie-breaker beyond the prefix.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pack compound sort keys into a `Uint8Array` or string, then sort once with a byte compare. • Native / systems: order-preserving key encodings in KV stores (FoundationDB tuples, CockroachDB keys, RocksDB memcomparable). • Data / databases: group-boundary detection in sorted files, and external sort tools.
Watch out: encodings must get NULL ordering, sign, float edge cases and collations right. Variable-length strings need escaping or a length scheme so that prefixes sort correctly.
Seen in DuckDB: a6ec0f2dbb 2021-04-06 Sort rework (with b77b1fa652, 8a95669145): when all keys are constant-size, `comp_res = memcmp(l_ptr, r_ptr, comparison_size)`. A blob tie-breaker is used only for long strings (c91e3b5430). 8622462574 sets the string radix size to the inline size. • d163f7ee3f 2022-07-18 Window partition and peer boundaries are found by comparing adjacent normalized-key prefixes with memcmp, instead of re-evaluating typed comparisons per column. • 197950408e 2024-10-18 Top-N uses one generic `std::push_heap/pop_heap` over byte-comparable sort keys for all types. `ORDER BY l_orderkey DESC LIMIT 5` (SF10): 0.76 s to 0.24 s. • e83107b03b 2026-08-03 BLOB sort keys must escape bytes 0x00/0x01; SWAR word-at-a-time detection makes building them cheap.

### duckdb-006 — Keep only the best K with a bounded heap and a shared cutoff
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: For "ORDER BY … LIMIT K", keep a K-element heap (O(N log K) time, O(K) memory) instead of sorting everything. Share the current worst-in-heap value across threads and reject rows before doing any expensive work on them.
Why it is faster: A full sort is O(N log N) and needs O(N) memory. A heap does almost nothing for rows that cannot enter it. A global boundary lets every thread discard most rows with one comparison, and checking it before building a sort key avoids paying key-construction cost for rows that will be dropped anyway. When K is a large fraction of N, a full sort wins again.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: leaderboards, "top 10 slowest requests" in APM agents, `heapq.nsmallest`. • Embedded: track the K largest sensor peaks in a fixed array. • Data / databases: search ranking with a threshold (WAND-like), and distributed top-K with per-shard heaps.
Watch out: adversarial input (reverse-sorted) defeats per-thread heaps without a shared boundary. Large K makes the heap slower than sorting.
Seen in DuckDB: b8f04cb340 2019-09-22 Top-K operator for ORDER BY + LIMIT; the message spells out the O(N log K) time and O(K) space argument. • e05075834c 2021-02-19 Each thread buffers only its own top N, and combining is faster because ordering values are cached. 9c57183e68 adds `boundary_values` and `CheckBoundaryValues`, which discard rows worse than the boundary before buffering. • 197950408e 2024-10-18 A real heap of normalized keys with a global boundary shared across threads: `LIMIT 10000` 1.59 s to 0.34 s. 8e2c944aac merges heaps directly in Combine and stops rescanning the heap on each Sink. 6064047fb2 checks the boundary before building sort keys, which fixed a regression. • 59ef36be2f 2025-04-17 Use the Top-N heap only when the limit is under 0.7% of the table; otherwise a full sort is faster. b99d7fda58: merging heaps sorts them instead of pop/push loops, and the scan is parallel: 5.5 s to 2.7 s.

### duckdb-007 — Probe a small array of tagged pointers; keep payloads elsewhere
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Make the probed hash array compact: each slot holds a pointer (or page/offset) plus a few hash bits (a salt). Store full rows separately. Compare salts first, and dereference only on a salt match.
Why it is faster: The probed array stays dense and cache-resident, and the salt rejects almost all mismatches without touching the row, so most cache misses are avoided. Power-of-two capacity turns modulo into a mask. Probing the next slot in a tight inner loop, instead of re-entering a vectorized round per miss, keeps the common case short. An odd step taken from the hash spreads collision clusters, and a lower load factor shortens chains further.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Swiss-table style control bytes in C++ or Rust maps, and tagged pointers in allocators. • Embedded: a compact index plus a separate value log in a key-value store. • Web / backend: custom open-addressing caches keyed by request hash. • Data / databases: fingerprints in cuckoo filters and on-disk indexes.
Watch out: pointer tagging assumes 48-bit address spaces. Linear probing with step 1 clusters badly on sequential keys, so use a good hash or an odd stride.
Seen in DuckDB: 9a932835d0 2019-06-25 Aggregate hash table with `bitmask = size - 1` (power-of-two capacity). 6fcb5ef8e9 uses `NextPowerOfTwo` for the join table too. • f20a1cf94a 2020-10-23 A two-part table: `aggr_ht_entry_64 {uint16 salt; uint16 page_offset; uint32 page_nr}` pointing into buffer-managed row pages. 98f05a435c precomputes salts per batch. 3b183472dd uses the cheaper `salt == hash >> shift`. • c54d7f5968 2023-09-04 The salt moves into the unused upper 16 bits of a 48-bit pointer (`SALT_MASK=0xFFFF000000000000`), so one 8-byte slot holds both. • 33e685b6e9 2023-11-03 Salt-mismatch probing moves into an inner `while(true)` loop. Only salt matches go to key comparison. • 896982b90f 2025-06-02 The probe step becomes `(salt >> 59) | 1` (odd, so it still covers the table) instead of 1, and the load factor goes from 1.25 to 1.5 capacity/count. COUNT(DISTINCT int) about 11% faster, strings about 5%. • 5849139fa0 2025-05-07 Join probing recomputes `ht_offset` and salt from the hash rather than storing and reloading them, because one AND costs about the same as a load.

### duckdb-008 — Spend a few cycles on hash quality to avoid long probe chains
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: A hash that is fast but weak (XOR combining, `h*33+c`, a single multiply) clusters structured keys. Use a proper mix: multiply-then-XOR combining and avalanche finalizers.
Why it is faster: Probe and collision cost grows sharply with clustering, so a hash that is a few cycles slower but well distributed makes the whole table faster. XOR-only combining maps (a,b) and (b,a) to the same value, and equal columns cancel to zero. Sketches such as HyperLogLog also need well-dispersed bits.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: composite cache keys and Java `hashCode` for compound keys (`boost::hash_combine`-style). • Native / systems: SplitMix or Murmur finalizers for integer keys in open-addressing maps, instead of identity hashing. • Data / databases: shard keys and HLL sketches.
Watch out: hash changes can break persisted or cross-version hashes. Measure total table time, not hash throughput alone.
Seen in DuckDB: ef472eeda7 2020-01-03 Combine per-column hashes with multiply and XOR instead of XOR only, and add specialized `tight_loop_hash<T>` loops. • c58ea95740 2021-05-08 String hashing switches from djb2 to Jenkins one-at-a-time plus a 64-bit finalizer. 41518482fa approx_count uses the common fast hash. • 02e395fd33 2022-08-07 The numeric hash goes from one multiply to three xor-shift/multiply rounds: "better but slightly slower". 3056dda649 stops using the system hash for floats. 9dec1c4f7f uses a murmur-style hash for `string_t` keys.

### duckdb-009 — Probe a batch of keys at once with match and no-match lists
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Hash and locate a whole batch of keys, compare them column by column, and move colliding rows to a "no match" list that advances its probe position and repeats. Each inner loop then does one simple thing over many rows.
Why it is faster: Per-row probing interleaves hashing, branching and comparison, so loops are irregular and poorly pipelined. Batched phases are tight loops that the compiler can optimize. Most rows finish in round one, and only the few colliding rows loop again.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: batched lookups in a key-value cache client (resolve all keys, then retry the misses). • Native / systems: hash joins in Rust or Go analytics code, and GPU hash tables. • Data / databases: any vectorized GROUP BY.
Watch out: needs scratch selection arrays per batch. Keep them in operator state rather than allocating them per call.
Seen in DuckDB: 9a932835d0 2019-06-25 The aggregate hash table uses vectorized operations: `templated_compare_group_vector(..., sel_vector, no_match_vector, ...)`. • 7bf9456a3f 2020-01-03 The join hash table switches to "select comparisons for probes" (part of 6fcb5ef8e9).

### duckdb-010 — Keep pre-aggregation tables cache-sized, reset them instead of growing them, and switch them off when they don't help
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: In two-phase aggregation, size each thread-local table to its share of cache. When it fills, clear it and keep going; a later merge removes duplicates. Measure the reduction ratio and grow, shrink or bypass the local phase accordingly.
Why it is faster: Lookups stay in cache and no rehash pass ever runs. Pre-aggregation pays off only if keys repeat locally: with nearly unique keys it is pure overhead. A sample (or an HLL estimate of distinct values) tells which case applies. Wide rows mean fewer entries fit in cache, so the table and partition count should shrink. If one thread built one table and nothing spilled, the merge phase can be skipped.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: per-core counters flushed to a global map, and request coalescing that disables itself under unique traffic. • Native / systems: map-side combiners (Spark, MapReduce, Flink) that turn off when the reduction ratio is low. • Data / databases: GROUP BY in any vectorized engine. Cache admission that stops when the hit rate is near zero.
Watch out: resetting creates duplicate groups, so the merge phase must handle them. Adaptive decisions need a warm-up sample and can misfire on skewed input orders.
Seen in DuckDB: c54d7f5968 2023-09-04 Radix-partitioned hash table rework: `SinkCapacity` comes from the L1/L2/L3 share per active thread. When full, `ClearPointerTable(); ResetCount();` ("This only works because we never resize the HT"). f2066f373b is the cache-size heuristic. • 6b317bdfd4 2023-08-02 With a single thread and no spill, mark all partitions finalized and skip the combine. The table is not reset at all with one active thread. • 8ddb090b56 2024-12-10 Smaller in-sink table and fewer radix partitions for wide rows, plus an unpartitioned buffer for small appends. • 5370afc08e 2024-12-13 After 262,144 tuples of which at least 95% were unique, skip local lookups and pass rows straight to the partitioned phase: TPC-H SF100 distinct query about 5.2 s to 4.5 s. • 96f79c1fae 2025-04-30 After about 1M rows, compare the observed dedup rate with an HLL distinct estimate. Grow the table if it could dedup more than 2x better, or skip lookups if more than 95% are unique. TPC-DS Q67 SF100 about 2x faster. 896982b90f then lowers the initial capacity to 32768 because the table adapts upward.

### duckdb-011 — Size hash tables from known counts and store hashes so growth never rehashes keys
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Collect build rows first and allocate the slot array once the exact count is known. Store each row's hash next to it so resizing or merging redistributes stored hashes instead of recomputing them. When successive tables are needed, double the capacity of each new one.
Why it is faster: Incremental resizing rehashes and moves everything log(n) times. Sizing from the real count gives a guaranteed load factor in one pass. Recomputing hashes over long string keys is expensive, and 8 stored bytes avoid it. Doubling capacities keeps the number of tables and finalization steps logarithmic in the data size.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `new Map()` filled after collecting keys, or `HashMap::with_capacity(n)` when n is known. Cached `hashCode` in immutable objects. • Native / systems: bulk-load indexes after the ETL count is known. • Data / databases: rehash a shard map using stored key hashes.
Watch out: two-phase build needs memory for the staged rows. Stored hashes cost 8 bytes per row, which matters for narrow keys.
Seen in DuckDB: 6fcb5ef8e9 2020-01-03 Join hash table rework: append build rows into blocks, then allocate `NextPowerOfTwo(2*count)` slots ("at least 50% empty space") and insert in vector-sized batches. The stored hash is read back from the rows. 6d1a04e737 allocates blocks from the buffer manager. • 84dd0a646e 2020-09-02 The group hash goes into the payload (`[HASH][GROUPS][PADDING][PAYLOAD]`) "so resize is faster". • 0ed5fcceec 2023-03-28 Exponentially larger successive hash tables (`new_capacity = list.back()->Capacity()*2`, 11ee761e58). ed6780b77b moves `Finalize()` outside the global lock.

### duckdb-012 — Exploit key uniqueness and existence-only semantics in joins
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: If a join only needs to know whether a key exists, store each key once. If the build keys turn out to be unique, every probe row matches at most once, so a single pass can replace a match pass followed by a NULL-fill pass.
Why it is faster: Chaining duplicate keys costs memory and compare-and-swap work for no benefit in SEMI/ANTI/MARK joins. Uniqueness, known for free after the build, removes chain walking and a second pass. Left columns can then be referenced without copying.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: use a `Set` for "WHERE EXISTS"-style checks, and pick a `Map` rather than a multimap path at runtime when the keys are unique. • Data / databases: lookup-table joins in ETL, and pandas `merge(validate='one_to_one')`.
Watch out: multi-condition joins still need all candidates. Uniqueness must be observed, not assumed.
Seen in DuckDB: 2259ad7316 2025-09-26 `insert_duplicate_keys = false` for single-condition SEMI/ANTI/MARK joins: on a key match the row is not chained. • 1d9a0e0c48 2026-03-08 Duplicate chains are also skipped when all predicates are equalities, removing needless CAS work: TPC-DS Q14 about 2.5x faster. • dc1b42d0ad 2026-04-13 `NextUniqueLeftJoin`, gated on `!ht.chains_longer_than_one`: one pass that references the left columns, gathers the right ones and uses predicated `result_count += found_match[j]`.

### duckdb-013 — Join on inequalities by sorting and sweeping, not by nested loops
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: For range or inequality predicates, sort both sides and sweep with pointers (piecewise merge join, IEJoin), using monotonicity so that earlier matches carry forward. Use exponential search and summary bitmaps to skip empty regions.
Why it is faster: A nested loop is O(N·M). After sorting, each side is scanned roughly once and matches are emitted in runs. If left row i matched right row j, all earlier left rows match too, so they can be emitted without comparing. Galloping (exponential then binary) search and a coarse bitmap of non-empty regions skip long stretches. An extra sort column can carry information that would otherwise need a binary search afterwards.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: join events to time windows, and calendar interval-overlap checks. • Native / systems: galloping search in TimSort merges, and two-level free-page bitmaps in allocators. • Data / databases: pandas `merge_asof`, trades-to-quotes joins, genomic range joins.
Watch out: sorting costs more than a nested loop when one side is tiny (DuckDB switches below about 5 rows). Output can still be quadratic when selectivity is low.
Seen in DuckDB: a8612c1ef3 2018-11-27 Piecewise merge join for range joins: quicksort with NULLs removed first, then a merge loop. 4c56c887ad extends it to all join types. • 4060e46673 2022-03-22 IEJoin implementation. 31db2bada7 adds exponential search in `SearchL1` (de440494a1) and a summary bitmap with one bit per 1024 positions (519ce29709). • c990ffc54a 2023-06-15 Piecewise merge join remembers `prev_left_index`: rows before it match the next right row without comparison (3590894a35). • c1cc21e5bb 2023-05-31 AsOf join partitions both sides by the equality keys and sorts partitions in parallel, so the join becomes a merge per partition. • 89ae5e0cb1 2024-10-27 IEJoin carries an extra boolean sort column instead of binary-searching ties afterwards: 2.61 s to 1.55 s.

### duckdb-014 — Update state incrementally as a window slides instead of recomputing it
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: For moving or running aggregates, add the entering values to the previous frame's state and remove the leaving ones. Running aggregates over "unbounded preceding" need only one pass with no materialization.
Why it is faster: Recomputing MODE, MEDIAN or a SUM over every frame costs O(frame) per row. Incremental updates cost O(changed elements). For quantiles, if replacing one element leaves the order valid around the target rank, reselection can be skipped entirely. Streaming windows avoid buffering and sorting the input.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: sliding-window rate limiters, moving averages in trading UIs. • Embedded: rolling-median sensor filters with incremental insert and remove. • Data / databases: rolling metrics in monitoring, running totals in stream processors.
Watch out: removal requires invertible state (counts work, MIN does not without extra structure). Floating-point running sums drift.
Seen in DuckDB: f06500acc6 2021-05-06 New `aggregate_frame_t` API, implemented for MODE: "over 10x perf improvement". 9025bb1279 reduces frame data to a union of both frames. • 46c09fe94c 2021-07-06 Windowed QUANTILE_CONT keeps an index array and replaces only the changed element: 10x for a 100-element windowed MEDIAN over 10M values. 3f8092c150 5% on IQR. d68950d422 moving MAD 8.25 s to 7.42 s. • 000fbffa3d 2021-12-16 `PhysicalStreamingWindow` computes ROW_NUMBER and similar functions with a running counter, without materializing. • c512cd78e5 2022-07-27 Streaming windowed aggregates over "unbounded preceding to current row" keep per-expression aggregate states and update them per chunk.

### duckdb-015 — Answer arbitrary range queries with precomputed trees
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Pre-aggregate the input into a segment tree (aggregates), a merge-sort tree (order statistics, distinct counts) or a skip list (ordered insert and delete). Any frame is then answered in O(log n) or polylog time.
Why it is faster: Frames of varying size defeat simple incremental updates. A tree combines O(log n) precomputed nodes per query. Computing many frames as one batch, and reusing internal nodes shared by neighbouring frames, saves more. A fan-out of 16 balances tree depth against wasted leaf work.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: leaderboard range sums, rolling p95 latency over windows. • Embedded: a Fenwick tree for cumulative counters. • Data / databases: rolling unique visitors over 30 days, range queries in time-series stores.
Watch out: construction costs O(n) to O(n log n) memory and time, which is wasted for fixed-size frames that incremental updates handle.
Seen in DuckDB: 7243ba4ed4 2019-01-02 Segment tree with fan-out 16 for window functions (`Construct`, `Compute(begin,end)`). ad17dee391 rebuilds it on native arrays. • 23392bd924 2023-06-07 A shared selection vector cuts the `ExtractFrame` footprint by 10x and halves benchmark time. 00693c7872 fully vectorizes the computation. 96eca5dbf8 reuses tree aggregations between frames. 86a527b390 fan-out 64 to 16: 10.031 s to 8.350 s. • aad21f3499 2023-11-08 Quantile performance: windowed quantiles move to skip lists (c764e4a37d), then to merge-sort trees (c4b338a338). 9ae5eb7a6f reverts a single-bit PRNG that halved speed. • 9b569a4035 2023-12-19 Windowed DISTINCT aggregates use a merge-sort tree built once per partition instead of a hash set rebuilt per frame.

### duckdb-016 — Detect degenerate cases and use the trivial algorithm
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When the problem collapses (one group, one thread, one key per partition, the same frame for every row), skip the general machinery and do the obvious thing.
Why it is faster: General algorithms pay for hashing, merging, sorting or per-row evaluation that a degenerate input does not need. The check is one comparison, and the saving can be 15x. The risk is only that the special path adds code that needs testing.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a single running total instead of `Map<"all", total>`. Per-group totals computed once (`groupby().transform`). • Native / systems: parallel reduce frameworks that fall back to one pass at parallelism 1. Skip sorting a bucket whose min equals its max. • Data / databases: aggregate per directory in data lakes when the path already encodes the group.
Watch out: every special path must produce exactly the same results, NULL handling included (DuckDB had to fix FILTER handling for the constant window path in 4fe39fdbb6).
Seen in DuckDB: fc19a1e0cb 2019-06-25 An aggregate without GROUP BY uses a single state with no hash table (`SimpleAggregate`). • d7408d0252 2023-03-23 When every row in a partition has the same frame, compute the aggregate once per partition and copy it: "about 15x faster". • 6b317bdfd4 2023-08-02 A single thread with one table skips the combine phase. • 2852d0f9e6 2024-10-12 If the input is already partitioned by the GROUP BY keys (for example Hive partitions), each partition is one group: run an ungrouped aggregate per partition with no hash table. • 65d2610cf5 2026-06-26 `PartitionKeyTracker` detects hash partitions that hold a single key and skips sorting them: partitioned COPY of TPC-H SF10 about 4.8 s to 4.1 s.

### duckdb-017 — Use small probabilistic sketches for counts and heavy hitters
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When an estimate is good enough, a fixed-size sketch replaces exact state: HyperLogLog for distinct counts, Space-Saving with a counting filter for top-k.
Why it is faster: Sketch memory is constant regardless of input size, updates are a hash plus a register update, and sketches merge cheaply across threads. They feed statistics, adaptive decisions and file-flush heuristics. Splitting HLL insertion into tight passes (hash all, then compute register and leading zeros, then update) vectorizes it. A small hashed counter filter in front of Space-Saving avoids churning the monitored map.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: unique-visitor counts, trending topics, TinyLFU-style cache admission. • Embedded: heavy-hitter detection in network monitoring with fixed memory. • Data / databases: cardinality statistics for planners, and partition-count estimates for writers.
Watch out: errors compound when estimates feed other estimates. Sketch hashing needs well-mixed bits.
Seen in DuckDB: 5bf7da0881 2022-05-12 Vectorized multi-pass HLL that always uses the dense representation so the update inlines (1834595222, e72dca6dc7). Its distinct counts feed column statistics and join cardinality estimation. • a14722b69b 2024-06-23 Filtered Space-Saving: a filter of `NextPowerOfTwo(capacity*8)` counters indexed by hash admits a value only when its count beats the current minimum. • 96f79c1fae 2025-04-30 HLL estimates decide whether to grow or bypass pre-aggregation tables. • aeb931a72c 2026-05-08 Partitioned writes estimate the number of distinct partitions with HLL and flush only when expected rows per partition reach a threshold.

### duckdb-018 — Drive substring search with memchr and recognize pattern shapes
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Jump to candidate positions of the needle's first byte with the platform's vectorized `memchr`, then verify with a word-sized compare and a `memcmp` tail. Hoist per-row pattern work (lowercasing, compiling) out of the loop, and recognize regex shapes that are really substring extraction.
Why it is faster: libc `memchr` is SIMD-optimized and skips most bytes. Comparing short needles as one integer replaces byte loops. Static dispatch to a library search beats virtual per-character iterators. A regex like `^P(G).*$` with `\1` is an anchored match followed by a substring, which can be returned without copying.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `indexOf` over hand loops; compile `RegExp` once outside the loop; rewrite `s.replace(/^(\w+).*$/,'$1')` as a match plus slice. • Embedded: command parsers matching short tokens as integers. • Native / systems: grep-like tools, log filters, HTTP header matching in proxies.
Watch out: word-sized compares need care with alignment and string ends. Case folding is only byte-simple for ASCII.
Seen in DuckDB: 29d7fe94e0 2020-11-01 `ContainsSmall<UNSIGNED, NEEDLE_SIZE>` (inspired by FreeBSD memmem) with a sliding integer window, plus a rolling-sum `ContainsGeneric`. cf20d4b40d adds a memchr skip to the first byte and aligned 2/4/8-byte needles. • 8e1076366c 2022-11-04 String split drops a virtual `StringSplitIterator` hierarchy for a templated `Find` that uses `ContainsFun::Find`, appending results directly into the list child vector. • 5ca240ba2a 2025-03-03 One `memchr`-driven path replaces three hand-tuned ones: `%google.com%` 0.43 s to 0.32 s on ClickBench hits. • da40609cca 2026-06-23 Constant-pattern ILIKE lowercases once, reuses a scratch buffer and dispatches to SIMD `FindStrInStr`: 2.4 s to 0.9 s. • bea13c1574 2026-05-01 `regexp_replace` recognizes anchored capture-then-truncate patterns. Dropping the trailing `.*$` gave about 70% of the gain and zero-copy substitution about 30%.

### duckdb-019 — Build indexes bulk-wise from (approximately) sorted input
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Instead of inserting rows one by one into a shared tree, sort locally, bulk-construct sub-trees bottom-up per thread, and merge them. Even an approximate sort gives most of the locality benefit.
Why it is faster: Random-order inserts touch random nodes, which is cache-hostile, and need serialization on a shared index. Sorted input builds nodes sequentially and lets threads work independently. Sorting has its own cost, so whether it pays depends on key type and on how cheap the sort is.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: bulk-load B-trees from sorted runs. Sort by Morton or Hilbert key before building spatial indexes. • Web / backend: build autocomplete tries offline from sorted terms. • Data / databases: per-segment inverted indexes merged later (Lucene), pre-sorted LSM ingestion.
Watch out: re-measure when the sort implementation changes, as DuckDB's reversal shows: the decision flipped as the cost of sorting changed.
Seen in DuckDB: a0fc1e9cd0 2022-09-24 Parallel CREATE INDEX: each thread sorts, builds a local ART with `ConstructAndMerge`, then merges into the global index. 4f495e8eea allows parallel batch insertion into indexed tables. • e4c51a8fb5 2023-01-12 Further parallelizes index creation by moving a parallel sort into the operator. • bfd297cba5 2023-08-07 Stops sorting VARCHAR and compound keys because the sort cost exceeded the benefit (`SinkSorted` vs `SinkUnsorted`), and frees nodes non-recursively. • 5dcf752260 2026-03-31 With a cheaper approximate sort, ART creation always sorts again, VARCHAR included: about 65 s to about 7 s. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-020): 89bda95d820 2006-04-01 Catalog indexes are built by sorting instead of incremental inserts, giving about 10% smaller initial states. • 787eba734be 2008-03-16 Hash index builds pre-sort entries by bucket when the index exceeds effective_cache_size, to avoid thrashing. • f1ea98a7975 2022-02-07 The sorted GiST build accumulates 4 pages and applies the opclass picksplit, reducing non-leaf key overlap; 7f3bd86843e added a double-sorting split for better trees. • e09d7a1262c 2022-07-28 Hash index builds sort by bucket and then by hash value, avoiding data movement in `PageAddItem` (5-9%); d09dbeb9bde appends at page end with no binary search (a further 5-15%).

### duckdb-020 — Append to a vector and normalize once instead of maintaining an ordered set
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When you only need the sorted, de-duplicated result at the end, append values to a flat array and sort plus unique once (or use a bitmap if the values are dense). Do not pay for tree insertion on every element.
Why it is faster: `std::set` allocates a node and rebalances per insert, with poor cache behavior. Appending is amortized O(1), and one sort runs at memory speed. Detecting already-sorted or reverse-sorted input makes the final pass linear. Batching many lookups also lets one scan state serve all of them.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: collect ids into an array, then sort and dedupe, instead of a `Set` plus sort for large batches. • Data / databases: search engines collecting posting ids, and stream processors de-duplicating event ids.
Watch out: only valid if nobody needs the ordered view while you are still inserting. Peak memory includes duplicates.
Seen in DuckDB: ecf3b9fcf1 2026-09-03 The ART row-id collector appends and then normalizes: it detects sorted or reverse order, uses a bitmap if dense, and otherwise pdqsort plus unique. `point_query_with_art_and_many_keys` 41.93 to 21.66 (48% faster). • 36e7b0097a 2026-09-16 ART batch equality scan: one scan state and one key generation for many lookups.

### duckdb-021 — Partition a batch with counting sort into one selection array
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB
Principle: To scatter rows into P partitions, count per partition, prefix-sum into offsets, fill one index array in a single pass, and then append each partition's contiguous slice. If the whole batch maps to one partition, exit early.
Why it is faster: Per-partition staging buffers add a copy and cost memory proportional to the partition count. A counting pass gives exact offsets with no reallocation, and slices append directly to their destinations.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: route log lines by tenant into per-destination batches without intermediate copies. • Native / systems: radix partitioning in GPU kernels, and shuffles in distributed engines. • Data / databases: partitioned writers and hash joins.
Watch out: needs two passes over the partition ids, which is cheap but not free for tiny batches.
Seen in DuckDB: 7354665c47 2022-10-03 `PartitionedColumnData::AppendChunk` uses `partition_entries` (offset, length), a shared `partition_sel` and a `size() == 1` early-out. • 32ad2126cc 2023-03-28 Tuple Data Collection deletes `partition_buffers` and their flush logic, slicing and appending straight into partitions. 191fa78639 computes hive partition keys per vector instead of building per-row `Value`s.

### duckdb-022 — Compute a position directly instead of walking to it
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB · Also: TigerBeetle
Principle: If an offset can be derived arithmetically (bit-packed skips, fixed-width units, cumulative segment counts), compute it in closed form or seek with metadata. Do not step through the preceding elements.
Why it is faster: Walking costs O(k) and often touches memory or pins buffers along the way. A formula or metadata sum costs O(1) or O(segments). For time truncation, one floor division replaces a calendar decomposition and recomposition.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: bucket timestamps by integer division, and cursor-based pagination instead of OFFSET walking. • Embedded: seek in fixed-width record files by multiplication. • Data / databases: offset indexes in log files, and footer-indexed range reads.
Watch out: closed forms are only valid for fixed-width units. Calendar months, time zones and DST still need the full computation.
Seen in DuckDB: 848dc0ebc9 2026-02-09 The Parquet bit-pack skip goes from an inner while-loop to a division per value. 64c6ef7c92 then does a single `total_bits` computation for the whole skip. • 8404a926e0 2026-05-05 `date_trunc` for hour, minute, second, ms and day uses `TruncFixed(ts, interval_us)`: 531 ms to 214 ms (2.48x). • c3e3615624 2025-11-14 `TupleDataCollection::Seek()` sums segment counts to reach chunk k instead of scanning and pinning chunks 0..k-1 (IEJoin `AppendKey`). • 7292ea53c1 2025-11-26 WAL `ReplayDelete` deletes in batches instead of row by row. • Also seen in TigerBeetle (tigerbeetle-013): 6ac9bf9e0c 2022-06-02 "VSR: Optimize pipeline lookup ... directly index into the pipeline. This is safe because the pipeline is always continuous and ordered." 65c57febca adds `RingBuffer.get(index)`. • 7ea1d8f048 2022-06-02 Fixed-size WAL slots: `offset = size_headers + message_size_max * (op % message_slots)`, deleting `next_offset()` and 60 lines of overlap repair.

### duckdb-023 — Use known-better algorithms for big-number arithmetic and conversion
Lens: algorithms · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Replace digit-by-digit or byte-at-a-time big-number code with the textbook algorithms and full machine words.
Why it is faster: Repeated division by 10 is O(n²) in the number of digits. Knuth's methods batch digits. 64-bit limbs do 8x the work per instruction compared with bytes, while the storage format can stay unchanged.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: BigInt polyfills, decimal libraries in finance backends. • Native / systems: crypto and arbitrary-precision libraries.
Watch out: limb arithmetic needs careful carry handling and endianness-aware conversion to the stored format.
Seen in DuckDB: cb05b0d2f1 2025-01-13 VARINT to VARCHAR uses Knuth TAOCP 4.4 Method 1b (as CPython does): 3.22 s to 0.72 s. • f177c34f79 2026-11-09 BIGNUM on 64-bit limbs: `sum` 2.4x, add/subtract 1.5-1.7x, multiplication 3.7-4.4x, to VARCHAR 4-9x. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-022): d72f6c75038 2003-03-21 NUMERIC was rebuilt on base-10000 digits, about 10x on its regression test. • e7a9ccdb6c5 2006-02-07 `cmp_numerics` compares packed digits without converting; a 1M-row CREATE INDEX went from about 37.6 s to 18.8 s on a Pentium III and from 15.4 s to 7.3 s on Itanium2. • 9cca11c915e 2016-09-02 `NumericSumAccum` uses 32-bit slots for base-10000 digits so up to NBASE-1 values add before a carry pass, keeps positive and negative sums apart, and allocates nothing per value. • 4083f445c0c 2020-03-28 Karatsuba square root replaced Newton iteration: 3-5x faster for small inputs and about 10x for large; `ln()` now computes only the digits it needs. • d1b307eef28 2022-02-27 `div_var_int()` short division for 1-2 digit divisors speeds up exp and ln; 0aa38db56bf added an int128 version for 3-4 digits. • 8dc28d7eb86 2024-08-15 `mul_var` multiplies in base NBASE² with 64-bit products, 3-6x faster on large inputs; ca481d3c9ab added a short-operand path; 9428c001f67 unified division, up to 20x faster than Knuth division when exact.

### duckdb-024 — Push filters as close to the data as possible, and derive new ones
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Move predicates through joins, aggregates, DISTINCT, set operations, unnest and windows down to the scan. Merge redundant predicates. Derive implied predicates through equalities (`a=b AND b=5` gives `a=5`) and factor single-column filters out of disjunctions.
Why it is faster: Each operator above a filter processes rows that will later be discarded. Filtering at the scan shrinks every downstream intermediate, enables block skipping, and reduces join and hash-table sizes. Transitive filters give the other side of a join the same reduction. Pulling filters up first can expose more pushdown opportunities. Volatile functions and outer-join semantics limit where filters can legally move.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: push WHERE conditions into REST or GraphQL data sources, and propagate `user_id=…` into joined fetches. • Native / systems: filter early in stream pipelines and before shuffles. • Data / databases: ORM-generated SQL, dataframe query planners (Polars), magic-sets-style constant propagation in Datalog.
Watch out: pushing through outer joins, non-deterministic functions or side-effecting expressions changes results. Every move needs a semantic guard.
Seen in DuckDB: b812f1515f 2018-09-13 Selection pushdown through joins and cross products. 82543c8c6c merges predicates on the same column and removes obsolete filters. • e01ff03c41 2019-03-05 `FilterCombiner` merges into FilterPushdown. e06a3e30c7 pushes through DISTINCT and aggregates. 4b5322a21a pushes LEFT JOIN conditions on the left side into the right side. • 77599e4ae9 2020-10-01 Transitive filters (af51d6d28a). ded5129add adds a Filter Pull Up optimizer so more pushdown is possible. • 5a7807c5cf 2021-01-05 Filter pull-up through set operations and projections. c1fd56df6a pushes filters to both sides of a join when they are on the join key. • 67f8cf39f5 2024-09-19 Push through UNNEST. 19c3f0358b window partitions, 3d805499b6 semi/anti joins, 189f92936a LIMIT below PROJECT, d4ed7c77c2 function stability so `NOW()` can be pushed down. • 84bd10b122 2025-08-13 Pushes filters on `COALESCE(l.k, r.k)` of full outer joins. f0428d52a6 pushes single-side ON-clause filters for all join types. • 457b1aceea 2026-06-03 Join filter mirroring extends to non-volatile expressions on join columns. c6a398eea4 `PredicateFactoringRule`: `(a=1 AND x) OR (a=2 AND y)` yields `a IN (1,2)`, which can reach the scan.

### duckdb-025 — Read and carry only the columns that are still needed
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Remove unreferenced columns at the scan, and drop columns from intermediates as soon as no later operator uses them. This includes filter-only columns, unused struct fields, and columns that would otherwise be spilled.
Why it is faster: Every carried column costs decode, copy, hash-table width, sort payload and spill bytes. Columnar storage makes unread columns free only if the plan never asks for them. Pass-through projections cost per-chunk work and block other rewrites. Moving cheap expressions below joins helps only if it frees input columns.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: GraphQL field selection passed down to SQL, and explicit `SELECT` lists instead of `*` in ORMs. • Native / systems: strip unused DTO fields before serialization. Tree-shaking. • Data / databases: drop columns before shuffles and before `df.merge`, and use projections on MongoDB subdocuments.
Watch out: column indices shift as rewrites reorder columns, so projection maps must be recomputed after each optimizer pass.
Seen in DuckDB: 1efe138540 2020-01-06 `RemoveUnusedColumns`. 4b7513e981 `ColumnLifetimeOptimizer` drops columns after joins and filters. 6befce1fd9 handles UNION. 1b3016a5de avoids unnecessary projections in DISTINCT. • 9f84ca4086 2022-10-06 Separate "columns to read" and "columns to output" so filter-only columns leave the pipeline after the scan. a500fcb1c9 pushes column-only projections below LIMIT. • 94bb773716 2022-09-24 Unused columns are pruned before ORDER BY, so less payload is moved during the sort. • 7d9ddfa1af 2024-10-29 Hash-join `left_projection_map` drops probe-side columns before they might be spilled. • da7bc76970 2024-11-08 Recursive `ColumnIndex` reads only referenced struct children and fills the rest with constant NULL. 00e9fb9364 does it for Parquet, 0b7b67dc84 for JSON. • 7ca142de46 2026-02-19 `ProjectionPullup` removes column-reference-only projections. 3914ad4d9c pulls up computed projections. 94addef607 turns `GROUP BY x, x-1, cast(x)` into `GROUP BY x`. 5bb2658ebd prunes through materialized CTEs. 3ad79f7175 stops disabling pruning below ROLLUP, which had been downloading every column of remote tables. • e54bd57cc4 2026-11-12 Does not push cheap expressions (cost of 20 or less) below joins unless that frees an input: TPC-DS q66 SF3000 1.19 s to 0.62 s.

### duckdb-026 — Keep per-block min/max summaries and skip blocks that cannot match
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Record cheap statistics (min, max, null and all-null information) per file, row group, segment and page. Before reading a block, test the pushed filters against them, and skip whole files, groups or pages that cannot contain a match.
Why it is faster: Skipping a block avoids its I/O, decompression and filter evaluation all at once. It works best on data that is sorted or clustered by the filter column, and is useless on random data. Synthesized statistics (row ids are sequential) and path-encoded partition values extend it to virtual columns and files. For Top-N, the same statistics decide which blocks to scan first, so the heap fills with good values early and more blocks become provably irrelevant.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip time-partitioned log files whose [min_ts, max_ts] miss the query, and serve "latest 100" from the newest chunk. • Embedded: per-page min/max headers in flash-resident logs. • Native / systems: per-tile bounding boxes in GIS renderers. • Data / databases: Postgres BRIN, Parquet/ORC/Iceberg statistics, partition pruning in data lakes.
Watch out: statistics must be maintained or conservatively widened on updates. Truncated string bounds must round in the safe direction. Order-changing casts (such as to boolean) invalidate min/max propagation (86b509d2ac).
Seen in DuckDB: 848a097fd1 2020-04-02 `DataTable::CheckZonemap` skips segments whose [min,max] cannot satisfy `col op constant`. d37cb5e7e2 adds strings (min/max truncated to 8 bytes). 1187b356e2 persists statistics on disk. • 1f52b8467b 2021-05-27 Skips whole row groups using per-group statistics. cb67efd811 skips segments within a row group. da37337127 handles NOT EQUAL. • 77592d18a8 2022-08-08 Hive/filename partition filter pushdown drops files before opening them. eebd336fb5 tracks file filters in the scan. • 83822080bf 2024-12-04 Synthesized exact min/max for rowid per row group: a point lookup `WHERE rowid=500` on SF10 went from about 0.29 s to near-instant. • ead3ca87f7 2025-11-01 Top-N scans row groups ordered by min (ASC) or max (DESC): TSBS `ORDER BY time DESC LIMIT 100` 0.7 s to 0.02 s cold. 6c5e16c2fb excludes row groups by overlap reasoning. aa588f86ae applies it to `LIMIT M OFFSET N`: offset 730K went from 11 s to 0.05 s. • 1c0f8ace6d 2026-05-04 `CanSkipFileFromFilters` runs before creating a reader: 8.54 s to 2.57 s. 27a2f193b8 Parquet page statistics skip. 6dd33fcd90 pending skips avoid page I/O entirely. 69496c8342 all-NULL statistics give a constant NULL without decoding.

### duckdb-027 — Rewrite predicates into forms that block-skipping can use
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Turn predicates that statistics cannot check (IN-lists, ORs, inequalities, functions of columns) into conservative range or set filters on bare columns. Push those down for pruning and keep the exact predicate above.
Why it is faster: Zone maps and indexes only understand `column op constant`. `date_trunc('day', ts) >= C`, `x IN (3,4,5)` or `floor(x) > c` look opaque until they are mapped back to a column range. A derived filter can be "optional": used only to skip blocks and never evaluated per row, so a weak one costs almost nothing.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: rewrite `WHERE YEAR(ts)=2024` as a range on `ts` so the B-tree index applies. Turn prefix searches into range scans. • Data / databases: partition pruning with derived predicates, and time-series stores mapping `date_trunc` filters to chunk ranges.
Watch out: derived filters must be strictly conservative (a superset of the matches). Non-monotone functions, collations and float rounding break naive range mapping.
