### duckdb-028 — Order joins by cost with exact search, bounded by a budget
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Enumerate join orders with dynamic programming over connected subgraphs (DPccp/DPhyp), each pair exactly once. When the search space exceeds a budget, fall back to greedy ordering.
Why it is faster: Join order decides intermediate sizes, which can differ by orders of magnitude. Exact DP is optimal under the cost model but exponential, and past about 12 relations planning time exceeds the gain. Canonical enumeration avoids duplicate work and missed neighbours. Bringing LEFT joins into the enumerator widens the search.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: order microservice fan-out calls by expected result size. • Native / systems: time-boxed planners that fall back to heuristics. Bitmask DP with canonical enumeration orders. • Data / databases: dataframe join ordering and federated query planners.
Watch out: an exact search over bad estimates is still a bad plan. Planning time is part of query latency on small queries.
Seen in DuckDB: 267bdeb227 2018-12-10 Initial join order optimizer, with filter pushdown during reordering in e880890257. The source at 57b2d0f995 is "a straight implementation of the paper 'Dynamic Programming Strikes Back'" and switches to Greedy Operator Ordering at 2000 pairs. f27e8d387f fixes plan costs. • a38bdd1aa6 2022-08-25 Cardinality estimator over equivalence sets, cost = cardinality plus children, greedy above 10,000 pairs. eecef371db prunes redundant CSG/CMP enumeration. • aa4a965634 2023-06-23 A `NeighborSubset` class enumerates subsets from small to large, fixing duplicate enumerations and lost neighbours. fbcb140544 re-runs join ordering after the deliminator removes DELIM_GETs. • ae2178ec2c 2024-11-24 Switches to greedy eagerly at about 12 relations: IMDB 27a 0.060 s to 0.031 s, 27b 0.071 s to 0.025 s. • 8cab422a70 2025-06-27 Caps join-order search depth. • a504c24a47 2026-05-29 LEFT joins participate in DP enumeration. d5bb9ca2c2 fixes the cardinality lower bound.

### duckdb-029 — Feed the cost model every cheap signal about sizes
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Estimate cardinalities from distinct-count sketches, min/max domain sizes, file sizes, cached metadata and block-level statistics. Default selectivity guesses mislead join ordering badly.
Why it is faster: A poor estimate can choose a plan that spills terabytes. Signals that are already available (HLL, footer metadata, glob listings, zone maps) make estimates tighter at almost no cost. Bounding a filter's estimate by the rows in blocks that could match gives an exact upper limit.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: cost-based API gateways that estimate result sizes from id ranges. • Native / systems: schedulers that estimate task cost from input file sizes. • Data / databases: planners over CSV or S3 estimating rows from byte sizes, and BRIN-informed estimates.
Watch out: proxies such as the min/max domain overestimate distinct counts on sparse data. Combine signals as bounds, not as replacements.
Seen in DuckDB: 5bf7da0881 2022-05-12 HLL distinct statistics stored per column feed the total-domain estimates used by the cardinality estimator in a38bdd1aa6. • 6c5b04e096 2025-04-22 Better estimates for inequality joins, and 3c0d466943 for aggregates. • 2076dd79b5 2026-04-21 Multi-table OR predicates count as selectivity: TPC-DS Q85 at SF1000 went from a timeout with 1.3 TB spilled to about 300 s. • bd6af0e452 2026-06-08 `max-min+1` as a distinct-count proxy for integral columns without HLL (Parquet, Iceberg, Delta). a641c01909 estimates rows per file from glob sizes. ba60b17cea uses cached Parquet metadata. • 74d82475fb 2026-11-17 Scan estimates bounded by rows in row groups whose zone maps can satisfy the filter. 445271a96e caps GROUP BY estimates by the key domain.

### duckdb-030 — Pick the build side by size, and by what it lets you filter
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: The materialized (build) side of a hash or nested-loop join should normally be the smaller input; flip outer-join types to allow it. But prefer a selective build side when its runtime filter can prune a much larger probe-side scan.
Why it is faster: Build-side size sets hash-table memory and spill risk. Swapping LEFT to RIGHT (with a "found" flag to emit unmatched build rows) keeps the small side in memory. The cheapest build is not always best: a selective build's key summary pushed into a large scan can skip most of the I/O.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in in-memory joins, build the `Map` from the smaller list. Fetch the selective list first and pass its ids as `IN`. • Data / databases: broadcast the small table in Spark, and push its keys as a Bloom filter to scanners.
Watch out: swapping changes output order and outer-join semantics, so the executor must support the mirrored join type.
Seen in DuckDB: 7ae4fdde03 2020-11-09 When the right side is more than 2x larger, swap the children of a LEFT join and execute it as a RIGHT OUTER join. 33e9539445 adds the `left_outer_join_right_big` benchmark. • d1d6ccab47 2023-05-11 The same swap for non-equi ANY joins (0322fb5efe). • 5f297cd66a 2025-08-13 Flips delim joins by cardinality to reduce memory pressure. • c518cf52c2 2026-05-13 Multiplies build cost by a dynamic-filter benefit: TPC-DS SF100 cold Q13 57.2 s to 11.6 s, Q85 13.3 s to 4.5 s.

### duckdb-031 — Pass runtime join filters sideways into the probe-side scan
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: After building a hash table you know its keys. Push a summary of them (min/max range, a small IN-list, a Bloom filter, a prefix-range bitmap) into the other side's scan so non-matching rows and blocks are dropped before the join.
Why it is faster: Probe rows that cannot match still cost decode, pipeline work and a hash probe. A range filter enables zone-map skipping of whole files. An IN-list catches few-but-scattered keys. A cache-line-blocked Bloom filter costs a few branchless instructions per row and is far smaller than the table. Choose the cheapest filter that also prunes, pause filters that do not pay off, and schedule probes only after the filters are built.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: check a Bloom filter of existing ids before hitting the database. Add `WHERE id BETWEEN min AND max` to the second query of a two-step fetch. • Native / systems: LSM per-SSTable Bloom filters, and ad-serving candidate pruning. • Data / databases: Spark and Trino dynamic filtering, and shipping Bloom filters to remote scanners in distributed joins.
Watch out: filters cost time to build and probe. Measure their selectivity and disable them adaptively, and do not stack redundant filters. Probes that start before the filter exists gain nothing.
Seen in DuckDB: 9937fcf25a 2024-07-09 Pushes build-side min/max into probe-side scans. 294b622c91 also prunes Hive partitions and files with it. • b470dea7ee 2024-11-18 IN filter when the hash table has at most 50 rows, pushed as an optional (zone-map-only) filter: 0.22 s to 0.04 s on SF10. • cb8ecb6b96 2025-01-10 Index scans move to scan-init time so dynamic join filters can drive ART lookups. 0c964e61dc extends that to IN filters. • 7d6adf970f 2025-12-02 Register-blocked Bloom filter: 64-bit sectors, cache-line-aligned, 12 bits per key, branchless lookup accumulated into a selection vector, paused when selectivity is too high. Probing is 2-4x faster than the hash table and about 5x smaller; IMDB 13-17% faster. 4c3627e963 uses it to skip row groups with tight ranges. • 60efcc9d7d 2025-05-02 Inequality-derived bounds pushed into nested-loop joins (about 10x). e144689926 piecewise merge join, 30e907b52f IEJoin, 1fdb10c960 IN pushdown only for equalities. • ff4f70eeee 2026-03-17 Prefix Range Filter, a bitmap over key bit-prefixes with no false negatives that also supports min/max pruning: 10x on a microbenchmark. 48837b3d3f VARCHAR via inline prefixes. 83ae79e5b7 probes the perfect-hash array directly. 35b8279eba selection policy: 50 keys or fewer use IN, range below 2^26 uses exact PRF, otherwise the smaller of PRF and Bloom. 5909259229 defers pushdown until the filter is built. bd5ed38448 pushes through casts. • b678afa97b 2026-11-13 Per-key-column filters for multi-key joins, and 3932b00c0b for spilling joins. dd28194097 IN threshold on distinct values (Iceberg files read 1,586 to 434). ed0443f18a pushes through aggregations and windows. 9a5d13571d adaptive min/max filters measured on real rows. 5c8008b4cf orders small filter builds before scans.

### duckdb-032 — Publish a tightening Top-N boundary as a live scan filter
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: While an ORDER BY … LIMIT runs, the worst value in the heap only gets better. Expose it as a shared, mutable filter that the scan checks, so blocks and rows that cannot beat it are skipped.
Why it is faster: Without feedback the scan reads everything. With a live boundary, zone maps skip whole row groups once the heap is good, and especially if good blocks are read first. Updates need a lock or atomic, but they happen rarely.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: "load more" requests passing the last seen key as a filter. • Native / systems: k-nearest-neighbour search with a shrinking radius. • Data / databases: WAND/MaxScore minimum competitive score in search engines, and LIMIT with ORDER BY in KV stores skipping SSTables by max timestamp.
Watch out: NULL ordering and float edge cases must be encoded correctly in the filter. Contention appears if the boundary updates on every row.
Seen in DuckDB: a987c5afa6 2024-12-03 `DynamicFilter` holds a `shared_ptr<DynamicFilterData>` (child filter plus lock), updated on each Sink. • e2b7d36007 2025-05-15 Applies Top-N dynamic filters to pre-existing TopN nodes. 9c7844ee6e NULLS FIRST support. ca7af7ffdc keeps it when cardinality is unknown. 9a48abd913 extends to FLOAT/DOUBLE.

### duckdb-033 — Push predicates, projections, casts and field extraction into the data source
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Let the reader evaluate filters, decode filter columns first, and decode other columns only for surviving rows. It should also return exactly the type and sub-field the query needs.
Why it is faster: Inside the reader, a failing row costs only its filter columns, and the reader can evaluate on encoded data (dictionaries, statistics). Casting once in bulk, or natively in the reader, beats converting per vector afterwards. Extracting one struct or variant field avoids materializing the whole nested value.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: S3 Select or OData `$filter`. Check a discriminant before decoding the rest of a JSON body. • Native / systems: simdjson on-demand path selection, and protobuf partial decode. • Data / databases: GraphQL resolvers passing field selections to SQL, and Parquet/ORC late column decode.
Watch out: readers must preserve the exact semantics of casts and NULLs. A reader that cannot evaluate a predicate must say so, and the filter must stay above it.
Seen in DuckDB: 7a9c76e4ba 2020-12-03 Parquet filter pushdown: scan filter columns first, build a mask, stop when it becomes empty, and skip filtered values during dictionary and plain decode. abce2722a1 projection pushdown into pandas scans. • 2f159aebc6 2021-07-08 Projection pushdown into Arrow scans. 911c081f0a filter and projection pushdown to Arrow. 4b5e920b69 date filters in Parquet. 83c30b3202 decimal filters. • 0069af20ab 2025-04-23 `pushdown_expression` callback for arbitrary predicates: `UserId::VARCHAR LIKE '%9999999%'` 3.6 s to 0.11 s (DuckDB storage), 3.7 s to 0.32 s (Parquet). • 06c9a25e3a 2025-12-17 `struct_extract` pushed into storage, emitting only the referenced field. f6fad205ea pushes a CAST on top of it. • 4f6deb4926 2026-06-26 `CAST(col,T)` becomes `GET(col as T)` (Parquet, Vortex). bca135e67f `struct_extract` into Parquet. d8ea37cf71 `variant_extract` plus cast into storage, skipping unshredding. d25d62a258 skips struct validity. 18b593788d removes a call that forced unshredding.

### duckdb-034 — Late materialization: choose rows on narrow data, then fetch wide rows by id
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: For Top-N, LIMIT/OFFSET or SAMPLE, first compute which rows win using only the sort key plus a row id, then fetch the full payload for just those rows. Do this only when it actually avoids reading columns.
Why it is faster: Wide payload columns are otherwise read, decompressed and carried for rows that are thrown away. The second fetch is a few point lookups. If the payload columns must be read anyway (for example because they are filtered), the rewrite adds a useless re-scan.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: paginated APIs that select ids ordered by created date with LIMIT/OFFSET, then hydrate the rows by id. • Native / systems: sort an index array of [key, idx] pairs instead of large objects. • Data / databases: search engines ranking on doc ids, fetching stored fields for the top-k only.
Watch out: random point fetches are expensive on remote storage. Gate the rewrite on a row threshold and on actual column savings.
Seen in DuckDB: 2a1d5e2ab8 2025-01-14 Rewrites `SELECT * … ORDER BY x LIMIT 5` into `WHERE rowid IN (SELECT rowid … LIMIT 5)`: Top-N 0.07 s to 0.01 s, LIMIT/OFFSET 2.8 s to 0.14 s, sample 0.46 s to 0.07 s. e0d79a7eb0 adds the `late_materialization_max_rows` threshold. • ced12aa42a 2025-05-02 Extends to Parquet with (`file_index`, `file_row_number`) as a composite row id: 0.53 s to 0.06 s. 664366d25a adds `read_duckdb`. b276d53629 and 19e969f163 apply it in the TopN-window rewrite. • ff2cd933f0 2026-04-28 Counts filter columns as "must read" before deciding, so ClickBench Q24 no longer gets a useless semi-join and re-scan.

### duckdb-035 — Propagate value statistics to select cheaper kernels
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Carry min/max, null presence, maximum length and an ASCII-only flag through the plan. When they prove that a condition (overflow, NULL, non-ASCII) cannot occur, choose the kernel that skips handling it.
Why it is faster: Overflow checks, 128-bit accumulators, UTF-8 decoding and NULL tests cost something on every row. A single proof at plan time removes them for the whole query. The cost is maintaining correct statistics through every expression.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip validation when the schema guarantees non-null. Take ASCII fast paths for known-ASCII protocol fields. • Native / systems: range analysis in compilers to drop bounds checks. 32-bit accumulators when counts are bounded. • Data / databases: spreadsheet engines picking faster numeric paths for small ranges.
Watch out: a wrong statistic silently gives wrong answers (overflow, garbled text). Propagation must be conservative through every function.
Seen in DuckDB: bbc153aa21 2020-11-20 Statistics rework and propagation. 3d27214ad5 SUM uses an `int64` state when `max*max_cardinality` fits. 5526a65709 swaps in unchecked arithmetic when no overflow is possible. f77ccf9523 adds the overflow-checking variants. • f6e07ff96e 2018-08-07 Uses statistics to avoid NULL checking. • 0fc432c7cc 2022-06-10 `abs` drops overflow checking unless min is INT_MIN, and becomes a no-op on unsigned types. 2d5cbf2018 `date_trunc` statistics come from applying it to min and max. • db193c5dd9 2026-08-25 Statistics-proven ranges select unchecked casts.

### duckdb-036 — Prove predicates and subtrees trivial, then delete them
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Use statistics and plan structure to prove filters always true (remove them) or always false (replace the subtree with an empty result), and propagate emptiness upward.
Why it is faster: A filter proved always true costs nothing to remove. An always-false branch removes whole subplans, including their scans. `COUNT(x)` on a non-null column becomes `COUNT(*)`, which needs no data.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: fold feature flags at build time, and short-circuit empty pagination. • Native / systems: dead-code elimination after constant propagation. • Data / databases: prune impossible GraphQL branches.
Watch out: statistics must reflect transaction-local and uncommitted data, or pruning is wrong.
Seen in DuckDB: a9938d50ad 2019-03-01 `LogicalEmptyResult` for statically pruned branches. • 9d4b6ed5ee 2020-11-05 Statistics pushdown into filters prunes filters that are always false. d37979473c rewrites `count(column)` to `count(*)`. 2fb583117d adds `constant_or_null`. • b8e49fdaf2 2022-03-16 `LIMIT 0` becomes an empty result. • 051b9894e5 2024-10-11 The PullUp Empty Results optimizer propagates provably empty subtrees.

### duckdb-037 — Answer aggregates from metadata instead of data
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: If block metadata already holds exact counts, min or max, combine the metadata. Scan only the blocks that partially qualify.
Why it is faster: A metadata read replaces a full scan: milliseconds instead of seconds, and fewer remote requests. Exactness matters: deletes or transaction-local data make stored counts approximate.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: keep maintained counters instead of running `SELECT COUNT(*)`. Sum object sizes from a listing instead of HEAD requests. • Data / databases: Parquet footers for row counts, and chunk headers for `max(ts)` in time-series stores.
Watch out: metadata must be exact for this to be correct, not just conservative as for pruning. Track exact versus approximate explicitly.
Seen in DuckDB: 115a8aedcd 2024-12-12 `get_partition_stats` returns exact or approximate counts: `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM lineitem` SF10 0.008 s to 0.001 s. • 07d41f48b9 2025-12-07 Ungrouped min/max from row-group statistics: `max(time)` cold 0.10 s to 0.016 s, hot 0.035 s to 0.002 s. d62ae80789 the same for Parquet. ced12aa42a answers COUNT(*) in the multi-file reader. • c523bc547f 2026-07-09 Exact row-group statistics so `count(*)` avoids a sequential scan. • 2957fd1e3f 2026-05-14 Precomputes min/max/count_star for fully qualifying row groups and merges them with a scan of the rest: 14 ms to 0.6 ms (23x).

### duckdb-038 — Compute shared subexpressions and subplans once
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Detect repeated expressions, aggregates, filter predicates and whole plan subtrees. Compute each once (in a projection, a shared aggregate or a materialized CTE) and reference the result.
Why it is faster: Duplicates in generated SQL are common: `SUM(x)` in SELECT and HAVING, BETWEEN expanded to two comparisons, identical FILTER clauses on many aggregates, repeated CTE references. Structural equality can be checked cheaply by normalizing ids, serializing the tree and comparing the bytes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: deduplicate identical sub-requests in a GraphQL batch by hashing a normalized request, and merge identical dashboard metric queries. • Native / systems: hash-consing and GVN in compilers, and build systems memoizing actions by input hash. • Data / databases: materialized CTEs, shared scans.
Watch out: materializing a shared result costs memory and blocks pipelining, and an inlined CTE may get better filter pushdown. Volatile expressions must not be shared.
Seen in DuckDB: 796ee18b1a 2020-11-23 CSE pushes a projection for repeated expressions. d081c4a3f8 skips side-effecting expressions. • eed3403796 2019-02-16 CommonAggregateOptimizer (7810a6e906). 75b1099e0c dedupes during binding. 5498524047 re-adds it, followed by column lifetime analysis. • 96e0e347f4 2021-06-14 BETWEEN becomes a first-class node so `x` is evaluated once. d56fd4cc87 avoids copying subqueries in BETWEEN. • e00dfa4b49 2025-06-12 CTEs are materialized by default instead of inlined, so a multiply-referenced CTE is computed once. • f635c7886e 2025-09-30 Common subplan elimination via bottom-up serialized signatures (table indices renumbered, `BinarySerializer`), materialized as CTEs. • 6bb57d29db 2026-11-03 Each distinct FILTER predicate is evaluated once and each (filter, column) pair sliced once: 15 aggregates with 5 filters 0.289 s to 0.076 s (26% had been atomic refcounts of slices).

### duckdb-039 — Rewrite aggregates algebraically into cheaper or shared primitives
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Decompose composite aggregates into shared base aggregates (`SUM(x+C) = SUM(x) + C*COUNT(x)`, `AVG = SUM/COUNT`). Turn sort-based constructs (row_number ≤ k, DISTINCT ON, mode) into streaming aggregates such as `arg_max`, bounded heaps or plain GROUP BY stages.
Why it is faster: Ninety affine sums over one column collapse to two aggregates. A per-group top-k heap replaces sorting whole partitions. Aggregates that hide hash maps in their state cannot spill or parallelize. Rewritten as ordinary GROUP BY stages, they use the engine's best machinery. Moving CASE into a FILTER clause lets non-matching rows skip state updates.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: derive mean and variance from shared sum, sum-of-squares and count accumulators. "Latest N per user" with a per-key heap. • Native / systems: fuse GPU reductions that share inputs. • Data / databases: Spark's expand-and-aggregate for countDistinct, and Prometheus recording rules.
Watch out: algebraic identities fail under overflow, floating-point rounding and NULL semantics. Exclude types where they do not hold, as DuckDB does for hugeint and `kahan_sum`.
Seen in DuckDB: ac42443c5a 2024-02-15 `DISTINCT ON (k) … ORDER BY v` becomes `arg_min_null(row, v)`, a constant-space streaming aggregate. • 3be8f0ba9f 2024-11-29 `SUM(x + C)` becomes `SUM(x) + C*COUNT(x)`: 90 sums over `ResolutionWidth + k` 0.48 s to 0.025 s. 9e6fd53f3d excludes hugeint because of overflow. • 353406bd7f 2025-10-08 `row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY g ORDER BY v) <= k` becomes `arg_max(row, v, k)`: 19.4 s to 1.5 s. Follow-ups 7977b60c54, 0f1edb74c7, 425aae5e33. • 5e52773a7f 2026-03-04 `AVG(x)` becomes `SUM(x)/COUNT(x)`, so TPC-H Q1's averages share the existing sums and count. • b95e49bf7a 2026-07-23 DISTINCT aggregates become CTE branches with shared dedup. 840649a3f5 `mode` becomes `arg_max(v, count)` over `GROUP BY g, v`. • d2e6be75c1 2026-11-18 `agg(CASE WHEN c THEN x END)` becomes `agg(x) FILTER (WHERE c)`, with NULL-correct forms.

### duckdb-040 — Aggregate early: push partial aggregation below joins, and derive coarse groupings from fine ones
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Pre-aggregate by (group keys plus join keys) below a join, then combine partial states above it. For ROLLUP or CUBE, aggregate the raw input once into the finest grouping and re-aggregate that small result for each coarser set.
Why it is faster: A join that fans rows out multiplies the work of the aggregate above it, and pre-aggregation collapses rows before the multiplication. Coarser grouping sets computed from the finest one touch only its small result instead of pushing every raw row into N hash tables. This requires aggregates with mergeable (exportable) partial states.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: GROUP BY in a subquery before joining to a dimension table. Batch N+1 aggregate queries. • Native / systems: map-reduce combiners before the join stage. • Data / databases: OLAP cube rollups from the finest cuboid, hour, day and month from minute aggregates, BI semantic-layer pre-aggregation.
Watch out: pre-aggregation is wasted when keys are nearly unique, so gate it on cardinality. Some decompositions overflow (DuckDB excludes `kahan_sum`).
Seen in DuckDB: 3527a98456 2026-05-18 `PartialAggregatePushdown`: an `EXPORT_STATE` partial below the join and a combine above, gated on combinable states and cardinality. • 58a70740ba 2026-06-16 Grouping sets: the widest set exports states, the others combine over it. `CUBE(l_returnflag,l_linestatus)` pushes lineitem into one table instead of four. • 2daa4fc9a4 2026-06-30 Yan and Larson eager aggregation, including "double-eager" for fact-by-fact joins: a self-amplifying join 1.866 s to 0.003 s (about 622x). • 7e14bd24e0 2026-08-12 Aggregate Reuse Optimizer. ec6b0234a3 cost-based duplicate-eliminated domain. 68f4a4b8ba merges sibling scalar subqueries over the same file into one scan. f87fab226c rewrites self-joins into group-bys (TPC-DS q95). • ab1d353db9 2026-11-16 Partial aggregate pushdown for ROLLUP/CUBE via exported states.

### duckdb-041 — Choose the physical algorithm from estimated sizes, using thresholds
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: The same logical operation has several implementations whose costs cross over: index lookup versus hash join, nested loop versus sort-based range join, filtered aggregates versus GROUP BY with LIST, heap versus full sort, serial versus parallel LIMIT. Pick by cardinality.
Why it is faster: Setup costs (building, sorting, coordinating threads) dominate on small inputs, while asymptotics dominate on large ones. Simple thresholds on estimates capture most of the benefit. The thresholds come from benchmarks and are documented in the code.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: point lookups by primary key for a handful of ids instead of loading a table. Do not fan small requests out to many shards. • Native / systems: linear scan under about 8 elements, a hash map above. • Data / databases: pandas `pivot_table` versus repeated masks, IN-list versus join in ORMs.
Watch out: thresholds depend on estimates, which can be wildly off. Make important ones configurable and re-benchmark when the implementations change.
Seen in DuckDB: 479fd00c50 2020-10-19 Index nested-loop join when the other side is under 1% of the indexed side. 811cb3a11b gives it a faster output function. • fb163c3803 2022-11-18 Range joins use a nested-loop join when either side is estimated at 5 rows or fewer. • b4099d9d0c 2023-04-05 PIVOT via one GROUP BY plus LIST instead of N filtered aggregates. 2f47878fe3 adds `pivot_filter_threshold`: "filtered aggregates are faster when there are FEW pivot values, LIST is faster when there are MANY". • b4c1529172 2023-07-08 Optional `prefer_range_joins` for selective inequalities alongside weak equalities. • 5304647326 2024-06-08 No order-preserving parallel LIMIT when the child is a plain scan. a595c8108a uses parallel LIMIT when the scan has filters: 21 s to 1.5 s. • 59ef36be2f 2025-04-17 Top-N heap only when the limit is under 0.7% of the table.

### duckdb-042 — Turn a large literal IN-list into a hash semi-join
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: `x IN (c1..cN)` evaluated per row costs O(N) per row. Materialize the constants and probe a hash table instead, at O(1) per row.
Why it is faster: Comparisons per row drop from N to one hash probe. The rewrite has to happen before filter pushdown, so the large list is not shipped into scans as a per-row filter.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `new Set(list).has(x)` instead of `list.includes(x)` in loops. Compile allow-lists into hash sets. • Data / databases: join against VALUES or a temp table instead of a giant `IN (...)` from an ORM.
Watch out: for short lists, direct comparisons (and zone-map pruning on them) are better, so apply the rewrite above a size threshold.
Seen in DuckDB: b3eee9787b 2019-03-06 Large IN clauses become a ChunkCollection of constants plus a MARK join. 5d2b5a905e runs this before join ordering. 42e2e7e3f6 large IN-list benchmarks. fe31d9f677 reworks OR/(NOT) IN. • e96d111782 2026-05-18 `contains(list_value(...), col)` is normalized to `COMPARE_IN`, and large IN lists are kept out of scan filters so they become a hash join: 100M rows 3.29 s to 0.07 s.

### duckdb-043 — Eliminate operators whose effect is provably nil
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Remove joins whose columns are unused and whose keys are unique, sorts whose order is discarded, DISTINCT over provably distinct inputs, redundant decorrelation joins, and outer joins that a later filter makes inner.
Why it is faster: Each removed operator saves its full cost, which is often a hash table or a sort. Simplifying outer joins to inner joins also unlocks reordering and more pushdown. Decorrelation introduces duplicate-eliminated joins, and many of them turn out to be unnecessary.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ORMs dropping eager joins whose fields are not selected. GraphQL resolvers dropping unused sorts. • Native / systems: compiler dead-code elimination after inlining. • Data / databases: rewrite ORM LEFT JOINs that have WHERE clauses on the right table.
Watch out: every proof of uniqueness or NULL rejection must be exact. A wrong elimination returns duplicate or missing rows.
Seen in DuckDB: 483eba5417 2021-01-18 The deliminator removes redundant DELIM_GET/DELIM_JOIN operators. c2ca23eef9 also under inequality joins. f171e39899 emits COALESCE for USING columns only in full outer joins. • d8add1ee3f 2023-02-01 UNNEST in lateral joins moves into a projection, dropping the delim join (77712d5f44 INNER only). • 1857feb1e4 2025-02-13 UNPIVOT no longer projects all input columns into UNNEST: 10,000 columns 145.4 s to 0.77 s. • 1d9a0e0c48 2026-03-08 `OuterJoinSimplification`: a NULL-rejecting operator above makes the join LEFT or INNER, about 3x on TPC-DS Q93. • 5a5e75c1a2 2025-11-13 Removes LEFT/RIGHT joins when no inner columns are used and uniqueness is guaranteed. • 315db3e56f 2026-10-21 `redundant_order` pass. 371dc6d73f `redundant_distinct` with a structural distinctness proof. 4f3f4f7deb folds trivial ANY joins into cross products. 46a4e337b8 LEFT with IS NULL on the null-extended side becomes ANTI. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-024): e2159f38426 2005-05-22 SubqueryScan nodes that do no selection or projection are removed; 0d49838df6f inlines single-SELECT set-returning SQL functions. • 488d70ab463 2009-09-17 Left joins to a unique inner side that is never referenced are removed (common in generated SQL); f15821eefd7 extended join removal. • 4387cf956b9 2013-03-14 No Result node for identity projections ("non-negligible overhead, especially when there are many columns"); 1a1832eb085 avoids no-op Limit nodes and 8edd0e79460 removes single-child Append and MergeAppend. • 55d5b3c0827 2014-06-12 Unused output expressions of unflattened subqueries are replaced by constants, so they are never evaluated and join removal can apply. • 9c7f5229ad6 2017-04-07 Proven-unique inner sides let joins stop after the first match and skip mark/restore in merge joins; 92a43e4857d reduces semijoins with a unique inner side to inner joins. • b262ad440ed 2024-01-23 `col IS NOT NULL` on a NOT NULL column is dropped and `IS NULL` becomes a constant false; e2debb64380 moved this into constant folding so it cascades and cb7b7ec7a1c extended it to `ROW(...) IS NULL`. • bd10ec52979 2024-12-12 GROUP BY drops columns functionally dependent on any NOT NULL unique index, preferring the index with fewest columns. • fc069a3a631 2025-02-13 Self-join elimination removes a self-join proven to match the same row. • 9c9d41af4db 2025-10-07 UNION, INTERSECT and EXCEPT branches with provably empty inputs are marked dummy so they are never executed.

### duckdb-044 — Rewrite general operations into specialized primitives at plan time
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Classify constant patterns and predicate shapes when planning: LIKE `'abc%'` becomes a prefix check, a regex of literals becomes `contains`, an enum compared through VARCHAR becomes an integer compare, `a=b OR (a IS NULL AND b IS NULL)` becomes a hashable `IS NOT DISTINCT FROM`. Hash on the equality part of a mixed join condition.
Why it is faster: A general matcher (backtracking, regex VM, string materialization, nested loop) does work the specific case does not need. The classification happens once per query, and every row benefits. Rewriting a join condition into a hashable form can turn O(N·M) into O(N+M).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: compile globs to `startsWith`/`endsWith`/`includes`. Web routers matching literal routes with string compares. • Native / systems: regex engines with literal fast paths, and comparing interned symbols instead of strings. • Data / databases: spatial joins on grid-cell equality followed by an exact distance check, and log correlation on request id plus a time-window check.
Watch out: escapes, collations and NULL semantics (NOT LIKE, ILIKE) must be preserved exactly. Recognize only patterns you can prove equivalent.
Seen in DuckDB: 46cc668ea9 2020-04-14 LIKE rewritten to PREFIX, SUFFIX or CONTAINS. 90231b45a5 greedy `LikeMatcher` segments for `X%Y%Z`. c389429957 empty needles. 9902432111 RE2 replaces `<regex>`. • fa20a88b6e 2021-08-29 Constant, prefix, suffix and contains patterns (also NOT LIKE) without an RE2 dependency in the optimizer. • 710a969caa 2022-06-08 `regexp_matches(x, 'literal')` becomes `contains`. 2311f7eb09 IS NOT DISTINCT FROM rewrite, so the condition can be a hash-join key. • 6b84f2a00b 2023-06-05 Walks the RE2 AST: literals and `.*` become a LIKE pattern, which existing rules reduce further. • 6c84958b1e 2021-10-13 `CAST(enum1 AS VARCHAR) = CAST(enum2 AS VARCHAR)` becomes an enum-code comparison (b95ee12acd, 89cc9b7c5f). d3049af575 stores enums as `uint16_t`. • 42ba8038ab 2026-01-27 `t1.a=t2.b AND t1.x+t2.y>100` becomes a hash join with a residual predicate instead of a blockwise nested loop.

### duckdb-045 — Order predicates by estimated cost so cheap ones run first
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Run cheap predicates (integer compares) before expensive ones (casts, string functions, LIKE), so fewer rows reach the costly terms. Assign costs with simple, benchmark-derived heuristics at plan time.
Why it is faster: With short-circuit evaluation on survivors, total cost depends on the order: an expensive predicate evaluated after a selective cheap one sees only a fraction of the rows. A static cost table gets most of the benefit with no runtime overhead, and gives a good starting order for the runtime adaptive reordering covered under measurement-driven tuning.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: order middleware and validation checks by cost. In a WAF, put cheap header checks before body parsing. • Native / systems: rule engines that evaluate cheap rules first. • Data / databases: order WHERE terms in ORM-generated queries.
Watch out: static costs ignore selectivity, which depends on the data. Only side-effect-free predicates may be reordered.
Seen in DuckDB: 8cf352bcd4 2019-12-20 `expression_heuristics.cpp` costs: comparison +5, cast 5 or 200, unknown function 1000, column reference x8. 59f94c182c makes the optimizer invoke filter reordering. b09453057b adds benchmarks to estimate the costs. • c658c26345 2025-02-08 The Parquet reader takes its initial table-filter order from the same kind of heuristics.

### duckdb-046 — Exploit existing partitioning and clustering of the data
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: If input is already partitioned or clustered by the grouping or ordering key, process disjoint key ranges independently. If each block's row count is known, ordinals can be computed per block in parallel.
Why it is faster: Independent smaller pipelines bound memory and avoid spilling. They also remove hash tables or sorts entirely when each partition is one group. Prefix sums of per-block counts make "serial" numbering parallel.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: process sorted logs one day at a time. Assign ids in parallel with per-shard offsets. • Native / systems: line numbering across file chunks via a prefix sum of newline counts. • Data / databases: aggregate per directory in data lakes, and shard batch jobs by primary-key range.
Watch out: range overlap destroys the benefit, so measure it (DuckDB requires an overlap ratio of 0.1 or less) and fall back to the general plan.
Seen in DuckDB: 2852d0f9e6 2024-10-12 `PhysicalPartitionedAggregate` with the `get_partition_data`/`TablePartitionInfo` API. • 27f1246396 2026-04-29 `PartitionedExecution` splits input by min/max key ranges (above 4,194,304 rows, overlap ratio 0.1 or less): about 75 s with 70 GB swap went to about 15 s with none. 1ce496bb2e batches windows for single-value partitions. • 161b745400 2026-04-09 A virtual `row_number` column computed per row group from version info: 1.6 s to 0.12 s.

### duckdb-047 — Fold constants and cast the constant, not the column
Lens: query-planning · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Evaluate constant subexpressions at plan time, reassociate commutative operands so constants gather, and in comparisons cast the literal to the column's type instead of casting every row.
Why it is faster: A per-row cast or arithmetic on constants becomes one evaluation at plan time. The column stays bare, which also enables pushdown and zone maps. Casting the constant is only valid for invertible, order-preserving casts.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: compare dates via a timestamp precomputed from the literal. Precompile regexes outside loops. • Native / systems: Babel or Terser constant folding, and compiler reassociation (integers only).
Watch out: reassociating floats changes results. Non-invertible casts (such as truncation) make "cast the constant" wrong.
Seen in DuckDB: b6ee32b66f 2018-08-09 Constant casting optimizer. e913dacbdf restricts it to invertible casts. 409b26b96f skips a cast only when SQL types match. 2eea9a5f1a expression rewriter rules such as distributivity. • f8913ffd9a 2025-10-28 `col0+1+2+col1+3+4` is normalized so `1+2+3+4` folds.

### duckdb-048 — Stop as soon as the answer is determined
Lens: laziness · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Let operators signal "finished" upstream: a satisfied LIMIT, an empty join build side, a DISTINCT that already has N groups. Scan from the end when you need the last value, and skip an update when a batch adds nothing.
Why it is faster: Without a stop signal, sources keep reading and decoding data nobody will consume. If the build side of an INNER, RIGHT or SEMI join is empty, the whole probe pipeline is pointless. A tri-state result (need more input, have more output, finished) costs nothing and lets a LIMIT stop the source scan. Returning "finished" immediately avoids fetching one extra chunk.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: async iterators that `return()` early and close upstream I/O. Skip downstream service calls when an upstream filter returns nothing. • Embedded: read the most recent record from the tail of a ring buffer. • Native / systems: `head -n` closing pipes, `grep -m N`, `findLast` instead of `reduce`. • Data / databases: paginated readers that stop once the quota is filled.
Watch out: the stop signal must propagate through every operator, including parallel ones. Outer-join and empty-input semantics decide when "empty" really implies no output.
Seen in DuckDB: 0fcf3bb4fd 2020-03-10 When dedup leaves no new values in a DISTINCT aggregate, skip the update step (`if (match_count > 0)`). • 20031c8137 2021-06-22 Empty-hash-table early-out extended to RIGHT joins. 06bf3c935f adds `SinkFinalizeType::NO_OUTPUT_POSSIBLE`, so the downstream pipeline is never run. • 49c0e1db8c 2021-09-08 `Execute` returns an enum, and `OperatorResultType::FINISHED` terminates the pipeline early (filter, limit, joins). • 2f4627fee3 2026-01-20 LAST scans the batch backwards and breaks on the first hit instead of overwriting through the whole batch: 0.156 s to 0.049 s. • 6898b72b86 2026-01-22 Streaming LIMIT returns `HAVE_MORE_OUTPUT` then `FINISHED`, so no extra source chunk is fetched. • 263ef59866 2026-01-21 Globs expand lazily during the scan, so `FROM glob('**') LIMIT 10` stops early: 0.206 s to 0.052 s. • 5df2a10dbf 2026-04-28 `PhysicalLimitedDistinct`: a LIMIT hint pushed into DISTINCT/GROUP BY stops once N groups exist.

### duckdb-049 — Evaluate each later step only on the rows still undecided
Lens: laziness · Source: DuckDB
Principle: For `A AND B AND C`, evaluate B only on rows that passed A. For `COALESCE(a, b, c)`, evaluate b only where a was NULL. Narrow a selection list after each step and stop when it is empty.
Why it is faster: Rewriting COALESCE as a CASE chain, or evaluating every conjunct on every row, does work on rows whose result is already known. A shrinking selection list keeps each step's cost proportional to the undecided rows and preserves short-circuit semantics for expressions that could error.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: cache tiers where tier 2 is queried only for tier-1 misses. Validation pipelines that run expensive checks on survivors only. • Native / systems: filter chains over arrays carrying an index list between stages. • Data / databases: `a ?? b ?? c` over dataframe columns.
Watch out: maintaining selection lists costs something, so for very cheap predicates on nearly all-passing data, evaluating everything branch-free can be faster.
Seen in DuckDB: ad6ed2b9d9 2018-11-23 Join hash-table predicates: "avoid pointlessly evaluating subsequent predicates… if we already know tuple is non-matching". • a4c38e4be0 2019-12-20 `ExpressionExecutor::Select` for conjunctions narrows the selection vector after each child and returns 0 as soon as nothing survives. • a659125d2b 2021-10-23 COALESCE implemented directly: child k runs on `current_sel`/`remaining_count`, results split into non-null and still-null, stopping when nothing remains.

### duckdb-050 — Skip work whose result cannot change the output
Lens: laziness · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Detect computations that are ignored or redundant (no-op updates, checks already done, reorders whose effect is never observed, re-renders of identical output), and choose a cheaper equivalent semantics when it gives the same answer.
Why it is faster: These costs are pure waste and often large: a collation transform for an equality that bytes can decide, a SHA hash nobody verifies, a row-group rewrite for an UPDATE that sets values to themselves. The detection is usually a single comparison or flag.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ORM dirty tracking so UPDATE touches only changed fields, React `memo`, throttled progress output, validating input once at the boundary. • Native / systems: hash raw bytes for equality and use a collator only for sorting. Encode a request body once before retries. • Data / databases: skip checksum verification where integrity is guaranteed elsewhere.
Watch out: "equivalent" must be proven for the use case (code points versus graphemes is not equivalent for display). Comparing old and new values has its own cost.
Seen in DuckDB: 22a5f36ee4 2020-04-29 Collations that change only ordering get `not_required_for_equality`, so DISTINCT, GROUP BY, joins and `=` compare raw bytes. • 8d63f7c248 2021-09-02 Payload strings are not reordered during sorting when the data stays in memory as one run, because the pointers stay valid. • b67e10e8dc 2022-10-19 String functions count Unicode code points instead of grapheme clusters. bade062078 skips computing a SHA when unsigned extensions are allowed. • 5b768c8d42 2023-06-20 Builds the ICU `UnicodeString` once and reuses it for both `getSortKey` calls when retrying with a bigger buffer. • 2e3d7058de 2024-01-22 The progress bar is not re-rendered if the percentage is unchanged. • 5f23f06bf7 2025-07-04 UPDATE compares new and old values and skips unchanged ones; in the best case the update and any row-group rewrite are skipped. • 5c4f75c96f 2026-02-13 Skips the second `VerifyAppendConstraints` when conflict handling already ran.

### duckdb-051 — Skip pipeline phases whose output will not be used
Lens: laziness · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Requests that only need metadata (describe, bind-only), objects that already exist, and re-executions of prepared plans should bypass the planning, binding or optimization phases they cannot benefit from.
Why it is faster: For short OLTP-style statements, front-end phases dominate latency. Binding a view that already exists can trigger network requests, and binding twice to build a wrapper query doubles the cost. The converse also holds: caching a generic plan is wrong when re-planning with known parameter values is cheap and unlocks optimizations.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HEAD/OPTIONS fast paths, skipping hydration for metadata requests, `IF NOT EXISTS` short-circuits in migration tools. • Native / systems: type-check-only compiler modes. • Data / databases: custom versus generic plans for prepared statements (as Postgres chooses).
Watch out: skipped phases sometimes have side effects (errors, permission checks) that callers rely on.
Seen in DuckDB: 2d123094bb 2024-12-05 Builds the physical plan for PREPARE only when it will be used. e5c89d8468 avoids unnecessary rebinding. • 9a7bbe6f15 2026-02-10 Prepared-statement plan caching becomes less aggressive in favor of re-binding with known parameters. • 9c4e82ecae 2026-03-20 `CREATE VIEW IF NOT EXISTS` skips binding when the view exists, avoiding network requests. e31cb5d547 JSON COPY no longer binds twice. • f9e0566246 2026-08-05 The WAL prescan skips deserializing payloads it does not need. • 9ab4527707 2026-10-20 EXECUTE reusing a prepared plan skips the optimizer. 570472871c `PlanningMode::BIND_ONLY`: 1.17x fewer instructions per describe.

### duckdb-052 — Keep summary flags so one check stands for many items
Lens: laziness · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Track per-block facts such as "nothing deleted here" or "all rows written by the same transaction". In the common case, one check decides the whole block instead of a check per row.
Why it is faster: Per-row visibility or validity checks become one comparison per 1024 or more rows, and the per-row path runs only when the flag says something unusual happened. Maintaining the flag costs a write when the block changes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: dirty bits on UI tree nodes, so clean subtrees are skipped during render. • Native / systems: "no tombstones in this block" flags in SSTables. • Data / databases: page-level all-visible bits (the Postgres visibility map).
Watch out: flags must be cleared on every mutation path. A stale "all clean" flag returns wrong results.
Seen in DuckDB: 8338db9943 2019-05-27 A dirty flag on segments skips version-info lookups. • aef4f15d7f 2020-09-10 `any_deleted` and `all_same_id` in chunk info, with a four-way `GetSelVector` fast path.

### duckdb-053 — Bound exploratory work: sample by block, and draw only what you need
Lens: laziness · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Detection, inference and sampling should look at a bounded amount of input. Sample whole blocks instead of scanning everything and dropping rows, and generate only the random numbers the sample needs.
Why it is faster: Trying every candidate configuration over many chunks multiplies detection time. Block-level sampling skips I/O for unsampled blocks. A partial Fisher-Yates shuffle draws k numbers instead of shuffling n.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: content-type sniffing from the first N KB, and schema inference over a sample of JSON records. • Native / systems: "pick k of n" with partial shuffles. • Data / databases: approximate analytics with page-level sampling.
Watch out: block sampling is biased when data is clustered, and bounded sniffing can misdetect formats whose distinguishing rows appear late.
Seen in DuckDB: dba3b77df7 2023-02-04 CSV auto-detection stops looping over all sample chunks for every dialect candidate and uses a single sniffing parse (5866dbc0e1), fixing a regression. • 714c4f90c4 2024-10-16 TABLESAMPLE SYSTEM samples by chunk during the scan: up to 100x on SF1 sample aggregations. b26e45dd06 partial Fisher-Yates instead of a full `std::shuffle`.

### duckdb-054 — Hand out results that materialize on access
Lens: laziness · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When converting results into a host language is expensive, return a proxy that wraps the native buffers and converts each value only when it is read.
Why it is faster: Up-front conversion of every string to a host object costs time and memory proportional to the whole result, even if the user inspects only a few values. A lazy wrapper costs nothing until access.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JS Proxy or lazy getters over binary buffers, and cursor-backed ORM lists. • Data / databases: memory-mapped data frames, and Arrow-backed result objects.
Watch out: the wrapper keeps native memory alive as long as any proxy exists. Repeated access without caching converts the same values again.
Seen in DuckDB: b2faac152e 2021-03-19 R string columns are returned as an ALTREP vector wrapping DuckDB vectors (`DuckDBAltrepStringWrapper`), so R strings are created only when accessed. Merged in 7da9ca455d.

### duckdb-055 — Group many small operations so fixed per-call costs are paid once
Lens: amortization · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Appends, updates, lookups and shared-state publications each carry locks, bookkeeping and storage calls. Buffer them per worker, sort them by locality where it helps, and apply them in batches.
Why it is faster: A storage call that touches one row and takes several locks per row spends its time on overhead. Buffering 100 chunks before flushing, sorting random row-id updates so about 21 rows hit each storage call, and deferring sub-row-group leftovers to a parallel finalize all divide the fixed cost by the batch size.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: batched inserts and `COPY` in Postgres clients, analytics events batched in the browser, DataLoader-style lookup batching. • Native / systems: elevator-sorted disk writes, and sorting keys before bulk B-tree inserts. • Data / databases: per-thread log buffers flushed periodically, and cache invalidations sorted by shard.
Watch out: batches add latency and need a memory budget. Bound them, as DuckDB does with its per-thread sort budget.
Seen in DuckDB: 2df6da514e 2021-08-03 The Appender gathers `FLUSH_COUNT = 100` chunks before flushing, with an `append_mix` benchmark (629bf1796e). • 8a3a51774d 2026-06-07 `RowGroupCollection::Fetch` fetches co-located rows in batches: 0.85 s to 0.79 s. • 9b69a2d305 2026-08-19 PhysicalInsert defers sub-row-group leftovers to Finalize and packs them in parallel: about 24 s to about 12 s in a customer query. ee7d48d883 keyed recursive UNION buffers per worker and pre-aggregates duplicates before the shared hash table. • 36e7b0097a 2026-09-16 ART batch equality scan: one scan state and key generation serve many lookups. • 678f0ebab3 2026-11-17 `PhysicalUpdate` stages out-of-order updates in a bounded per-thread sort keyed by row id (1-64 MB). The stats lock is taken once per chunk. The baseline had 700-1000 s of kernel lock time on 96 threads.

### duckdb-056 — Replace many small sorts with one large sort keyed by group
Lens: amortization · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Instead of sorting each group's buffer separately, prefix every row with its group id, run one sort on (group, keys), and hand each contiguous run to its consumer.
Why it is faster: Millions of tiny sorts each pay setup, allocation and dispatch. One big sort amortizes them, uses the best parallel and external sort machinery, and produces runs in order.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: order per-user events by sorting once by (user, ts) instead of per user. • Data / databases: build many small sorted lists in ETL with one global sort.
Watch out: for few groups with small buffers, per-group sorting can be cheaper, so use a threshold.
Seen in DuckDB: eaf507009f 2023-04-15 Ordered aggregates (`agg(x ORDER BY y)`) sort all input payloads at once on (state_idx, orders): 50% faster on the `list(c ORDER BY c)` benchmark (8aa68d2eb7). 54459684f2 adds a sorting threshold and honours external sorting.

### duckdb-057 — Keep setup alive across iterations of an iterative computation
Lens: amortization · Source: DuckDB
Principle: In loops of query-like work (recursive CTEs, fixed-point solvers), reset and reuse operators, hash tables and filters instead of rebuilding them each round. Keep loop-invariant pipelines, and size parallelism to the actual working set.
Why it is faster: When each iteration processes little data, setup (building pipelines, allocating hash tables, spinning up threads) dominates. Putting the loop-invariant input on the build side lets its hash table persist across iterations. A tiny working set runs better single-threaded.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: graph algorithms reusing frontier buffers, and pooled request contexts. • Native / systems: game loops with object pools, ML training that reuses allocated tensors. • Data / databases: iterative graph queries and fixed-point Datalog evaluation that keep invariant indexes.
Watch out: state must be fully reset. Leftover entries from a previous iteration corrupt results.
Seen in DuckDB: 60e81d3a94 2026-05-15 `Reset()` on physical operators, invariant-pipeline tracking, and dynamic thread counts from the known working-set size: Advent of Code recursive-CTE suite 1.05x to 11.25x. • 24b84818b2 2026-10-20 Recursive CTEs keep join hash-table shells and Bloom filters, and size aggregate partitions to the actual workers. A reproducer had made 3.69 GB of transient allocations for a 9.1 MB peak.

### duckdb-058 — Represent a filtered subset as an index list, not a copy
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: After filtering, keep the original data where it is and pass along a small array of surviving positions. Later steps read through that array, refine it, or share it, and no payload bytes move.
Why it is faster: Compacting every column after every filter costs a read and a write per surviving value per column. An index list costs one small integer per surviving row, no matter how many columns there are, and successive filters only shrink it. The cost is one indirection on every later access. When almost every row survives, use a null "identity" marker instead of building the list. And never allocate an index list per group or per tiny unit when one shared buffer would do.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In a JS data grid, keep a `Uint32Array` of visible row indices after filtering and sorting instead of calling `array.filter` again for each new criterion. • Native / systems: Pass (base pointer, index list) pairs between pipeline stages, and use a null index list to mean "all rows". • Data / databases: Search engines intersect posting lists of document ids. A GPU stream-compaction step produces an index buffer, not a compacted copy.
Watch out: Long chains of indirection hurt locality once the surviving rows are sparse and scattered. Compact when the next stage does a lot of work per row. Never materialize an identity list just to satisfy an API.
Seen in DuckDB: f5319e0399 2019-10-28 The filter operator produces a selection vector instead of copying the qualifying rows. • 8cf352bcd4 2019-12-20 "Selection Vector and Heuristics" PR spreads selection vectors through the executor. • 17b18ef5a6 2020-03-27 "Selection Vector Rework": `SelectionVector` becomes a first-class, shareable buffer. 8930a4bda6 2020-02-18 removes per-vector cardinalities, so one count and one selection live at chunk level. • 048e68a5da 2021-06-15 8df329b748 2021-06-15 Stop building a potentially huge identity selection vector just to call a generic copy routine. 0c35556a8c 2021-07-02 removes `IncrementalSelectionVector`: "no selection" now means identity. • 32ad2126cc 2023-03-28 The "Tuple Data Collection" rework deletes per-partition staging buffers. Input is sliced with a selection vector and appended straight to its partition. • c7032623d4 2023-03-25 Ordered aggregates share one selection vector instead of allocating one per group state: memory about 90% lower (20 GB to 2 GB), runtime 15 s to 5.5 s. • 1ee8a1791a 2025-02-13 UNNEST/UNPIVOT packs many tiny lists into one output vector with selection vectors instead of copying. b33c23c1cb 2025-02-24 array columns find runs of selected rows and alternate Skip and Scan: a 1/2000 selection over 1024-element arrays went from 1.89 s to 0.023 s.

### duckdb-059 — Point at existing bytes instead of copying them (views and shared buffers)
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When the source bytes already have the layout you need, hand out a pointer or view plus a reference that keeps the source alive. Copy only when you must mutate or when the source would die first.
Why it is faster: The copy itself goes away (memory bandwidth plus an allocation), and so does the second resident copy. The price is lifetime management. A view must hold a reference to its backing buffer, and that buffer stays alive as long as any view exists. A tiny view can therefore pin a huge buffer. This works when the on-disk or foreign layout matches your in-memory layout byte for byte.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In Node, use `Buffer.subarray` rather than `slice`-and-copy. Parse HTTP headers as offsets into the request buffer. • Native / systems: Use Rust `&str` or `Bytes`, C++ `string_view`, mmap plus slices, and `Arc` clones instead of deep clones. • Data / databases: Read Arrow IPC with zero copies. Use NumPy `frombuffer`. Share statistics and schema objects between plan versions.
Watch out: A view's bytes have to mean what you assume. DuckDB's pandas shortcut misread Latin-1 as ASCII. Views also keep their parent alive, so a small slice of a large buffer retains the whole buffer. Copy and release when the parent is large and the slice is long-lived.
Seen in DuckDB: d45fcb2438 2020-05-03 "Fast Pandas Scan": numeric NumPy columns are wrapped with `FlatVector::SetData` instead of being copied. A string shortcut that read CPython's 1-byte representation directly was wrong (Latin-1, not ASCII) and was removed in e3740f5b40 2020-06-30. ### duckdb-060 — Fixed-size string headers with an inline prefix (short strings fully inline)
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Store each string as a 16-byte header: length, a 4-byte prefix, then either the rest of a short string inline or a pointer to the bytes. Most comparisons, and every short string, finish without touching the heap.
Why it is faster: Equality checks compare length plus prefix as one 8-byte word, so most unequal strings are rejected with no pointer dereference. Strings of up to 12 bytes need no heap allocation at all. If the inline area is zero-padded, two short strings are equal exactly when their 16-byte headers are equal. Ordering can compare the byte-swapped prefix as an integer first. The costs are 16 bytes per string even when it is tiny, and any code that writes the header must keep the padding and prefix rules intact.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Small-string optimization in a custom string type. Compare fixed 16-byte keys (such as UUIDs) as two `u64`s. • Data / databases: Umbra/Velox-style string views in a Rust or C++ engine. Prefix keys in B-tree pages. • Web / backend: In a large JS sort, compare a cached numeric prefix of each key before running the full `localeCompare`.
Watch out: Data with long shared prefixes, such as URLs or paths, gets little from a 4-byte prefix. Header equality is only valid if the inline area is always zero-padded.
Seen in DuckDB: b64e32b8d2 2018-08-13 An early form: inlined strings used as hash-table group keys. • 31b4718f48 2020-02-25 "Rework String Representation": `PREFIX_LENGTH = 4`, `INLINE_LENGTH = 12`. Equality starts with `memcmp` of length plus prefix. ca74946959 2020-02-24 benchmarked removing inlining and 2a55c4d036 2020-02-24 reverted it. • 0a2b84a3d6 2021-05-07 Inlined strings are zero-padded, so their equality is one fixed 16-byte `memcmp` (PR 2a374a03bc 2021-05-08). • 41104b611e 2023-03-24 Equality loads length+prefix as a `uint64`, then the next 8 bytes, and only then calls `memcmp`. `GreaterThan` compares byte-swapped prefixes as integers. 1413f6d987 2023-03-14 fast-paths the 4-byte prefix check. b40c9d0a18 2023-03-14 sort compares raw `const char*` without building `string_t`.

### duckdb-061 — Keep NULLs in a separate, lazily allocated bitmap where "absent" means "all valid"
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Track missing values in a side bitmap, not inside the values, and don't allocate the bitmap until the first NULL appears. Functions that cannot create NULLs pass their input's mask through by reference.
Why it is faster: Value arrays stay dense and fixed-width. A missing mask can be tested once per batch, so NULL-free data (the common case) pays nothing. When a mask does exist, 64 rows are handled per word. Because the mask is a separate buffer, sharing it is free. The catch is that a function that can introduce NULLs (overflow checks, for example) must say so, or a shared mask becomes wrong.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Arrow-style null bitmaps with `null_count == 0` shortcuts in any columnar code. • Web / backend: Store optional fields as a presence bitmask next to a dense array instead of `null`-holed object arrays. • Embedded: Keep sensor arrays dense, track dropped samples in a separate bitset, and skip the check when no sample was dropped.
Watch out: Sharing masks is only correct when the function cannot add NULLs, so make that a declared property of each function. Bit-level access is slower than byte flags when you check single rows at random.
Seen in DuckDB: 6e220ef7a3 2021-02-23 "Nullmask Rework": `ValidityMask::AllValid()` is just "no mask allocated", with word-level access. 60269295df 2021-02-18 adds the tight loops back for the all-valid case. • 9599a24a4f 2021-02-22 The validity mask is zero-copied from input to output unless the function declares `AddsNulls()`. • 2821b92fef 2021-02-18 Scans copy the null mask only when some value is actually invalid. c36974616e 2021-05-27 allocates the result mask only when an input word is not all-valid. • 4fce11b251 2021-04-02 On disk, NULLs move into their own validity segment, separate from the data. • 9e977aeddd 2024-06-27 `LIST(VARCHAR)` skips the nullmask for string data. 096249a612 2024-06-20 `CopySel` returns early when neither side has NULLs and writes 0xFF in bulk when the source has no mask. • 6729faed7c 2025-03-17 A non-null constant vector gets no validity mask at all, so all-valid fast paths fire (about 1% on TPC-H).

### duckdb-062 — Use row-major layout for data accessed by key; give each row one contiguous heap area
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Scans favour columns, but hash tables, sort payloads and spill buffers are accessed one key at a time. For those, store all of a record's fields together in a fixed-width row, and put that record's variable-length data in one contiguous heap region referenced from the row.
Why it is faster: A probe or comparison that touches a record loads one or two cache lines, not one per column. Batched scatter, gather and match primitives over selection vectors keep this vectorized. With a per-row heap, moving, spilling or rewriting pointers for a row touches one region instead of one allocation per column. The cost is converting between columnar and row form at operator boundaries, which is paid back only when the row is accessed more than once.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Store key and value inline in open-addressing maps. In an ECS, choose archetype rows for components accessed together. • Web / backend: Serialize a message into one buffer per message, not one allocation per field. Use one arena per request. • Data / databases: Use a row format for join and aggregate state while keeping scans columnar.
Watch out: Wide rows waste cache when a probe needs only the key. Keep the key and hash at the front, or store a compact key and fingerprint array separately. Row layouts that point into heaps need pointer swizzling before they can be spilled (see Allocation).
Seen in DuckDB: d1bbb69e2d 2021-06-17 "String heap row format": all variable-size data of a row goes into one heap entry with a single pointer in the row (4b7fdc0ba5 2021-06-17 serializes the heap in row format). • 8045e42405 2021-06-06 "Join Row Layout" replaces about 700 lines of column-wise hash-table code with shared `RowOperations::Scatter/Gather/Match`. cfec7599b5 2021-05-31 moves aggregation to the same layout, and 24cd65241c 2021-05-26 converts the perfect aggregate hash table.

### duckdb-063 — Store variable-length values as one byte blob plus an offsets array; derive what you can
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB · Also: NATS
Principle: Lay variable-length items out back to back in a single buffer, with a separate array of end offsets. Don't store anything that can be computed from neighbours, such as lengths or start positions.
Why it is faster: A length is the difference of two adjacent offsets, so per-item length prefixes are redundant. Encoding NULL as "same offset as the previous item" makes the length computation branch-free. Serializing a column becomes two bulk writes instead of a call per element. Random access needs the offsets array, and updates in place are hard.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Send string tables over the wire as lengths plus a data blob. Protobuf packed repeated fields. • Native / systems: CSR graph storage. String tables in game engines and compilers. • Data / databases: Arrow offsets and data buffers. Drop derivable fields from on-disk metadata.
Watch out: Offsets must be wide enough for the blob (32-bit overflows around 4 GB). Derived fields must be recomputed correctly everywhere the stored ones used to be read.
Seen in DuckDB: 9789bcbb01 2022-06-27 Uncompressed strings drop the u16 length prefix: length = `offset[i] - offset[i-1]`, NULL is stored as the previous offset, and overflow strings are flagged with negative offsets (1a091698df 2022-06-08 removes lengths from the dictionary). • 014e36ab0b 2025-09-24 Segment start rows are no longer serialized because they follow from the row-group start: metadata 14 MB to 12 MB. 5f94550266 2025-11-20 deletes an unused block header size. • cb9e7c2193 2026-02-06 String vector serialization writes one length blob and one concatenated byte blob instead of a list of elements: 1.40 s to 0.25 s (about 5x). • Also seen in NATS (nats-045): 4a46a92cea 2020-05-19 The filestore block cache becomes `cache{buf []byte; idx []uint32; fseq uint64}`, indexed by `seq - fseq`, replacing `map[uint64]*fileStoredMsg`. The same commit sets bit 31 of an offset (`hbit`) once the record's checksum has been verified, so later lookups skip it.

### duckdb-064 — Inline small payloads into pointer slots and use spare pointer bits as tags
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When most payloads are tiny (a short prefix, a single row id), store them inside the 8-byte word that would otherwise point to them, and use bits the pointer never needs to say which case applies.
Why it is faster: Each inlined item saves one allocation, one pointer chase and the allocator overhead. Tagging lets one 8-byte field replace a pointer plus a separate type or swizzle flag, which shrinks every node and so improves cache density for the whole structure. The cost is bit-twiddling on every access, and the scheme depends on assumptions about address ranges.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: SmallVec/`absl::InlinedVector`, tagged pointers, and NaN-boxing in interpreters (V8 Smis, LuaJIT). • Data / databases: Inline short keys or single values in B-tree and trie leaves. • Embedded: Pack a small value plus a type tag into one 32-bit word instead of pointing at a heap object.
Watch out: Address-space assumptions (unused top bits, alignment bits) break on some platforms and with pointer authentication. Bit fields are not portable, so use explicit masks and static asserts.
Seen in DuckDB: fc9fe05841 2022-11-11 "Improve memory management of ART indexes": c45f5436ad 2022-11-10 stores prefixes of 8 bytes or fewer in a union with the pointer. 49ee258180 2022-11-10 inlines leaves that hold a single row id. • e322d56ff9 2022-10-13 A swizzleable pointer assumes the top 33 bits of a block id are zero, so it takes 8 bytes instead of 12 (0a410a1b84 2022-10-08). • 8bf525ee94 2023-07-18 "ART: inline row IDs into node pointers": the common single-row key keeps its row id in the tagged 8-byte child pointer. 01999f81be 2023-06-26 static-asserts the 8-byte node size. 8f28dd38a6 2023-06-28 replaces bit fields with explicit masks for portability. • 7bdda63647 2023-04-06 The LIST aggregate stores its first linked-list entry inline instead of allocating it.

### duckdb-065 — Align and pad record fields, but only when something needs alignment
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Pad fixed-width records so fields that need natural alignment (aggregate states, SIMD-loaded values) start on aligned addresses, and leave the padding out when nothing in the record needs it.
Why it is faster: Unaligned loads are slower on some CPUs, can split cache lines, and are undefined behaviour in C++ for many types. Padding costs space in every record. DuckDB skips it when a row has no aggregate state, which saved up to 7 bytes per tuple and so fitted more rows per cache line in hash tables.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `#[repr(align)]`/`alignas` for SIMD buffers. Order struct fields from largest to smallest to minimize padding. • Embedded: Align DMA and network packet buffers to what the peripheral requires, and pack everything else. • Data / databases: Spend unavoidable padding on useful bytes, such as a longer key prefix.
Watch out: Padding inflates every row. Measure before applying it everywhere, and remove it when no field needs alignment.
Seen in DuckDB: 07eaace602 2020-09-12 "Alignment for hash tables": `Align(n)`, group padding and aligned payload width. • 3710b0df02 2020-09-23 The last persistent segment becomes transient on append "to avoid the vectors on this boundary to be unaligned". • b4f8e0df5f 2021-08-31 Sort entry size is rounded up to a multiple of 8, and the leftover bytes become extra string prefix. • 896982b90f 2025-06-02 `TupleDataLayout` skips alignment when there are no aggregates, saving up to 7 bytes per tuple (part of a PR that made `COUNT(DISTINCT)` about 11% faster).

### duckdb-066 — Co-locate on disk what is read together
Lens: memory-layout · Source: DuckDB
Principle: If storage blocks mix columns, reading one column drags in the others. Buffer enough data to write each column's segments contiguously, with one partial-block packer per column, so a projection reads few blocks.
Why it is faster: Reading N unrelated bytes per useful byte multiplies I/O and cache footprint. Grouping by access pattern brings the I/O for a projection close to the column's size. The cost is buffering several row groups before writing, which uses more memory and adds latency to the write path.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Size Parquet row groups and column chunks. Group time-series chunks per series. • Native / systems: In game asset packs, place textures loaded together next to each other. • Web / backend: Bundle assets that load on the same route together, and keep unrelated assets out of the bundle.
Watch out: Write buffering uses memory and delays durability. Mixed access patterns may want the opposite grouping.
Seen in DuckDB: 7992e70b4d 2025-09-25 Buffer `write_buffer_row_group_count` (default 5) row groups and write them column by column: ClickBench AdvEngineID went from 698 to 318 distinct blocks. • 3e00750a54 2025-09-30 One `PartialBlockManager` per column: AdvEngineID 314 to 255 blocks, ClientTimeZone 672 to 392.

### duckdb-067 — Keep data encoded through execution and work once per distinct value
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Let in-memory batches carry their encoding (constant, sequence, dictionary plus indices) from storage through operators to writers. Decode only where a consumer truly needs flat values.
Why it is faster: A constant needs one computation instead of N. A dictionary with D entries needs D hash lookups or function calls instead of one per row, and the result can stay a dictionary for the next operator. Materialization points that store only indices also shrink memory, sometimes enough to avoid spilling. The cost is that every operator must accept several representations or normalize cheaply, and a dictionary is only worth it while D is much smaller than the row count.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Run pandas/Arrow categoricals and Arrow dictionary arrays end to end, and map UDFs over categories, not rows. • Web / backend: Run a regex or formatter once per distinct log template or enum value in a batch, then fan results out by index. • Native / systems: Apply image transforms to the palette of an indexed-color image. Represent `range(0, N)` as (start, step) instead of an array.
Watch out: High-cardinality dictionaries add an indirection with no gain. Check the ratio of distinct values to rows before taking the dictionary path. Functions that can throw, or that are not deterministic, must not be evaluated on dictionary entries no row actually references.

It also removes a bitpacking alignment rule that made DICT_FSST dictionaries usable only 1 time in 32. • fd1b726c1c 2025-06-12 `ColumnDataCollection` copies only the unique strings of constant and small dictionary vectors: Parquet-to-Parquet SF100 peak RSS 5.1 GB to 3.5 GB. • 3ff79ea07c 2026-04-30 A small hash-join build side emits zero-copy dictionary vectors (about 2x on affected TPC-H SF100 queries). 7e58291717 2026-06-23 adds a "global dictionary" contract: the hash-join sink stores indices, an IMDB query's peak fell from 4.75 GB to 0.83 GB, and a 641 MB build fell to 68 MB, avoiding the spill. 8abfd3a6ea 2026-06-25 the chunk cache accumulates indices instead of flattening. 04cd23231e 2026-04-23 slices dictionaries without stacking them. • 0ccee9a087 2026-10-27 Intermediate collections preserve CONSTANT/DICTIONARY: RSS 1494 MB to 563 MB, sink CPU 2.54 s to 0.77 s. e813f6ea72 2026-11-04 the Parquet writer consumes dictionary and constant views (dictionary strings 2.35x, constant strings 4.48x). 9e4a120508 2026-11-13 hash joins emit reusable dictionaries, so `list_sort` runs once per build value (2,047 ms to 82 ms).

### duckdb-068 — Let codecs compete per block: analyze cheaply, pick the smallest, fall back to raw
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB · Also: zstd
Principle: Don't hard-code one encoding per type. For each block, let every candidate codec estimate (or actually produce) its output size, keep the winner, and store the block uncompressed when nothing beats raw.
Why it is faster: Data shape changes across a column: sorted, constant, low-cardinality or noisy. Per-block choice adapts to it, and a new codec automatically competes wherever it wins. A raw fallback bounds the worst case. The cost is analysis time at write. Exact estimates (compress into a reusable buffer) cost more than cheap ones but never pick wrong.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Choose gzip, brotli or identity per response from a sample, and skip compression for tiny or already-compressed bodies. • Data / databases: Pick a time-series encoding per chunk (delta, XOR, RLE). Pick a per-page encoding in a columnar writer. • Embedded: Store a firmware log block raw whenever the compressed form would be larger.
Watch out: Estimate-based selection can be fooled by unrepresentative samples. Smallest is not always fastest to decode, so add a decode-cost bias when the data is hot.

### duckdb-069 — Narrow values before materializing them (frame of reference on intermediates)
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Before data enters anything that buffers, hashes, sorts or spills it, use known value ranges to subtract the minimum and cast to the smallest unsigned type that fits. Pack short strings into integers. Decode after the operator.
Why it is faster: Intermediate state is copied, compared and maybe spilled several times, so each byte saved pays off repeatedly. Fixed-width keys enable faster paths: perfect-hash aggregation, `memcmp` sorting, fewer radix passes. This needs trustworthy min/max statistics and adds pack and unpack steps, which only pay when the operator does substantial work.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Downcast `int64` to `int16` in pandas before a join or shuffle. Frame-of-reference-encode ids before a Spark shuffle. • Web / backend: Store timestamps in a charting buffer as u32 offsets from a base. Pack (lat, lng) into one 64-bit key for sorting. • Embedded: Sort or bucket 16-bit deltas instead of 32-bit absolute readings.
Watch out: Statistics must be exact bounds, never estimates. Subtracting the minimum can overflow at type limits, and DuckDB excludes such cases.
Seen in DuckDB: 4db8ac8267 2021-09-01 ecaf624374 2021-09-02 ORDER BY keys are range-mapped to `CAST(x - min AS UTINYINT/USMALLINT/UINTEGER)`. The same PR (8a95669145 2021-09-09) drops the null byte when there are no NULLs and sizes string prefixes from the maximum length. 54480d7c89 2021-09-08 uses statistics everywhere sorting happens. • 983659be12 2023-06-23 "Compressed Materialization" wraps hash aggregate, distinct and order operators in compress and decompress projections. f44ed86425 2023-04-14 TPC-H Q1 now uses a perfect hash aggregate. b0fcde39f1 2023-04-07 branch-free string compress/decompress via fixed-size `memcpy`. 3f6a8a9c67 2023-05-01 83faecc0a1 2023-05-01 optimize inlined-string compression. • 08792d5ed2 2024-08-13 Extended to comparison joins. 9c9c2ca52b 2024-04-04 moved into the statistics propagator. • a6d1fbf408 2025-07-12 All-NULL columns are compressed too: about 8 GB to 0.5 GB in the author's test. • 24beac6170 2026-05-13 Grouping sets: TPC-DS SF100 Q67 62.2 s to 50.9 s. 16a5aec84d 2026-06-22 POINT XY packed into a UHUGEINT: ORDER BY 0.880 s to 0.196 s.

### duckdb-070 — Bitpack integers with frame-of-reference and delta modes
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Per small group (such as 32 values), subtract a reference (the min, or the previous value for deltas) and store each value in the minimum number of bits. Unpack with tight, SIMD-friendly fixed-width kernels.
Why it is faster: Most integer columns use a tiny part of their type's range, so storage and scan bandwidth drop several-fold. Decoding is pure shifts and masks over 32-value blocks, which vectorizes well. Delta mode turns sorted or sequential data into near-zero widths. Group-local widths limit the damage from outliers. Watch the sign: a branch-free sign extension, or none at all when min is at least 0, keeps decoding cheap.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Delta-plus-bitpack timestamps and ids in time-series stores. Posting lists in search engines. • Web / backend: Ship compact id lists to mobile clients as FOR-bitpacked blocks. • Embedded: Pack 10-bit ADC samples into a ring buffer instead of storing 16-bit words.
Watch out: Very wide groups lose to outliers. Packing at nearly full width saves nothing, so round up to the native width. Random access needs per-group offsets.
