### duckdb-071 — Dictionary-encode repetitive strings per block, with a purpose-built builder
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Store each distinct value once per block and replace occurrences with small (bitpacked) codes. Build the dictionary with a fast hash map keyed on views, not owned strings.
Why it is faster: Low-cardinality string columns shrink to a few bits per row. Codes are fixed-width, so they are cheap to compare, hash and move, and can be operated on directly. Writing depends on the speed of the distinct-value lookup. A generic ordered map or node-based hash map dominates write time, while a flat linear-probing map built for the job does not. The dictionary must be bounded, or high-cardinality data bloats it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Intern repeated labels when archiving JSON logs. Use enum codes instead of strings in hot paths. • Data / databases: Low-cardinality columns in Parquet, ORC or ClickHouse-style stores. A Bloom filter is cheap to build from the dictionary when you have one. • Native / systems: Symbol tables in compilers keyed by `string_view` into the source buffer.
Watch out: High-cardinality data makes the dictionary as big as the data plus the codes. Cap its size and fall back. Equality of codes across blocks requires a shared (global) dictionary.

### duckdb-072 — Encode runs, constants and sparse bitmaps compactly
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Store repetitive data as (value, run length) pairs. Store an all-identical block as just its statistics. Store booleans and validity per fixed container as whichever is smallest: a bitset, a list of runs, or a list of positions.
Why it is faster: Runs and constants remove data entirely. A constant block costs zero data bytes. Scans of runs can emit constants or `memset`s instead of decoding every value, and filters can test a run once. Containers sized to the batch avoid any key indirection. Fixed-width run counters must split long runs, and a random pattern makes run encoding larger than raw.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Roaring bitmaps in inverted indexes. Sorted status columns RLE-encode to almost nothing. • Web / backend: Omit default-valued fields from JSON payloads. Send a "constant column" flag instead of N copies. • Embedded: RLE for sprite and tile data or long idle stretches in sensor streams.
Watch out: Unsorted, high-entropy data defeats RLE. Analyze first (see codec competition). Run-length counters need a split policy.
Seen in DuckDB: a4862d7b05 2021-07-03 "Constant Segment Compression": an all-identical (or all-NULL) segment keeps only its statistics, and the test database shrank from 11,022,336 to 6,828,032 bytes. 39f797b583 2021-07-31 moves it into the compression framework. • 94186981cb 2021-08-05 RLE with a 16-bit counter (NULLs extend the current run). 2069420c15 2021-08-03 lets RLE overflow past one block. a952822afa 2021-08-11 stores multiple RLE segments per block. • 148c8b9a7d 2024-11-30 A rewritten RLE scan loop with bulk copies for long runs: ClickBench q02 0.015 s to 0.007 s, q08 2.5x (b64d6e7723 2024-11-29). • aa3787b0c1 2024-12-07 Roaring-style validity and boolean compression with 2048-entry containers (one per vector), so no container keys are needed. An "inverted array" variant handles mostly-valid data.

### duckdb-073 — Push filters and row selections into the decoder
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Give each codec two operations: "which rows match this predicate" (evaluated on encoded data where possible) and "decode only these rows". Fall back to full decode plus slice only when the selection is dense.
Why it is faster: Decoding is often the main cost of a scan. Testing a run or a dictionary entry once covers many rows, and decoding only the needed 32-value groups or strings skips the rest. You can also skip validation work, such as UTF-8 checks, for filtered-out rows. Each codec needs extra code paths, and for dense selections a full decode is cheaper.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Evaluate predicates on dictionary codes or bitmap words. Page-level skipping in Parquet. Lucene doc-values `advance()`. • Web / backend: Partial JSON decoding: check a discriminant field before parsing the rest of the record. • Embedded: Query run-length-encoded telemetry per run instead of per sample.
Watch out: Keep a correct generic fallback (scan plus slice) for every codec. Pick sparse or dense paths by selectivity, not one fixed path.
Seen in DuckDB: 9fc7270fb2 2022-10-15 Patas/Chimp scans skip whole groups using only metadata. • 2e88160d03 2025-02-09 Parquet table filters are evaluated once per dictionary entry inside the `DictionaryDecoder`. 6fa3b7a5a2 2025-02-07 adds a dedicated Skip. 4c77e9cf21 2025-02-11 a string Select that skips UTF-8 validation of filtered rows. 1300137440 2025-02-08 reuses `ColumnSegment::FilterSelection`. • 1db3def66e 2024-12-08 An optional `compression_select_t` (RLE, uncompressed strings, FSST, validity). 23dc2bc4dc 2024-12-09 an optional `compression_filter_t` (RLE, constant validity). • 876a09d63b 2026-11-05 Bitpacking select unpacks only the 32-value groups that contain selected rows (sparse UPDATE 31% faster). d6bf14694e 2026-11-09 uncompressed fixed-size gather (12-17% faster). 746abd0059 2026-11-13 avoids double-decoding DELTA_FOR groups. 02aa9fa280 2026-11-04 UPDATE fetches only the base values of updated rows (it previously decompressed 2,047 unneeded FSST strings).

### duckdb-074 — Write each decoder for its common case
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When scans are decode-bound, rewrite each encoding's inner loop for the dominant shape: fewer per-value branches, bulk copies for long runs, structure-of-arrays metadata unpacked up front, and table-driven bit reads.
Why it is faster: Generic decoders interleave control decisions with data movement, which serializes the work and adds a branch per value. Splitting control streams (flags, lengths) into their own arrays lets each be unpacked in a tight loop. Lookup tables replace computed masks and shifts. These are codec-specific code paths, so add them where profiles show decode dominating.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Stream-VByte-style varint decoding with separate control bytes. A 1-byte fast path in a varint decoder. • Web / backend: Custom binary protocols that put length and flag fields in a header array ahead of the payloads. • Embedded: Table-driven bit readers in codec firmware.
Watch out: Specialized loops multiply test surface. Keep a reference decoder and fuzz the fast path against it.
Seen in DuckDB: 4a2469fb88 2022-09-23 The Chimp bit reader gains mask and shift lookup tables ("much faster"). 5754674585 2022-09-29 592b359643 2022-09-29 store flags and leading-zero codes as separate packed arrays and unpack them in vectorized passes. • f69f9320ac 2022-10-12 Patas decodes a full 1024-value group into an array instead of keeping a 128-value cache that needed `memmove`. • c9d2be9e84 2024-11-30 A specialized uncompressed-string scan: `avg(strlen(l_comment))` SF10 0.042 s to 0.030 s (2db410fcbc 2024-11-29).

### duckdb-075 — Use heavyweight compression only where it pays: cold or large values, and adaptively for spill
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: General-purpose codecs (gzip, zstd) get great ratios but cost CPU on every access. Apply them to large, cold values at block granularity with an index for random access, drop them from hot paths, and choose them at runtime by measured cost.
Why it is faster: Compressing a whole vector of long strings beats per-value schemes on ratio, and a per-vector directory keeps it seekable. On hot data, decompression time outweighs the saved I/O, especially when lightweight encodings already apply. For spill files, whether compression helps depends on how contended I/O is at the moment, so measure write times per option and keep exploring.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Compress large API payloads but not tiny ones. Compress network links only when they are bandwidth-bound. • Data / databases: TOAST-style compression of large values only. Zstd blocks of log lines with an offset index. • Native / systems: Epsilon-greedy codec choice for scratch files based on measured throughput.
Watch out: A codec that was a win in one release can become a loss once lighter encodings arrive. DuckDB added gzip in 2022 and removed it in 2023. Re-measure periodically.
Seen in DuckDB: 96b36146cc 2022-06-07 Overflow strings compressed with gzip (miniz), storing both sizes. b1a2028f14 2022-06-15 decompresses straight from the pinned block when the value fits, with no scratch buffer. • 49aae284b3 2023-09-26 Gzip removed from overflow strings again, because it cost CPU on every read and write (991a3ad524 2023-09-25 stores raw length plus bytes). • 4ebeb16350 2024-11-20 Block-level ZSTD for large strings, with per-vector page id, offset, sizes and lengths for random access. • de91c645e2 2024-10-31 Adaptive spill compression: track the last write time for raw and for ZSTD levels -3/-1/1/3, pick the fastest with a bias toward compression, and explore occasionally. Single-threaded spill Q1 7.107 s to 5.845 s, another benchmark 65.5 to 59.1, while a small query regressed slightly (0.346 s to 0.380 s). Spill files are also smaller.

### duckdb-076 — Compress strings with a per-block symbol table that keeps each string separately decodable (FSST)
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Replace frequent 1-8 byte substrings with 1-byte codes from a small per-segment symbol table. Each string still decodes on its own with fast table lookups, so random access survives compression.
Why it is faster: High-cardinality strings such as URLs, logs and JSON keys don't dictionary-encode well, but they share substrings. FSST gets a good ratio with near-memcpy decode speed and no block-level decompression. Combined with a dictionary, it compresses the dictionary itself. Decoding straight into the destination removes a copy. Compressing from the real input, rather than fixed-size chunks, keeps greedy matching optimal.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: URL, log and JSON-key columns. Memory-resident key-value caches of short strings. • Embedded: Compact on-device string tables (UI strings, logs) that still allow single-string decode. • Web / backend: Compress per-record keys in a cache without losing per-key access.
Watch out: Symbol-table training costs time and needs representative samples. Short or unique strings gain little.
Seen in DuckDB: ffa0b9daf7 2022-10-03 "FSST compression": a symbol table per segment, bitpacked delta-encoded lengths, the scan caching the last decoded index to speed up delta decoding, and compaction at finalize. • 71520798ba 2025-05-07 DICT_FSST runs FSST over the dictionary and bitpacks both indices and string lengths. • 978e67093e 2026-08-05 `compressBulk` reads the input directly instead of copying 511-byte chunks, so matches no longer break at chunk boundaries (which also enables compressed LIKE).

### duckdb-077 — Compress floats by exploiting their structure: XOR with similar neighbours, or recover hidden decimals (Chimp/Patas, ALP)
Lens: compression · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Consecutive floats share sign, exponent and high mantissa bits, so XOR each value with a well-matching recent value and store only the meaningful bits. Better still, most doubles are decimals in disguise: scale them to exact integers, bitpack those, and store the values that don't round-trip as exceptions.
Why it is faster: Generic integer codecs fail on IEEE bit patterns. XOR schemes cut size substantially, and byte-aligned variants (Patas) decode faster than bit-aligned ones. ALP turns float columns into integer FOR and bitpacking, which is both smaller and very fast to decode. Sampling to choose the exponent and factor, with early exit, keeps analysis cheap. Exceptions must stay rare, or the scheme falls back to raw.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Gorilla-style metric storage. Price and sensor columns in Parquet-like formats. • Embedded: Compress sensor readings with fixed decimal precision as scaled integers on the device. • Web / backend: Encode JSON numbers with known decimals as scaled integers in compact binary payloads.
Watch out: Lossless means bit-exact. Handle -0.0, NaN payloads and infinities explicitly. Truly random doubles compress poorly with any scheme.
Seen in DuckDB: 5733a227cf 2022-10-20 Chimp128: XOR against the best of the last 128 values, found through a hash on the low bits. • d40d997a29 2022-11-03 Patas: byte-aligned trailing-zero and significant-byte counts for faster decoding. • 1d678c4a3e 2024-01-22 ALP: per-row-group sampling (`RG_SAMPLES=8`, `SAMPLES_PER_VECTOR=32`, early exit at 2) picks exponent and factor, then FOR plus bitpacking. Exceptions are detected with a predicated comparison. ALPRD dictionary-encodes the left bits of "real" doubles. 4bada3246e 2024-01-25 fixes -0.0.

### duckdb-078 — Allocate once, reset and reuse per batch
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Scratch buffers, output batches, executor state and hash-table shells should live as long as the operator or query, and be reset between batches or iterations instead of being freed and allocated again.
Why it is faster: Each allocation is a function call, possibly a lock, possibly a page fault and zeroing, plus a matching free. In a loop over thousands of batches these costs dominate small kernels. On many-core machines the global allocator becomes a point of contention, so cutting the allocation count improves scaling, not just speed. The cost is holding peak-sized buffers for the operator's lifetime, so size them to the batch, not the worst case.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pool request buffers in Go (`sync.Pool`) or Netty. Reuse `TextEncoder` and typed arrays across requests. • Embedded: Preallocate DSP and audio-callback scratch space at init, and never call malloc in the callback. • Native / systems: Reuse tensors across ML training iterations. Keep a `Vec` outside the loop and call `clear()` on it. • Data / databases: Profile allocation counts (heaptrack, jemalloc stats), not just bytes, when scaling to many cores.
Watch out: Reused buffers keep their peak size. Shrink or release them after an outlier batch. Cached state across iterations must be reset correctly, which is a source of subtle bugs (DuckDB reverted one cache).

### duckdb-079 — Bump-allocate same-lifetime objects from an arena and free them all at once
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: When many small objects die together (per query, per group, per metadata parse), carve them from large chunks with a pointer bump and release the whole arena in one call. Grow chunk sizes geometrically up to a cap.
Why it is faster: Allocating becomes a few instructions with no lock. Freeing millions of objects becomes freeing a few chunks. Objects end up packed together, which helps locality, and memory accounting is exact because you know the arena size. Pure doubling wastes memory at large sizes, so cap the chunk size. Arenas can't free individual objects, so long-lived arenas with churn leak.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-request pools (Apache/nginx). Per-request arenas in C++ or Rust services (`bumpalo`). • Native / systems: AST and IR arenas in compilers and parsers. Per-frame linear allocators in game engines. • Data / databases: Per-group aggregate state and per-query metadata in arenas, with exact memory tracking.
Watch out: Destructors don't run unless you track them, so don't put objects that own external resources in an arena. Long-lived arenas with churn grow without bound. Cap growth or reset periodically.

### duckdb-080 — Use fixed-size slab allocators and block pools for uniform objects
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: Netty
Principle: For objects of a few fixed sizes (tree nodes, storage blocks), allocate from large buffers sliced into slots and track free slots with a bitmask or a lock-free index queue. Reuse freed blocks, ideally CPU-local, instead of returning them to malloc.
Why it is faster: A slab avoids per-object malloc headers and fragmentation, and finds a free slot with a bit trick. Filling a buffer sequentially before searching for free bits keeps the fast path trivial. A pool of large blocks avoids repeated mmap, page faults and zeroing. Per-CPU or per-NUMA caches avoid cross-socket traffic. Even a one-element cache helps with remove-one-insert-one patterns. Slabs must be vacuumed to give memory back, and pools need decay so RSS doesn't stick at the peak.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Kernel and memcached slab allocators. DPDK per-socket mempools. tcmalloc per-CPU caches. • Web / backend: Netty pooled ByteBufs. Reuse evicted LRU nodes for the incoming entry. • Embedded: Static pools of fixed-size message buffers, with a bitmap for free slots.
Watch out: Pools hide leaks and keep RSS high without decay or trimming. Reserving most of RAM as virtual memory interacts with overcommit settings, so make it opt-in or lazy.
Seen in DuckDB: 55889ee74d 2022-06-29 When eviction frees a buffer of the needed size, it is handed to the new user directly instead of free plus malloc. 0ee73c4776 2022-06-29 34aaee460b 2022-07-01 spill blocks share a few temp files with a free-slot index, truncating when the tail frees. 4888a72601 2022-07-04 removes an allocation in the eviction queue. • 86084ec11b 2023-04-26 "Towards buffer managing the ART - no more tiny allocations": per-node-size allocators with a bitmask header per buffer (becf666d0d 2023-03-14, d1a1a0f5d3 2023-03-09 faster free list, 03d8f8c2e0 2023-03-15 bit tricks to find free bits, ff69b908c1 2023-03-15 fewer calls, 693f342de4 2023-03-31 adaptive vacuum threshold). 3a795d02b3 2023-02-01 tracks exact ART size. • 437aa2fe05 2023-10-25 The skip list used for windowed quantiles keeps a one-element node pool to avoid a new/delete pair per slide. • e120b5bc24 2025-11-15 `BlockAllocator`: one mmap of 80% of RAM, fixed-size blocks handed out by index through a concurrent queue with thread-local caches, and `madvise` to give memory back. ### duckdb-081 — Choose and tune the general-purpose allocator, and size requests to its classes
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: Varnish
Principle: The system allocator can serialize threads and hold on to freed memory. Use a scalable allocator, tune arenas and decay, trim memory back to the OS after spikes, and pick buffer sizes that land exactly on a cached size class.
Why it is faster: Thread-caching, size-class allocators remove lock contention. More arenas mean less sharing. Short decay and periodic trims bring RSS back down after peaks. An allocation just over a cached class falls through to fresh mmap, page faults and zeroing every time. A few bytes of header overhead can cost seconds of system time. Each knob trades footprint against speed, and the right settings differ by platform.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Link jemalloc or mimalloc into Rust and C++ services. Set `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX` for glibc JVMs in containers. Call `malloc_trim` after spikes in long-running Python or Node services. • Native / systems: Size structs and buffers to allocator size classes or huge-page multiples. • Embedded: Pick a pool block size that matches the largest common message exactly.
Watch out: Allocator settings that help one platform regress another. DuckDB toggled retain and huge pages several times. Benchmark per OS and architecture, and keep the knobs configurable.

### duckdb-082 — Start small, grow geometrically, and pre-size when the size is known
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: Envoy, simdjson, PostgreSQL
Principle: Per-item buffers should start tiny and double up to a cap. Successive tables should double in capacity. When the final size is known, allocate it up front. Never pre-allocate the worst case for every one of many small objects.
Why it is faster: Fixed-step growth is quadratic and worst-case pre-allocation wastes memory, which at millions of groups causes OOM. Geometric growth amortizes copies to O(1) per element. Doubling successive tables keeps the number of finalization steps logarithmic. When the size is known, one allocation avoids a series of reallocations, which some system allocators handle badly. Powers of two also make buffers reusable across requests.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Start per-connection buffers at 4 KB. Use `StringBuilder(capacity)` or `Buffer.allocUnsafe(n)` when the payload size is known. • Native / systems: `Vec::with_capacity` from size hints. Multimap value lists that start inline and grow. • Data / databases: Per-key buffers in group-by and windowing that scale with the actual group size, not the batch size.
Watch out: Doubling without a cap overshoots at large sizes. Pre-sizing from a bad estimate wastes memory as badly as worst-case allocation.
Seen in DuckDB: 8e4674f5a9 2019-02-21 Non-materializing streaming result sets. 51074cbdd5 2020-12-16 NumPy result conversion grows raw arrays with `realloc` instead of materializing twice. 8b55961eec 2020-12-16 avoids fetching already-materialized results. • 26451453dc 2021-11-20 The parser's fixed 1000-entry pointer array becomes a doubling array. • bafa9335e5 2022-06-05 LIST segments grow with NextPowerOfTwo. bf595f7162 2022-10-30 the column-data allocator starts at 4096 and grows by `min(last*2, last+BLOCK_SIZE)`. 7b17f449b2 2022-10-27 smaller allocations for tiny tables (c7286d2396 2022-10-25 the first segment is one vector, not one block; ### duckdb-083 — Allocate on first write; skip side structures nobody uses
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Don't give every instance its full buffers up front. Allocate payload storage, caches and directories when they are first written. Outputs that only reference or slice their inputs need no storage of their own.
Why it is faster: Many instances never use their buffers: columns that are only passed through, statistics on columns nobody queries, partitions that receive no rows. Eager allocation multiplies RSS by the instance count and adds init time. The cost is a check on each access path, and allocation can then happen at an awkward moment.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Create log files and directories on first write. Initialize per-request caches lazily. • Native / systems: Kotlin `by lazy`, Rust `OnceCell` for rarely used fields, copy-on-write buffers. • Data / databases: Allocate per-column statistics or sketches only when that column is analyzed.
Watch out: Lazy initialization on a shared object needs thread-safe publication. Watch for first-access latency spikes on latency-sensitive paths.
Seen in DuckDB: 210ccc2206 2024-01-04 The WAL is created lazily. 392ecc7dd1 2024-04-02 read_json defers allocation. 14e192d130 2024-04-22 Hive partition states are initialized lazily, and 5214320b99 2024-04-23 creates partition directories only when a partition is instantiated. • 6af32330b5 2024-10-27 `DistinctStatistics` no longer pre-allocates a 16 KB hash vector per column, which had been 78 MB of 116 MB when attaching 50 catalogs of 97 columns. 214997a87b 2024-10-28 puts hash caches back at a better layer. • dc5ab0ac4d 2026-10-19 Hash join skips buffers for reference-only output columns. 5d10bf4615 2026-10-20 does the same for Filter, Limit, Sample and Projection (peak buffer memory 341 to 177 MiB). 7ceda5103f 2026-10-26 `DataChunk::Initialize` allocates payloads lazily.

### duckdb-084 — Build outputs at their final size, in their final place
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: Envoy, Kafka, H2O
Principle: Compute each output's exact size first (count, then write), allocate once in the destination, and write or decode directly into it. Never build a temporary string or block and copy it over.
Why it is faster: Concatenation-style building reallocates repeatedly, and in-place insert or replace moves the tail every time, which is quadratic. Building in a temporary, then copying, doubles memory traffic. Two passes cost one extra cheap scan but remove both problems. Building large blocks in place also avoids partition-then-copy cycles.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pre-size buffers in JSON serializers. Use `Array.join` instead of repeated `+=`. Size with `snprintf(NULL,0,...)`, then format. • Native / systems: `String::with_capacity` from a computed length. `TextDecoder.decodeInto`-style APIs. • Data / databases: Write JSON-to-columnar ingestion straight into Arrow builders with no object tree in between.
Watch out: The sizing pass must exactly mirror the writing pass. Share one templated routine for both, as DuckDB did, or they drift apart.

### duckdb-085 — Eliminate hidden copies and per-row temporaries
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: Envoy, TigerBeetle
Principle: Hunt down copies nobody asked for: by-value parameters and loop variables, owning strings made from slices, case-folded copies made just to hash, staging buffers between stages, and per-value `memcpy` where one bulk copy would do.
Why it is faster: Each hidden copy costs a malloc and free once it exceeds the small-buffer limit, plus refcount traffic for shared pointers. Inside row loops these dominate. Views, references and in-loop transforms remove them. When values are already contiguous in the source, a single `memcpy` replaces thousands of small ones.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `const auto&` in C++ loops. Rust `&str` lookups via `Borrow`. `writev` for adjacent slices. • Web / backend: Case-insensitive header maps that hash without lowercasing copies. Avoid repeated `[]byte(string)` conversions in Go parsers. • Data / databases: Route rows to partitions by index instead of copying them into per-destination buffers.
Watch out: References and views must not outlive their source. Use a profiler (heaptrack, allocation sampling) to find copies. Reading code rarely finds them all.

### duckdb-086 — Route big memory through one buffer manager with a limit, pinning and cheap approximate eviction
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Put persistent pages, temporary intermediates and cached file data in one manager that enforces a global memory limit, pins blocks while in use, and evicts unpinned blocks. Use lock-free, lazily invalidated queues for eviction instead of a locked LRU list.
Why it is faster: One budget lets hash tables, sorts and caches share memory fairly, and lets them overflow to disk instead of crashing. Direct I/O with aligned buffers avoids the OS page cache holding a second copy. An approximate LRU built from (weak handle, timestamp) nodes in a concurrent queue avoids a global lock. Stale entries are skipped when dequeued and purged occasionally. Eviction order should follow the access plan: evict what is needed last, and free destroy-on-unpin buffers immediately. Pinning has a cost too, so keep hot, repeatedly probed blocks pinned and skip re-pinning the same block.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: A self-managed buffer pool (InnoDB-style) with direct I/O. Eviction hints from the query plan. • Web / backend: CLOCK or lazy-deletion LRU in an in-process cache. A single memory budget shared across caches. • Native / systems: `madvise(DONTNEED)` on pages that will be used last. Map renderers evicting the tiles that will be drawn last.
Watch out: Approximate LRU can evict hot blocks under adversarial patterns. Direct I/O needs aligned buffers and is a poor fit when the OS cache would have helped (DuckDB later made the I/O mode selectable).
Seen in DuckDB: 13cce32f9d 2019-05-04 Direct I/O on all platforms (O_DIRECT, F_NOCACHE, NO_BUFFERING) with aligned buffers. 8baeb76c35 2019-08-29 all block reads go through a caching buffer manager. 5cb684652f 2019-11-06 "Add a working Buffer Manager". 6d1a04e737 2020-01-02 the join hash table allocates blocks from it. • 7251a55379 2020-12-08 The global LRU lock is replaced by a concurrent queue of versioned eviction nodes. A stale timestamp means "skip" (PR 13b80d7042 2020-12-09). • d48e5c34d0 2022-06-17 The queue is purged on enqueue so dead entries don't pile up. • 740e8e8dd6 2021-08-03 Merge-sort readers skip unpinning and re-pinning when the next read is in the same block. 3d929d4f7a 2022-11-08 perfect-hash-join blocks are pinned once for the whole probe. • cf65f6bbe7 2024-10-21 Six eviction queues: buffers of higher partitions (processed later) are evicted first. Out-of-core TPC-H join 16.1 s to 15.2 s. • b1bae91af9 2024-08-28 Buffers destroyed on unpin are freed immediately instead of entering the eviction queue (2ff56935bd 2024-08-27). 570cfcb4cd 2024-08-20 destroyable blocks are unloaded right away. 3ce36ca9af 2024-06-06 single-threaded CSV reading releases old blocks when acquiring new ones.

### duckdb-087 — Pack many small objects into shared full-size blocks
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB
Principle: With fixed-size blocks, small segments and buffers waste most of a block each. Track partially filled blocks and pack new small items into the best fit, or sub-allocate tiny buffers from one shared block.
Why it is faster: File size and memory both drop. One shared block is a single allocation and a single unit the buffer manager can evict, where many tiny private allocations could not be evicted. Best-fit placement keeps fragmentation low. Freeing then needs per-region accounting, and compaction may be needed later.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Pack small files into large objects (the HDFS and S3 small-file problem). SSTable packing. • Native / systems: GPU buffer sub-allocation (VMA-style). Texture atlases. • Embedded: Pack small config records into one flash page instead of one page each.
Watch out: A shared block lives as long as its longest-lived occupant. Mixing very different lifetimes keeps memory pinned.
Seen in DuckDB: 5898d10da8 2021-08-10 Checkpointing tracks partially filled blocks and places a segment in the best fit (`lower_bound(segment_size)`). PR 7dee73fc89 2021-08-18 "Multi-Occupancy Blocks in the Storage". b2c128aa79 2021-08-14 aligns offsets within blocks. • 6415640753 2025-02-18 Small overflow strings are placed inside the vector instead of in a dedicated block each. • a33f37c884 2026-07-28 Transient segments sub-allocate from one full-sized temp buffer. 0acb6721d1 2026-11-04 extends this to validity and nested segments so they can be offloaded: an in-memory 256 MB load of 20M rows went from OOM to 3.4 s. • aeb9f4e670 2026-11-06 Checkpoint defers publishing segments and index buffers so those from many tables pack into shared blocks.

### duckdb-088 — Release memory as soon as a phase is done; stream instead of buffering
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Free big buffers at the end of the phase that uses them, not when the owning operator is torn down. Don't keep caches that are only briefly useful. Process large inputs in bounded chunks instead of loading them whole.
Why it is faster: Peak memory is the sum of what is alive at once. Freeing early lets the next phase run in memory instead of spilling. Long-lived objects that keep references to transient buffers silently pin them. Streaming makes memory constant in input size. The risk is freeing something a later step needs, so phase boundaries must be explicit.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Null out large references before long awaits. Hash uploads with streaming `update()`. Don't buffer gzip bodies for rewinds you'll never do. • Embedded: Verify firmware images chunk by chunk. Free decode buffers between pipeline stages. • Data / databases: Free a partition's hash table right after it is consumed. Keep REPL result history weakly referenced.
Watch out: Early release makes ownership more complex. Use RAII or explicit phase objects so a later step can't touch freed memory.
Seen in DuckDB: 891443936d 2021-08-29 A Parquet scan no longer keeps every string dictionary alive through a long-lived dummy result. 3b8ad037bf 2021-12-22 bounds Parquet writer memory (smaller max page size, configurable row-group size). • 866efc2db6 2022-11-08 Parallel DISTINCT aggregates skip the unneeded main group table and destroy each partition's hash table as soon as it is scanned. • f6f46d1824 2023-10-11 CSV clears cached buffers before emitting the next chunk (ac658cf2df 2023-10-10). 19f5fa14b3 2023-04-26 non-seekable compressed inputs disable reset, so earlier data isn't retained (PR a273ac8442 2023-04-26). 60ed227816 2023-09-01 read buffers are initialized per thread on demand. • f714995d8a 2023-08-23 Extension signature checks feed 8 KB chunks into an incremental SHA-256 instead of reading the whole file (72c4057a1a 2023-08-22, 38d0265920 2023-08-22). • c27c0529b1 2025-02-17 The Parquet column reader stops caching the compressed buffer (50-100% extra memory, no speed change). 45de60480e 2025-10-02 window sort buffers are destroyed eagerly. ed31512477 2025-11-10 fbaf3ed806 2025-11-06 44b706b2b7 2025-11-07 HashedSort releases memory after each phase. 1cd8d83750 2025-02-04 a 5-row reservoir sample stops over-allocating 10×2048 slots. • 77ee7d296d 2026-04-28 The CLI drops the previous result early if the next query doesn't reference it: repeated runs went from about 5-8 s to 1.4-2.7 s. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-099): 1332c1e1440 1999-05-17 The genetic join planner frees memory after evaluating each gene, which "bounds memory usage". 1cffbfcb56d 2000-07-27 Planning memory is freed when planning completes. • 6f922ef88e4 2012-04-04 dblink uses libpq's row processor to stream rows into a spillable tuplestore. 2f557167b19 2014-05-07 libpq stops doubling its input buffer when space can be reclaimed by discarding processed messages.

### duckdb-089 — Make in-memory structures relocatable so they can spill (pointer swizzling)
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Before evicting rows that hold raw pointers into heaps, rewrite those pointers as offsets relative to their block ("swizzle"), and restore them after reload. Then any block can be written out and read back at a different address.
Why it is faster: Without relocatability, pointer-rich structures (sort payloads, join hash tables, string collections) must stay resident, so data larger than memory fails outright. Swizzling is a linear pass, and doing it in vector-sized batches with each row's heap pointer loaded once keeps it cache-friendly. Data that never leaves memory should skip swizzling entirely, and so should inlined strings.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Relative or offset pointers in mmap'd files and shared memory mapped at different addresses. FlatBuffers-style serialization. • Web / backend: Store indices instead of object references in structures you may persist or send to a worker. • Embedded: Position-independent data blobs in flash that can be copied to any RAM address.
Watch out: Every pointer field must be found, or a missed one corrupts memory after reload. Keep swizzling state per block and test spill paths under tiny memory limits.
Seen in DuckDB: 38b72293e5 2021-04-29 "External merge sort" introduces `SwizzleColumns`/`UnswizzlePointers`. b5a091818a 2021-12-06 makes the loop batch-wise and cache-friendly (PR 0546552f6d 2021-12-08). • 0e8ff9fff9 2022-08-25 Range-join data swizzling. 1a02280262 2022-05-16 fewer buffer allocations when swizzling. • 2e664acf15 2022-12-08 Strings in `ColumnDataCollection` can be written to disk (b3f2749272 2022-11-28). a166b67be7 2022-11-28 unswizzles in parallel. c517fcf07d 2022-11-30 skips inlined strings. The 2022 out-of-core hash join also swizzles its hash-table blocks. • 005bf35e9c 2022-08-22 Windows keep heap blocks pinned when they won't be spilled, so they skip swizzling.

### duckdb-090 — Spill by hash partition, and adapt partition count and order to memory
Lens: allocation · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When state exceeds memory, radix-partition it by hash bits so that only one partition, or a few, must be resident at a time. Choose the partition count from available memory, process small partitions first, detect skew that partitioning cannot fix, and keep spill files cheap.
Why it is faster: Peak memory drops from the whole table to one partition. Building on many small partitions first means more probe data can be handled immediately instead of being spilled. Too many partitions under low memory thrash, and repartitioning a single dominant key only adds I/O. Spill files live within one process, so they need no checksums. Smaller physical blocks let threads do I/O concurrently.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Grace and hybrid hash joins sized from free memory. External group-by in ETL. • Web / backend: A log aggregator that flushes per-key-hash buckets to disk when RSS passes a budget, then merges each bucket on its own. • Native / systems: Skip checksums on scratch files that are rebuilt on failure. Special-case heavy hitters instead of re-sharding them.
Watch out: Partitioning can't split one hot key, so detect that case. Spill code paths are rarely exercised, so test them under small memory limits.
Seen in DuckDB: e50755fefa 2021-08-15 External sort separates the logical merge partition from a small physical block size for concurrent I/O. 38bcbb8271 2021-07-06 shrinks the last heap block to the bytes used. 2e67ff2595 2021-08-16 retunes the radix-sort budget afterwards. • ee7375fa4f 2021-08-04 Temporary spill blocks are written and read without checksums. • b51257ea69 2022-08-24 Out-of-core hash join: 4-8 radix bits, at least 8 partitions per probe round, empty partitions skipped, and probe chunks split into "probe now" and "spill". ae9d7c3236 2022-08-04 deletes probe blocks after reading so they are never written back. • eea0a67630 2023-07-04 Out-of-core hash aggregate: ad399ff570 2023-06-02 abandons a thread's table once it passes 60% of memory divided by threads. The 2023 rework c54d7f5968 2023-09-04 adds 3 radix bits, unpins and repartitions the abandoned data. • 62582045a3 2024-12-18 Spilling joins choose a bitmap of the smallest partitions first. e7765f25f1 2024-12-30 keeps that order afterwards. • 75c349e47f 2024-12-04 Hopeless skew (one dominant key) is detected and repartitioning stops, and buffers go through a non-spilling allocator (e57d8a0405 2024-12-03): a single-key Parquet join went from about 40 s to about 6 s. • eb7da1057b 2026-03-11 Radix bits for external aggregation are computed from available memory. a5c128a833 2026-01-02 forces repartitioning even with 1-2 threads. • cb4190eb72 2026-11-12 Abandon heuristics for arena-only states: an h2o q08 run that hit OOM at 500 MB after 68 s now takes 0.39 s. 1c75d17a7a 2026-08-13 spills aggregate states via export. 32de60b035 2026-10-30 a sort may go external after a thread has finished. c46869255d 2026-10-30 frees segment buffers once copied. 23807a8403 2026-11-02 sequential CSV buffers spill instead of being re-read. 1e0351e4d2 2026-10-26 out-of-core sort with less redundant spilling and better skew resistance (20-50%).

### duckdb-091 — Cache parsed file metadata, keyed by file identity and validated cheaply
Lens: caching · Source: DuckDB · Also: curl
Principle: Keep parsed footers, schemas and file stats in a process-wide cache keyed by path. Revalidate with a cheap signal (modification time, version tag), bound the cache with LRU, and make it evictable under memory pressure.
Why it is faster: Repeated queries over the same files skip footer reads and deserialization, which for remote files means skipping round trips. The validation check must be much cheaper than re-reading. For remote objects, even a HEAD request can cost more than it saves. Coarse mtime resolution can hide fast rewrites, so add a safety margin. An unbounded cache becomes a memory leak.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Use ETag/Last-Modified for conditional requests. Cache compiled templates or parsed configs by mtime. • Native / systems: LSP servers and build tools caching parsed files by (path, mtime, size). • Data / databases: Cache footers in a data-lake query service, capped by bytes rather than entry count.
Watch out: Weak validation such as mtime alone can serve stale data. Know your consistency needs, and document when remote checks are skipped.
Seen in DuckDB: fe7b981b7d 2020-11-09 An `ObjectCache` and a Parquet metadata cache with read time (PR d2d65e8ac6 2020-11-10). 40926fb2b9 2020-11-28 re-reads if mtime and read time are too close (timestamp resolution). 71049da246 2020-11-20 is a follow-up fix. • 7ad346ff93 2021-12-20 Typed cache lookup invalidated when `mtime >= read_time`, replacing `dynamic_pointer_cast` with a type-tag check. • a386815bf3 2022-12-08 A query-local or global HTTP metadata cache (size, last-modified) with expiry and invalidation (46890acbd7 2022-11-15). • 798a0dede1 2024-03-07 The object cache skips the last-modified check for remote files (PR 953959558c 2024-03-07). • a35d8b6d5b 2025-05-03 Partition stats come from cached metadata with no extra file-system calls. b8db3cde7d 2025-12-29 the Parquet metadata cache becomes a memory-capped LRU. • 278d021a04 2026-01-20 The object cache is registered under the buffer pool so it can be evicted under memory pressure. • Also seen in curl (curl-077): 3c16697ebd 2022-09-28 OpenSSL keeps the parsed `X509_STORE` per multi handle with the CA file name and a timestamp, shares it via `X509_STORE_up_ref`, and rebuilds after 24 h or when the file differs; 1fdca35ddd added `CURLOPT_CA_CACHE_TIMEOUT`. • 2039253c6e 2023-03-24 The test runner memoizes its `getpart*` readers "to prevent redundant test case file parsing". • 1af46f2f93 2023-11-03 The same for Schannel, identifying in-memory blobs by size plus SHA-256.

### duckdb-092 — Cache expensive-to-build handles, artifacts and learned state across calls
Lens: caching · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Reflection handles, module imports, database instances, grammar matchers, inverse lookup maps and adaptive tuning state are costly to derive and change rarely. Resolve them once and reuse them for later calls, statements or files.
Why it is faster: These costs are per call, not per row, but in OLTP-style or driver-heavy use they dominate. An O(n) reverse lookup done once per parameter becomes O(n2). Warm-starting adaptive state skips the learning phase on each new input. Caching too much can backfire, though. DuckDB made prepared-plan caching less aggressive so that plans could be re-bound once parameter values were known.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Connection pools. Caching `Method` objects in Java reflection. Memoizing required modules. • Native / systems: Build lookup tables and parser automata once at startup. Build enum reverse maps once. • Data / databases: Carry learned predicate order or codec choice from one file or partition to the next.
Watch out: Cached plans and handles can encode stale assumptions (parameter values, schema). Invalidate them on change, and re-plan when the specifics matter.
Seen in DuckDB: ae0f79afbe 2022-05-04 JNI class, method and field ids are cached. 40021bd379 2022-07-08 626900dac9 2022-06-28 a Python import cache. • c5d35b30cc 2022-10-12 Database instances are cached (2ebc74ec9f 2022-08-16 generic instance cache). 4c2a609bd8 2022-10-19 ODBC/JDBC use the instance cache. • 0761d5cc7a 2025-12-24 `duckdb_parameter_name` memoizes index-to-name. Drivers calling it per parameter had made binding O(n2). cd72163fe8 2025-08-11 replaces find-then-insert double lookups with `emplace`. • d22d3385bf 2026-03-17 The PEG matcher is built once and cached: parsing about 10x faster. 9a7bbe6f15 2026-02-10 the opposite case: less aggressive prepared-plan caching, re-binding with known parameters. • 5af66d401f 2026-04-23 Each thread caches the adaptive filter order it learned and seeds the next file's scan with it, mapped through global column positions.

### duckdb-093 — Cache per-distinct-value results next to the dictionary, keyed by dictionary identity
Lens: caching · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When consecutive batches share a dictionary (same storage segment or file page), give it a cheap identity and reuse the results computed from it (entry hashes, entry-to-group mappings, probe results) until the identity changes.
Why it is faster: Without the cache, per-entry work is repeated for every batch that references the same dictionary. Hashing long strings once and storing the hashes with the dictionary removes repeated hashing, and the gain grows with string length. A "resolved count" lets warm batches skip even the discovery pass. The identity must really change when the content changes, and shared caches need thread-safe initialization.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Cache a decoded-schema mapping by schema fingerprint in Avro or Kafka consumers. Reuse a compiled template while its id is unchanged. • Native / systems: String interning with precomputed hashes (as Java and Python cache string hashes). • Data / databases: Resolve GeoIP or category lookups per distinct value within a batch and reuse them across batches from the same page.
Watch out: Using a pointer as identity is unsafe if memory is reused for a different dictionary. Tie the identity to a lifetime or a version.
Seen in DuckDB: 2be6f64aed 2024-12-09 `DictionaryId` (segment pointer for native storage, file column offset for Parquet), and the aggregate hash table caches lookups keyed by it (df790570a7 2024-12-08). • 82eb38df57 2025-08-13 A lazily initialized buffer of dictionary hashes: 1.29x faster at 16-byte strings, 2.26x at 256 bytes, ClickBench Q34 about 5% faster. 56cfbb0c2b 2025-10-20 makes it thread-safe. 1a49242623 2025-09-11 avoids a copy of it. • eb90869f57 2026-05-21 Hash-join probing per distinct dictionary entry, with a per-thread state keyed by dictionary id: 0.287 s to 0.120 s. dc9dea3e73 2026-05-21 a `resolved_count` gate lets warm chunks skip the N-row discovery walk in aggregation.

### duckdb-094 — One-entry "same as last time?" memo in front of an expensive lookup
Lens: caching · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Data often comes in runs (sorted dates, repeated time zones). Before an expensive computation or map lookup, check whether the input equals the previous one and reuse the previous result. Put a per-thread cache behind that check for the general case.
Why it is faster: A single comparison replaces a calendar computation or an object construction on every repeat. On random data the check costs almost nothing. A per-thread map behind it avoids rebuilding heavy objects without needing locks.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Locale and formatter caches in i18n. A regex compile cache. DNS caches in HTTP clients. • Embedded: Cache the last RTC-to-calendar conversion, since consecutive readings share a date. • Data / databases: Memoize the last key's hash bucket or partition in sorted-input loops.
Watch out: Batch-carried state must reset at batch and thread boundaries. A missed reset returns wrong answers, not just slow ones.
Seen in DuckDB: d5edb8ca23 2020-11-22 Year extraction has a quick out when the date falls in the same year as the previous row, carried across the batch (cf2719c5d3 2020-11-23). a3ee53b709 2020-12-10 restores the cache after a regression. • daa81697e3 2026-08-04 ICU time zones are cached per thread, keyed by name, with a last-value check: 10M rows cycling through 638 zones went from 2.508 s to 1.464 s single-threaded, and up to 2.4x faster at 16 threads.

### duckdb-095 — Replace unpredictable branches with arithmetic (predication)
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When a branch depends on data with a near-random outcome, compute both sides or turn the condition into a 0/1 number and use it in arithmetic. Write unconditionally, then advance a cursor by the boolean.
Why it is faster: A mispredicted branch flushes the pipeline (around 15-20 cycles on modern cores). Near 50% selectivity a filter loop mispredicts about every other row. `out[n] = i; n += cond;` has no control dependency, so the loop runs at a steady rate whatever the data and can often be auto-vectorized. The cost is a few extra instructions per element and an occasional wasted store. With highly skewed outcomes (almost always true or almost always false) a well-predicted branch is just as fast, so measure.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In JS or WASM, filter typed arrays with `out[n] = i; n += pred | 0;` instead of `if` plus `push`. Merge-join two sorted ID lists with the branchless two-pointer step. • Embedded: Branchless clamping and selection (`x = a ^ ((a ^ b) & -cond)`) keeps timing constant and avoids pipeline stalls on simple in-order cores. • Native / systems: Branchless Lomuto partition in quicksort. Stream compaction in SIMD or GPU kernels. eBPF packet filters. • Data / databases: Any column-store predicate that builds a selection vector, and the merge step of LSM compaction or external sort.
Watch out: It only pays when the branch is actually unpredictable. Compilers sometimes turn your branchless code back into a branch (or the reverse), so check the generated assembly. Predicated loads must stay in bounds, because both sides are evaluated.
Seen in DuckDB: e2d559703b 2020-09-30 `BinaryExecutor`/`TernaryExecutor` select loops change from `if (...) sel[count++] = idx` to `sel.set_index(count, idx); count += result;`. 49530fcb00 applies the same pattern to the numeric segment scan-filter loops, followed by 322ab3af96. • fe52c27890 2020-11-23 PR #1138 "Use predication in filters" makes the pattern the default for filters. • 6a92c24120 2021-04-14 "branchless hotlooping" in the sort operator. 616d6647bf then makes the merge of two sorted runs branchless: compute `l_smaller` as 0/1 for a batch, then copy `l_smaller*size` bytes from the left and `r_smaller*size` from the right, and advance each pointer by the flag. • 983659be12 2023-06-23 Compressed materialization ships a branchless string compress/decompress (b0fcde39f1): a fixed-size memcpy from the inline string area replaces length-dependent branches. • 1b4c128b3e 2024-06-13 predicated selection-vector construction in `TemplatedFilterSelection` (column segment filters). ALP compression 1d678c4a3e detects exceptions with a predicated comparison. • 219bafadb7 2025-02-18 Branchless hashing of inlined strings (12 bytes or fewer): `h *= K * not_empty + (1 - not_empty)` turns the step into a no-op for empty input, and fixed-width loads replace variable `memcpy` (b53879b1a8). The result is asserted equal to the generic hash. Parquet write of SF10 lineitem went from 2.6s to 2.4s together with other fast paths. • dc1b42d0ad 2026-04-13 Single-pass unique-key LEFT join uses `result_count += found_match[j]`. 299408623e uses the "blind store" in the unrolled `BinaryExecutor` path, and 67a0a733f1 in `SelectNullLoop`. • 688937993a 2026-09-22 Parquet `DictionaryDecoder::Filter`: `new_sel.set_index(approved, row); approved += filter_result[offset];`. The filtered lineitem scan got 1.13x faster on EPYC and 1.25-1.33x on Apple M1.

### duckdb-096 — Hoist invariant work and checks out of the hot loop
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB · Also: libuv, Netty, PostgreSQL
Principle: Anything that does not change across iterations should be computed once before the loop: compiled patterns, metadata lookups, smart-pointer dereferences, bounds checks against a known worst case, and error-throwing validation.
Why it is faster: Repeated out-of-line calls and loads inside a loop cost cycles directly. They also block the compiler: it cannot vectorize or keep values in registers when a call might alias memory, or when a throw may happen mid-loop. One check against the maximum possible size (for example the maximum varint length) replaces a check per element. The trade-off is that the batch-level check must be conservative, so a slower fallback path is needed near buffer ends.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Build `new RegExp`, `Intl` formatters and JSON-schema validators once outside the request loop. Read `arr.length` or getters once. Encode a request body once before retries. • Embedded: Check `remaining >= MAX_FRAME_HEADER` once per frame, then parse without per-byte checks. • Native / systems: In Rust, an upfront `assert!(buf.len() >= n)` lets the compiler drop bounds checks. In C++, copy a `shared_ptr` target into a local reference before the loop. • Data / databases: Precompile constant patterns, paths and casts once per query or per batch when an argument is a constant vector.
Watch out: Hoisted values must really be invariant. Watch for data that changes between batches, such as a dictionary or pattern bound per file. A worst-case check needs a correct slow path for the tail of the buffer.
Seen in DuckDB: f66194d044 2021-08-10 Window boundary `IsScalar` was called twice per row. Caching it in `WindowBoundariesState` cut the integer range-window benchmark by 7%. • 49df7c2e61 2022-07-29 Jaro-Winkler detects a constant argument and builds its similarity cache once. In PR 3d23491fa7 the hive-partition regex is compiled once per filter instead of once per filename (20b89d2a9e, 9666f0795d). 2b8dcb8905 caches the constant regex in `regexp_replace`. • 5b768c8d42 2023-06-20 Collation keys: the `icu::UnicodeString` is built once and reused for both `getSortKey` calls (the retry with a larger buffer), a8457976c8. • 1584c228c8 2025-02-11 The RLE/bit-packing decoder checks buffer space once for the maximum varint length (5 bytes) instead of per byte, about 10% faster. cc188ac698 removes the per-iteration "exhausted?" check from fixed-size array comparison. 4dfc5eb2ab stops DENSE_RANK recomputing state per chunk, and 057f9753cb reuses peer ends (COUNT(*) 35% faster). • 8234eb4393 2026-04-07 Across 59 files, `StringVector::AddString(result, ...)`, which re-validated the target per call, becomes one `GetStringHeap(result)` before the loop followed by `heap.AddString(...)`. • da40609cca 2026-06-23 Constant-pattern ILIKE lowercases the pattern once, reuses one scratch buffer and dispatches `%term%` to a SIMD substring search: 2.4s to 0.9s. bea13c1574 recognizes "capture then truncate" regex shapes in `regexp_replace`. • 20180ceb03 2026-07-28 Dictionary-index validation throw hoisted out of the scan loop and a `shared_ptr` access removed: `SELECT DISTINCT l_shipinstruct` SF10 went from 160ms to 70ms. • 3a8bf4bdf4 2026-10-27 Chunk counts cached in `ReinsertTuples` and `PerfectAggregateHashTable::AddChunk`: perfect-HT `simple_group` took 31.8% less time and 43.6% fewer instructions. 8bf2888bcb hoists invariant accesses out of hash probing and row scatter/gather. • Also seen in libuv (libuv-043): c8c9fe1c74 2012-06-05 "move memset out of recvmsg inner loop": in `uv__udp_recvmsg` the `msghdr` is zeroed and filled once, and only the fields `recvmsg` overwrites are reset on each iteration. • cbac5f1693 2017-05-31 In the z/OS `epoll_wait` emulation, the scan of the pollfd array stops once `reventcount` reaches the count `poll()` returned.
