### duckdb-097 — Make hot calls inlinable: static dispatch and header-visible accessors
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Pass the operation as a type (template parameter or generic), not as a function pointer, virtual object or type-erased lambda. Put tiny hot accessors where every caller can see them, so the compiler can inline them into the loop.
Why it is faster: An indirect call costs a few cycles, and its bigger cost is that it is an optimization barrier. The compiler cannot vectorize across it, hoist loads past it or keep state in registers. Once inlined, the kernel body merges with the loop and auto-vectorization becomes possible. A function defined in another translation unit cannot be inlined without LTO, so per-row or per-token accessors pay full call overhead. The trade-off is more code: every instantiation is a copy.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In JS, keep hot helpers small and monomorphic (always called with the same shapes) so the JIT inlines them. Avoid passing a different callback each call in tight loops. • Embedded: Header-only `static inline` accessors for register and buffer access. Avoid function-pointer tables in per-sample DSP paths. • Native / systems: Pass comparator types to `std::sort`. Use Rust generics (`impl Fn`) instead of `dyn Fn` in hot loops. Kotlin `inline` functions. • Data / databases: Template kernel loops on an operator struct so arithmetic, comparisons and hashing fuse into one loop.
Watch out: Each specialization grows the binary and compile time. Large `static inline` helpers that the compiler refuses to inline are duplicated into every translation unit (see the build entry). Inline only small, hot functions.
Seen in DuckDB: 2d6881f39d 2020-01-20 Adds `StandardOperatorWrapper`, `SingleArgumentOperatorWrapper` and `LambdaWrapper` to `BinaryExecutor`. The message notes that normal lambdas lowered execution speed "because vectorization broke". • 5bf7da0881 2022-05-12 HyperLogLog always uses the dense representation "so we can inline" (e72dca6dc7), and the radix code becomes header-only for inlining (78ef0fcaa9). • 8e1076366c 2022-11-04 String split drops a virtual `StringSplitIterator` class hierarchy for a templated static `Find` policy that calls the optimized substring search (b9c39e82f5). • bca492b4af 2024-01-22 The CSV parser force-inlines its hot process function (ae105e4a6c). • d60c509f77 2026-07-17 `Vector::size`, which was 2.8% of ingest CPU, is cached and inlined (ingestion median -4.4%). ce80890a0e inlines `Value::IsNan`, which was 5.27% of an ingest profile. • dee65ec10f 2026-11-17 Arena `AllocateAligned/AlignNext/GetPosition/Rewind` move to the header, with only the slow `RewindSlow` case out of line: the parser runs 6.9% fewer instructions (geomean).

### duckdb-098 — Keep the hot path small: outline cold code and mark likely branches
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Lay out code so the common case falls through. Move error construction and rare cases into separate, non-inlined, `noreturn`/cold functions, and tell the compiler which branch is likely.
Why it is faster: Building an exception message (string formatting, allocation) inside a small function makes it look expensive to the inliner, so the whole function stops being inlined even though the throw never runs. Outlining leaves a compare plus a branch to a cold call, which the compiler happily inlines. Likely/unlikely hints put the hot path in straight-line code and the cold path out of the instruction cache. It costs nothing at run time. The main risk is annotating the wrong branch.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In V8, keep `throw new Error(`...${x}`)` in a separate helper so the hot function stays small enough to inline and optimize. • Embedded: Put fault handlers and logging in cold sections (`__attribute__((cold))`) so the hot loop fits in the small instruction cache or flash prefetch buffer. • Native / systems: Rust `#[cold]`/`#[inline(never)]` on error constructors. C++20 `[[likely]]`/`[[unlikely]]`. `__builtin_expect` in parsers and codecs. • Data / databases: Overflow and cast-failure paths in kernels: return a flag, and build the error message only in an out-of-line function.
Watch out: A wrong hint costs real performance on the actual common path. Profile-guided optimization (PGO) usually gets branch layout right automatically. Outlining only helps where the inliner was being blocked.
Seen in DuckDB: fb89452728 2021-07-26 "Place most likely branch first": `if (DUCKDB_LIKELY(OP::Operation(input, output))) return output;` in vector casts. • 6c0c1a68cd 2026-07-10 `DataChunk::size()` and `optional_idx` accessors move their throws out of line (size falls back to `DeriveSize()`). 1,251 previously out-of-line call sites became inlined, and the TPC-DS SF3 geomean got about 13% faster. • ff89fb7f6e 2026-08-03 "Checks inline, throws out of line" applied widely: CLI binary 2.44% smaller and about 8-9% faster on average across benchmark suites.

### duckdb-099 — Strength reduction and closed-form arithmetic
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB · Also: Aeron
Principle: Replace expensive operations with cheaper equivalents: modulo by a power of two becomes a mask, division by a runtime-constant divisor becomes multiply-and-shift, and an iterative loop becomes a formula.
Why it is faster: 64-bit integer division costs 20-90 cycles and is not pipelined, while a multiply is 3-4 cycles and an AND is 1. Generic library functions (`fmod`, 128-bit DivMod) handle corner cases you may not have. Loops that step one unit at a time do O(n) work when O(1) would do. The trade-off is that you must prove the equivalence (operand ranges, signedness, overflow).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Shard selection with `hash & (n-1)`. Bucket timestamps by fixed intervals with one integer division instead of date-library calls. Compute pagination offsets directly. • Embedded: Power-of-two ring buffers. Declare lookup tables `static const` so they live in flash and are not copied to the stack. • Native / systems: libdivide or fastmod for non-power-of-two hash table capacities. Allocator size-class math in closed form. • Data / databases: Seek in fixed-width or bit-packed data with arithmetic instead of stepping. Truncate fixed-width time units with integer math.
Watch out: Masks need exact powers of two. Fastdiv needs care with signed values and with the hugeint range. Floating-point rewrites are exact only for proven operand ranges. Compilers already reduce compile-time constants, so this pays off for runtime-constant values.
Seen in DuckDB: 9a932835d0 2019-06-25 The aggregate hash table uses `bitmask = size - 1` with a power-of-two capacity, and 84dd0a646e asserts `(e & bitmask) == (e % capacity)`. The join hash table in 6fcb5ef8e9 also uses `NextPowerOfTwo` and a mask. • de2a812e33 2024-07-05 `std::fmod(abs_value, 256)` in double-to-varint becomes `x - floor(x/256)*256` (the commit claims 100x). • c44752275d 2024-08-26 Division by a constant uses Lemire's fastdiv: precompute `M = 2^64/d + 1` once per vector (9104bf31df). The optimizer rewrites constant-divisor division automatically (55c115fa09), 8/16/64-bit variants are added (c686ca349a), and the hugeint multiply is inlined when `__uint128` exists (73ac1926cb). • 5849139fa0 2025-05-07 Hash-join probing recomputes `ht_offset`/salt from the hash instead of storing and reloading them, because one AND is as cheap as a load. • 848dc0ebc9 2026-02-09 Parquet bit-pack skip: an inner while-loop becomes a division per value, and then 64c6ef7c92 makes it a single `total_bits` computation for the whole skip. • 8404a926e0 2026-05-05 `date_trunc` to hour/minute/second/day uses one floor division (`TruncFixed`) instead of a calendar decompose and recompose: 531ms to 214ms (2.48x). • b9c30a9521 2026-08-03 Bitpacked hugeint `x % (1<<d)`, which ran a bit-at-a-time 128-bit DivMod, becomes `x & ((1<<d)-1)`: 16.8x faster per value. • 7ea45a710c 2026-08-27 De Bruijn tables in `CountZeros` made `static constexpr`, because GCC was copying 512 bytes to the stack on each call: an HLL test went from 2.104s to 0.399s. • 2569474e67 2026-10-19 `FixedSizeAllocator` layout computed in closed form, with the loop handling only the tail: 1,161 iterations down to 14 per ART, verified exhaustively over 8.1M parameter combinations. • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-077): efea6a6f20 2014-03-18 `BitUtil.align`; `d013509e23` (2014-03-20) ring buffer with `mask = capacity - 1`; `b8b7bbb12f` (2014-04-27) element offset as `ARRAY_BASE + (index << SHIFT)`; `66ac563432` (2014-07-14) enforced power-of-two term sizes; `ce4fbbb894` (2014-07-17) position from `numberOfTrailingZeros(capacity)`. • 2949229896 2014-10-07 `/1000000` replaced with `/1048576` in timer-wheel delay math ("optimizable division"). `4b9ee9cfff` (2014-08-31) common power-of-two check. • b1478ae54d 2015-07-05 C++ `fastMod3` for partition indexing; `abea12fd1f` (2015-06-09) "Save branch on nextPartitionIndex calculation". Later `e1adbaea16` (2017-06-06) "rely on compiler optimization of modulo PARTITION_COUNT" and removed the hand trick. • 6e07792aea 2018-01-22 `positionBitsToShift()` as a switch over valid term lengths, "more efficient implementation when running in un-compiled code"; archive code replaced `/ termLength` and `% termLength` with shift and mask. • 1301c817b2 2018-12-14 `m_termBeginPosition += termLength` on rotation instead of recomputing; `8cb2834f9a` (2018-12-14) and `996bf2aaf9` (2018-12-15) derive position and window from local state instead of decoding a volatile tail. • 81e519749a 2019-06-27 Replaced `/4`, `/8`, `/2` with shifts in code "that will happen in the interpreter before the JIT can optimise" (also `9af032b1bf`, 2019-08-23). • 2b6a77abd7 2022-01-28 Removed a redundant `& 0xFFFF_FFFFL` from `packTail`, which runs on every tail update. `52b14e673c` (2015-12-03) had dropped a redundant mask from the header write to "save a few cycles".

### duckdb-100 — Precomputed lookup tables instead of computation or comparison chains
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB · Also: Varnish, PostgreSQL
Principle: When an input domain is small (one byte) or the common range is bounded (dates in 1970-2050), precompute the answer into a table and replace logic with one indexed load. Add a one-entry "same as last time" check for runs.
Why it is faster: A chain of comparisons (`c == ',' || c == '"' || ...`) or a calendar computation is many instructions and data-dependent branches. A table lookup is one L1-resident load with no branch. Tables must stay small (a few KB) to remain in cache, and need a fallback for values outside the table range. A last-value check exploits sorted or clustered input for free.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: URL, HTML and JSON escaping with a 256-entry "needs escape" table. Character-class checks in tokenizers and template engines. • Embedded: CRC, gamma-correction and sine tables in flash. Day-of-year to month tables for RTC conversion. • Native / systems: Table-driven lexers and HTTP header parsers. Popcount and bit-reversal tables on CPUs without the instruction. • Data / databases: Date-part extraction, time zone offset tables, and per-byte state machines for CSV and log formats.
Watch out: Large tables evict hot data from L1 and lose to arithmetic. Range fallbacks must be correct at the boundaries (DuckDB fixed a fencepost). On wide-SIMD hardware, a vector compare can beat a per-byte table (see the SWAR entry).
Seen in DuckDB: d5edb8ca23 2020-11-22 `EXTRACT(year)` keeps a table of year-start day numbers for common years (1988-2052), does a 3-level split plus predicated `result += n >= table[i]`, and first checks whether the date falls in the same year as the previous row (cf2719c5d3, PR d4546550d0). • 890b0e6d17 2020-12-11 Month extraction indexes a 366-entry day-of-year to month table (`MonthPerDayOfYear`, `LeapMonthPerDayOfYear`) instead of looping over cumulative days. • 3a0cdcef2b 2023-05-03 The CSV writer's `requires_quotes` uses a 256-entry table. The CSV sniffer/reader moves to a `transition_array[state][byte]` state machine (3b58f4490f). 78b21bf685 reuses the shared `ascii_to_upper_map`. • bca492b4af 2024-01-22 CSV parser: the transition table is flipped to `[char][state]` for locality (585f463c39). A 256-entry `skip_char_lookup` lets STANDARD/QUOTED states run a tight loop past bytes that cannot change state (3b60bc5c18). 7456cef73e uses an array instead of an `unordered_map` in projection pushdown, and 348f4a1ae2 makes dialect detection more cache-local. • 7d742ed325 2024-06-13 `DateLookupCache`: a table indexed by days since the epoch over 1970-2050 turns year/month/day extraction into one load (4647a552e0, d02b521484). It is shrunk to `uint16_t` entries in 7602320f4c, and d29642dc9d fixes a fencepost. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-011): 7e89a04ca3 2008-06-15 Adds VCT, an RFC2616 ctype: `vct_typtab[256]` with `static inline vct_is()`; 322b18c64d 2008-06-21 and 60b50ef2cc 2008-06-21 "Avoid <ctype.h> entirely"; f81f87ce3e 2010-01-07 adds XML character classes. • 877851aad2 2008-12-03 Crit-bit tree finds the critical bit between keys with one XOR and a 256-entry leading-equal-bits table. • 3f6a251704 2021-08-31 `VNUM_uint()`/`VNUM_hex()` with `hex_table[]` replace `sscanf` in HTTP parsing. • bbf53b8839 2019-07-03 base64 decoding loses its nested loop: one table lookup per input char, and quad assembly driven by a counter. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-131): 33feb55c47 2006-01-20 XOR plus `number_of_ones[256]` for Hamming distance and bit counts in tsearch2, intarray, ltree and pg_trgm: "about a 20X speedup in building a large tsearch2 index". • ab5b4e2f9e 2009-07-21 `AllocSetFreeIndex` uses `LogTable256[256]` instead of a shift loop, which had been "a significant cost in palloc and pfree". • 1fd687a035 2020-02-01 `DIGIT_TABLE` of digit pairs plus `decimalLength32/64()` (log2 times 1233/4096 and one compare against powers of ten) so output is written right-to-left with no reversal. 02ddd49932 2019-02-13 switches float output to the Ryu shortest-round-trip algorithm because rounded decimal output had become "a noticable bottleneck" for COPY and large result sets. • e24d77080b 2025-01-15 a 512-byte `hextbl` with `memcpy(dst,&hextbl[2*b],2)` cuts hex-encode time by a third.

### duckdb-101 — Use the full machine word: bit-parallel logic over 64 elements at a time
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Pack booleans (validity, set membership, flags) into 64-bit words and operate on whole words with AND/OR/shift/popcount. Do bulk set/clear/copy/combine per word, and fall back to bits only at ragged edges.
Why it is faster: One ALU instruction handles 64 elements, so a bit-at-a-time loop does 64x the work. Word operations have no data-dependent branches, and an all-zero or all-one word can short-circuit a whole block. A byte array or a tree map replaced by a bitset also shrinks memory 8x or more. The cost is shift-and-mask bookkeeping for unaligned offsets and tail words.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Permission and role sets as bitmasks (check with AND). Feature-flag sets per user. Bitset dedup of small integer IDs. • Embedded: Packed flag arrays and GPIO-style masks. Free-slot bitmaps with count-trailing-zeros to find a free block. • Native / systems: Bloom filters, roaring-bitmap containers and allocator free lists, all word-at-a-time with popcount and tzcnt. • Data / databases: Arrow-style null bitmaps: combine validity of inputs by AND per word, and skip work for words that are all valid.
Watch out: Tail and unaligned handling is where bugs live, so test odd offsets and lengths. Very sparse sets are better as position lists or run lengths (roaring containers choose among these).
Seen in DuckDB: 4f5b24b897 2022-04-18 `ValidityMask::SetAllInvalid/SetAllValid` fill whole entries, then fix the last one with `MAX_ENTRY << last_entry_bits`, replacing a per-bit loop. • 8695b9645c 2023-06-03 `ValidityMask::SliceInPlace` aligned path combines `src >> tail | next << head` per word: a window benchmark went from 21.2s to 19.7s. • 493905d55b 2023-11-07 The Jaccard function moves from `std::map<char,...>` to a 256-entry array (3b5f2bf48e) and then to a bitset, so intersection and union are `(a & b).count()` (91b7c3e14e). • 7a61069345 2026-08-17 The unified `ScalarExecutor` combines validity a machine word at a time. 718716e85f selects NULLs by validity word. fce1cd28ce computes AND/OR three-valued logic for 64 rows with a few bitwise ops, and writes a result mask only for blocks that contain a NULL.

### duckdb-102 — Specialize copies on fixed widths and coalesce adjacent copies
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: A `memcpy`/`memcmp` with a runtime length is a library call that branches on size. Dispatch a loop-invariant length to compile-time-sized copies, and merge copies of data that already sits back to back into one call.
Why it is faster: With a constant size the compiler emits a few wide loads and stores inline, with no call, no size dispatch and no alignment prologue. For tiny copies (8-64 bytes) call overhead dominates, especially with some platform libcs (macOS). Coalescing turns N small calls into one large, bandwidth-efficient one. The cost is a `switch` over sizes and some code growth. Unusual sizes still fall back to the generic call.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In WASM or JS hot loops over typed arrays, specialize on the small set of record strides you see. Use `TypedArray.set` once for a contiguous range instead of per-element copies. • Embedded: Copy fixed-size packet headers by struct assignment, which inlines, instead of `memcpy` with a variable length. • Native / systems: Serialization libraries specialized for fixed-size structs. `writev` or a single copy for adjacent slices. • Data / databases: Row-format engines copying fixed-width rows. Sorters comparing normalized keys of known width.
Watch out: Every specialization adds code (DuckDB later had to remove `static` from `FastMem*` helpers because each translation unit got its own copy). For large copies, the libc `memcpy` is already optimal.
Seen in DuckDB: 042c976800 2021-03-10 When update info covers every tuple of a vector, one bulk memcpy of data and validity replaces a per-row scatter. • c91e3b5430 2021-11-18 Adds `fast_mem.hpp` with `FastMemcpy/FastMemcmp`, a switch over sizes that calls `MemcpyFixed<SIZE>`. It is used in radix sort, insertion sort, tie computation and the merge sorter, and 616d6647bf extends it to `MergeRows/MergeData` (PRs a73d33c140, b10ef8b111). • 29cf5c95bb 2024-06-26 FastMemcpy for string heap copies in the tuple data scatter, which is notably faster on macOS. • a0aec37cfa 2026-06-26 `ColumnDataCollection` string append tracks `expected_next_ptr` with branch-free bit operations, and when the source strings are contiguous it does a single `memcpy`. • ae6e64dc03 2026-11-17 `TupleDataCollection::CopyRows` switches on row width to `CopyRowBytes<8..64>`. With extra partitions, TPC-H Q16 SF704 got about 15% faster on 192 cores.

### duckdb-103 — Accumulate in registers and write back once per run
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When many rows update the same target (an aggregate state, a counter), group the rows per target first. Then load the state once, accumulate locally in a register, possibly in a narrower type that provably cannot overflow, and store once.
Why it is faster: Scattered read-modify-write to state pointers creates a store-to-load dependency through memory per row and defeats vectorization. A run of rows hitting one state becomes a tight register loop, and a COUNT becomes a run length. A 64-bit local sum is much cheaper than a 128-bit one when a per-run bound proves no overflow. The grouping step costs something, so it pays only when a batch has few distinct targets (DuckDB caps it at 256 groups).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Metrics libraries: batch increments per label set, then do one atomic add per label instead of one per event. • Embedded: Histogram building with local counters per bin in registers or on the stack, flushed once per block. • Native / systems: GPU reduce-by-key after sorting keys within a block. SIMD horizontal sums per run. • Data / databases: GROUP BY on low-cardinality keys: sort or cluster the batch by group, then run ungrouped-style tight loops per group.
Watch out: With many distinct groups per batch the clustering cost is wasted, so sample first and fall back. Narrow accumulators need a proof of no overflow for the run length times the maximum value.
Seen in DuckDB: 997c242768 2026-05-13 `ClusteredAggr`: with 256 or fewer groups in a chunk (found from a hot-key sample), row positions are reordered into `GroupRun{state, sel, count}` runs and updated with new `UnarySelectedUpdateLoop`/`NullaryClustUpdate` kernels. TPC-H Q1 SF10 single-threaded went from 1.15s to 0.68s. • 847e85c91e 2026-05-27 For hugeint sums, a run is summed in int64 when a "vector-overflow-safe" check passes, then added once: Parquet Q1 went from 1.75s to 1.55s. • 4733afd581 2026-06-19 The `list` aggregate appends a batch per run (`ListClusterUpdate`): about 40% faster.

### duckdb-104 — Fast number formatting and wide-word arbitrary-precision arithmetic
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB · Also: simdjson
Principle: Convert numbers to text by computing the exact length first and writing two digits per step from a table into the final buffer. Use asymptotically better conversion algorithms for big numbers, and run bignum kernels on 64-bit limbs instead of bytes.
Why it is faster: Digit generation is division-bound, and a two-digit table halves the divisions. Knowing the length upfront allows one exact allocation with no temporary string. Repeated division by 10 on a bignum is O(n²), and divide-and-conquer methods are much faster. Byte-wise bignum loops use 1/8 of the ALU width. Trade-offs: tables add cache footprint, and wider limbs need carry handling (128-bit intermediates).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JSON and log serializers formatting integers with digit-pair tables (as RapidJSON does). BigInt polyfills using large limbs. • Embedded: Number rendering on displays: precompute the length, write digit pairs, and avoid `printf`. • Native / systems: Crypto and decimal libraries on 64-bit limbs with `__uint128` products. • Data / databases: CSV/JSON export of numeric columns writing straight into the output buffer at its final size.
Watch out: Float formatting must round-trip correctly (use a proven library such as fmt or Ryu instead of rolling your own). Changing limb width must not change the on-disk format.
Seen in DuckDB: 0b3a19fe6e 2020-03-01 INT to VARCHAR: `UnsignedLength` computes the length, then `FormatUnsigned` writes backwards two digits at a time from a "00".."99" table (credited to Alexandrescu) straight into `vector.EmptyString(length)`. PR 35d26651ba "Fast To String Casts" uses the fmt library for floats (4821d8692b). • dbad217e40 2020-08-06 128-bit hugeint length by binary split over `PowersOfTen`. • cb05b0d2f1 2025-01-13 VARINT to VARCHAR uses Knuth TAOCP 4.4 Method 1b (as CPython does): 3.22s to 0.72s. • f177c34f79 2026-11-09 BIGNUM arithmetic on 64-bit limbs, keeping the storage format: `sum` 2.4x, add/subtract 1.5-1.7x, cast to HUGEINT 4.3x, to VARCHAR 4-9x, multiplication 3.7-4.4x. • Also seen in simdjson (simdjson-093): 60c139a844 2020-09-23 The DOM serializer replaces `ostream` output with an append-only buffer and a custom `fast_itoa`. • c806e955c4 2025-07-14 The string builder computes `digit_count` with `fast_digit_count_32/64`, then writes back-to-front with 2-byte `memcpy`s from a 200-byte pair table. • 781a7d6c89 2025-10-27 Branchless sign: `buffer[position] = '-'; position += negative ? 1 : 0;`. • f902769b35 2026-05-04 A forward magnitude cascade instead of digit count plus backward loop, and de-recursed jeaiii helpers: signed ints 2272 to 2685 MB/s (+18%). • af2a43610e 2026-02-16 Four digits per loop iteration via /10000 (CITM +6%). • 683e686e47 2026-10-21 The DOM switches from `fast_itoa` (one division per digit) to the builder's jeaiii writer: 200k 32-bit ints minify 4261 to 1572 us, citm 414 to 344 us; the tables are no longer duplicated per implementation. • 82ca01f0b8 2026-11-20 A four-digit group table with a branch-free leading group; a separate 200-byte path for values below 100 because the branch-free path had made small-value arrays 15-33% slower. The table is a class-template static member, one copy per program.

### duckdb-105 — Detect the easy input class once, then take a cheaper byte-level path (ASCII)
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Unicode work (decoding, validation, case mapping, grapheme counting) is unnecessary when text is pure ASCII. Detect ASCII cheaply per column (from statistics), per block or per string, and switch to byte-indexed kernels.
Why it is faster: For ASCII, byte count equals code point count, case conversion is a 256-entry table, and substring is pointer arithmetic. UTF-8 decoding and validation have data-dependent branches per character. The detection itself can be one 64-bit high-bit test per 8 bytes, or free from column statistics. Mixed data pays for the check and still takes the slow path.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Validate or slice protocol fields known to be ASCII (headers, IDs) as bytes. Check "all ASCII" before running Unicode normalization in a search tokenizer. • Embedded: Command parsers that accept ASCII only can skip UTF-8 handling entirely. • Native / systems: Text libraries with an ASCII flag per string (as JS engines keep one-byte strings). • Data / databases: Per-column or per-block ASCII flags in statistics, used to choose kernels at plan time.
Watch out: "One byte per character" is not ASCII. DuckDB removed a pandas shortcut that treated Python's 1-byte (Latin-1) strings as ASCII (e3740f5b40). Statistics must be maintained correctly on updates.
Seen in DuckDB: e042810e15 2020-10-20 String columns track an "ASCII only" statistic. • 3e8ecafc9b 2020-11-19 When statistics say there is no unicode, the planner swaps in `ascii_to_upper_map`-based case conversion, `InstrAsciiOperator` and `substring_ascii_only`. c95a68e332 adds `ILikeOperatorASCII`. • d40ef659c4 2026-09-23 `Length`, `GraphemeCount`, `UnicodeCaseConvert` and `SubstringUnicode` get ASCII paths: `length(url) > 40` 1.29-1.32x, `substr` group-by 1.15x. • be2316e7e4 2026-11-16 CSV values inside blocks that `SwarBlock::HasHighBits` proves ASCII skip `Utf8Proc::IsValid`, which had been 7.3% of a single-threaded lineitem load.

### duckdb-106 — Unroll stubborn search loops by hand, and make the unrolled body cover whole blocks
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Some loops (finding the next set bit across words, delta decoding) compile poorly. A fixed-size inner unroll with an early exit, unchecked accessors inside the verified range, and counter updates after the loop can double speed. Align the unrolled body so it handles full blocks, not an off-by-one tail.
Why it is faster: Unrolling reduces loop overhead and exposes independent work to the out-of-order core. Hoisting checked accessors and bulk inserts (`insert(end, p, p + n)` instead of repeated `push_back`) removes per-element bounds and capacity checks. If the unrolled body starts at index 1 with element 0 handled separately, every block runs the slow tail path. Unrolling grows code and helps only when the compiler did not already do it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JS bit scans over `Uint32Array` with `Math.clz32`, unrolled over several words. • Embedded: Hand-unrolled FIR filters and checksums. Check that the unrolled block size divides the frame size. • Native / systems: Allocator free-bit search and bitmap index scans. • Data / databases: Decoders for delta/FOR blocks of 32 or 64 values processed as a fixed unit.
Watch out: Measure on the target compiler, since manual unrolling can block the compiler's own vectorization. Unchecked accessors are safe only inside a range that was validated once.
Seen in DuckDB: 7fd36c3ac3 2026-01-29 IEJoin `NextValid` unrolled by 8 ("improves performance by almost 2x" per the code comment), with `GetValidityEntryUnsafe`, `SetValidUnsafe`, `unsafe_vector` and bulk `insert`. • bc023aea45 2026-03-28 `TemplatedMergeJoinComplexBlocks` optimized: `range_join_big_result` about 2x faster. • d025f5fc83 2026-09-15 Bitpacking `DeltaDecode` loop starts at 0 so the unrolled body covers the full block: `bitpacking_read_dfor` about 0.0226s to 0.0206s.

### duckdb-107 — Let the compiler and linker work for you: ISA baseline, LTO, release flags, binary size
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Treat build configuration as a performance lever. Raise the target ISA, enable link-time optimization, compile out debug checks, give the optimizer whole-program visibility, and keep code size under control so specializations do not bloat the binary.
Why it is faster: A generic x86-64 build cannot use AVX2, BMI or FMA, so auto-vectorized loops stay at SSE2 width. LTO and amalgamation allow cross-unit inlining. Assertions in release builds cost branches everywhere. Binary size matters too: duplicated `static inline` copies, unused template instantiations and registries that defeat dead-code elimination inflate downloads and instruction-cache pressure. Trade-offs: a higher ISA baseline drops old CPUs (ship multiple builds), and LTO multiplies build time.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Strip dev-only checks with `NODE_ENV=production`. Avoid barrel files and global registries that defeat tree-shaking. Ship per-arch native modules as optional dependencies. • Embedded: `-Os` with generic paths when flash is tight. Audit `static inline` in headers. Use `--gc-sections`. • Native / systems: Rust `-C target-cpu` and `lto = "thin"`. glibc hwcaps or multiple builds per ISA level. PGO for hot services. SQLite-style amalgamation for C libraries. • Data / databases: Offer a size-optimized build (WASM, mobile) that trades specialized kernels for a generic path.
Watch out: A raised ISA baseline crashes with an illegal-instruction error on older CPUs, so detect at runtime or ship a fallback. LTO and unity builds raise build time and memory. Size flags can cost several percent of speed, so make them opt-in.

### duckdb-108 — Process data a cache-sized batch at a time, and keep batches full
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Run every operator over a fixed-size batch (around 1-2K values per column) instead of one item at a time. Per-call overheads (virtual dispatch, type switches, state setup) are paid once per batch, and inner loops are plain array loops. Vectorize the parts that were still per-row, and pack small outputs together so batches stay full.
Why it is faster: Interpretation overhead per value can exceed the useful work, and batching divides it by the batch size. Tight loops over arrays let the compiler unroll and auto-vectorize, and the batch still fits in L1/L2 so intermediates never go to DRAM. Larger batches amortize more but spill out of cache. DuckDB went from 1024 to 2048 once its operators were lean enough. A batch carrying only a few rows pays full overhead for little work, so expanding operators should fill batches across many inputs.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Process log lines, events or JSON records in batches of about 1K in Node/Go pipelines. Batch RPC handlers and DB inserts. Use struct-of-arrays typed arrays instead of arrays of objects. • Embedded: Process audio and sensor DSP in fixed blocks sized to the cache or scratchpad, never sample by sample through a callback. • Native / systems: Game ECS systems iterating component arrays. Tile SIMD loops to fill L1. Batch syscalls (io_uring, `recvmmsg`). • Data / databases: Vector-at-a-time interpreters, with batch size tuned to cache. Compact sparse outputs before passing them on.
Watch out: Batches that are too large overflow L1/L2 and lose the benefit. Changing the batch size can touch storage formats (DuckDB bumped its storage version). Latency-sensitive paths may prefer small batches.

### duckdb-109 — Specialize the inner loop per case at compile time
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Turn per-element runtime questions (is the input constant? is there a selection vector? can there be NULLs? do bounds need checking? what is the key width?) into compile-time template or generic parameters. Pick the matching loop once per batch, so each loop body contains only the work its case needs.
Why it is faster: A generic loop re-tests invariant conditions per element, and those tests block vectorization. A specialized loop is branch-free and often auto-vectorizes. Dispatch costs one switch per batch. The cost is code size: every boolean parameter doubles the number of instantiations. DuckDB added a small-binary mode, complement folding and a unified executor to bound that growth.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In JIT languages, keep separate monomorphic functions for the dense and sparse (or nullable) cases and choose once per batch. • Embedded: Generate separate loops for "with alpha" and "without alpha" (or mono and stereo) instead of testing per pixel or sample. • Native / systems: Rust generics over `const HAS_NULLS: bool`. C++ `if constexpr`. Image codecs specialized by channel count. • Data / databases: Kernel matrices over (constant or flat) × (nulls or no nulls) × (selection or not) × (type), dispatched per batch.
Watch out: Combinatorial explosion of instantiations (binary size, compile time, instruction-cache pressure). Specialize the common cases and keep a generic fallback. The fallback must be correct, so test both paths.

### duckdb-110 — Check once per batch, then run a check-free loop (no-NULL and dense fast paths)
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Decide per batch, or per plan from statistics, whether the slow cases can occur at all: any NULLs, a selection, an incomplete coverage. When they cannot, take a loop with no per-element checks, and skip allocating side structures such as validity masks.
Why it is faster: Most real batches have no NULLs, and many intermediates are dense. An "all valid" test (a null pointer, or one word compare per 64 rows) is far cheaper than a validity test per row, and it unlocks a vectorizable loop. Not allocating a mask also saves memory traffic. When statistics prove "no NULLs", even the batch check disappears. The fallback path remains for mixed data.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In JSON serializers, skip optional-field checks when the schema marks fields required. In JS, keep a "has holes" flag per typed buffer and choose a tight loop when it is false. • Embedded: Per-frame "all samples valid" flags from sensor drivers let filters skip per-sample checks. • Native / systems: Arrow kernels branch on `null_count == 0`. Protobuf presence bits checked per message, not per field access. • Data / databases: Declare NOT NULL so planners pick null-free kernels. Represent "all valid" as no mask at all.
Watch out: A wrong statistic (claiming no NULLs when there are some) produces wrong results, not just slow ones. Each fast path is another branch to test.
Seen in DuckDB: b25b9405a4 2020-12-16 Skips returning a null mask to pandas when there are no nulls. • 60269295df 2021-02-18 "Add fast loops back in when there are no null values": unary, binary and aggregate executors branch on `mask.AllValid()`. 6e220ef7a3 "Nullmask Rework" makes a null pointer mean "all valid". c36974616e allocates the result mask only when an input word is not all-valid. • 096249a612 2024-06-20 `ValidityMask::CopySel` returns early when neither side has NULLs, uses `SliceInPlace` when aligned and flat, and uses `memset 0xFF` when the source has no mask. • d52132a24f 2025-02-20 Parquet reader no-NULL fast paths: SF100 lineitem V1 5.5s to 4.4s, V2 4.2s to 3.6s. Writer no-NULL paths in 8605f802de and 219bafadb7. • 6729faed7c 2025-03-17 A non-null constant vector gets no validity mask, so `AllValid()` fast paths trigger (about 1% on TPC-H). • 1b6c85a228 2025-07-13 "No NULLs" statistics flow into the row-format `TupleDataLayout` used by aggregation: up to 10% faster.

### duckdb-111 — Let batches carry cheap encodings, read through one zero-copy accessor
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Allow a batch to be flat, constant (one value), a sequence (start plus step) or a dictionary (indices into another vector). Kernels read every encoding through one (data pointer, selection index, validity) view instead of flattening into a plain array first.
Why it is faster: Flattening a constant or dictionary vector copies data proportional to the batch size, and one accessor avoids writing a separate kernel per encoding combination. One indirection per element (`data[sel[i]]`) is cheaper than materializing. Encodings also let later operators detect "constant" or "dictionary" and do less work (next two entries). The indirection blocks some SIMD, so flat inputs often keep a dedicated fast loop.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Keep a filtered view (base array plus index list) in a JS data grid instead of copying rows. Represent `range(0, N)` lazily. • Embedded: Constant-fill and ramp signals represented as (value) or (start, step) instead of buffers. • Native / systems: Strided views (NumPy and PyTorch strides) let kernels run on slices and transposes without copies. • Data / databases: Arrow dictionary and run-end arrays, read through a single accessor in compute kernels.
Watch out: Each encoding must be handled correctly everywhere, or flattened at boundaries. Indirection through a selection vector loses contiguous SIMD loads, so keep a flat fast path.

### duckdb-112 — Compute once for constant inputs
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: If every input to a deterministic operation is constant across the batch, evaluate it once and emit a constant result. If every row maps to the same target (an aggregate state, a group), make one batched call instead of N.
Why it is faster: Work drops from O(n) to O(1) per batch, and the result stays constant, so downstream operators keep benefiting. Constant inputs are common: literals, broadcast parameters, and partition columns from Hive paths. Detection costs one encoding-tag check per batch. Volatile functions (random, now) must be excluded.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: A batch where all items share a tenant: take one lock, do one config lookup, make one counter update. Memoize pure functions when arguments repeat. • Embedded: Skip filtering a DC (constant) block. Output the precomputed response. • Native / systems: NumPy broadcasting fast paths for scalar operands. Spreadsheet engines evaluating a formula once per constant range. • Data / databases: `SUM(constant)` becomes value × count. Partition-column expressions evaluated once per file.
Watch out: Only for deterministic, side-effect-free functions. Error semantics must match: a function that would throw on some row must still throw.

### duckdb-113 — Keep hot paths on raw typed arrays, not boxed dynamic values
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: A generic tagged value object (type tag + union + owned string) per element costs allocation, type dispatch and conversion. Use templated loops over raw typed buffers, and build nested and foreign data straight into its final columnar buffers.
Why it is faster: Boxing allocates (strings, nested children), adds a type switch per access, and prevents vectorization. Typed raw access is a load. Converting foreign objects (Python, nested lists) through an intermediate value tree doubles the work and the allocations. The cost is more templated code paths per type.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Use typed arrays instead of arrays of objects or number boxes in JS hot paths. Avoid `Object[]` and boxed `Integer` in Java inner loops. • Embedded: Avoid variant/any message types in the sample path. Parse straight into fixed structs. • Native / systems: Prefer `Vec<f64>` to `Vec<Box<dyn Any>>`. Write JSON-to-columnar ingestion directly into Arrow builders. • Data / databases: Keep generic Value types for planning and APIs, never for per-row execution.
Watch out: Typed paths multiply code per type (see the binary-size entry). Keep one generic slow path for rare types instead of templating everything.

### duckdb-114 — Split mixed per-row work into tight single-purpose passes
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Instead of one loop that hashes, branches on state kind, checks NULLs and updates structures per row, first classify rows into selection vectors or compute an intermediate array. Then run one simple, branch-free loop per category or step, field by field.
Why it is faster: Each pass does one thing over contiguous data, so it vectorizes and pipelines well. Splitting removes per-row branches on category, and processing field by field (structure of arrays) turns strided accesses into sequential ones. Callbacks are invoked once per batch, not per row. The cost is extra passes over small, L1-resident intermediate arrays, which is usually cheap.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Batch Bloom-filter or cache inserts: compute all hashes first, then all bucket updates. • Embedded: Separate "decode headers" and "process payload" passes over a packet buffer so each loop fits the small instruction cache. • Native / systems: ECS systems over component arrays. Partition work by type before processing in an interpreter. • Data / databases: Hash once per batch into an array, then probe. Split NULL handling from value handling in combine and finalize code.
Watch out: Intermediate arrays must stay small (one batch) or the extra passes go to DRAM. Very cheap per-row work may not justify extra passes.
Seen in DuckDB: 5bf7da0881 2022-05-12 HyperLogLog insertion split into passes: hash all values (1834595222, "vectorized hash computation"), then compute register index and leading zeros, then update registers. • 344094eb03 2026-02-11 `ExportAggregateFinalize` iterates by field, not by row (`StoreFieldTyped`). • a25cf61616 2026-02-16 `AggregateStateCombine` partitions rows by NULL status (both-null, copy-left, copy-right, both-valid) into selection vectors, runs `LoadFieldForSelectedRowsOp`/`StoreFieldForSelectedRowsOp` loops per category, and makes one batched combine call.

### duckdb-115 — Use SIMD and SWAR (8 bytes per register) for byte scanning, and lean on vectorized libraries
Lens: batch-processing · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Classify or search 8 (SWAR) or 16-64 (SIMD) bytes per instruction with bit tricks or vector compares, and drop to per-byte logic only for flagged positions. Reuse platform primitives that are already vectorized (`memchr`, NEON codecs) instead of hand-written byte loops.
Why it is faster: Text and binary scanning (delimiters, quotes, non-ASCII, escape bytes) is byte-bound, and one 64-bit operation tests 8 bytes. Branches then happen per candidate, not per byte. SWAR is portable (no intrinsics) and works on any 64-bit CPU. Libc `memchr` uses the widest SIMD available. On AArch64, NEON is always present, so it can be enabled unconditionally. Gains shrink when candidates are dense (most bytes are interesting).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JSON/CSV/HTTP parsers with simdjson-style structural bitmasks. Use `indexOf` or `Buffer.indexOf`, which are vectorized natively, instead of char loops. • Embedded: SWAR needs only 32- or 64-bit ALUs, which suits MCUs without SIMD (newline and NUL scanning in UART buffers). • Native / systems: `memchr`/`memmem` as the first filter in grep-like tools. Compile SIMD unconditionally for baseline-guaranteed ISAs (SSE2 on x86-64, NEON on arm64). • Data / databases: UTF-8 validation, delimiter search and escape detection in loaders, and sort-key encoders.
Watch out: Reading 8 or 64 bytes at a time can run past buffer ends, so pad buffers or handle tails separately. Endianness matters for SWAR bit positions. Dense-candidate inputs lose the advantage.

### duckdb-116 — Overlap cache misses: touch or prefetch a batch of addresses before the dependent loop
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: DuckDB · Also: jemalloc
Principle: When a loop does scattered loads with branches or loop-carried dependencies (hash probes, bitmap and Bloom probes, packed-group decoding), first run a cheap, branchless pass with independent iterations over the same addresses, or issue software prefetches a fixed distance ahead. The real loop then finds the lines in cache.
Why it is faster: A dependent loop lets only about one miss be in flight, because the next address depends on the branch outcome. Out-of-order CPUs can track 10-20+ outstanding misses when iterations are independent. A "useless" pass (counting occupied slots), or `prefetch(addr[i + d])`, turns serial 100ns stalls into overlapped ones. It pays only when the structure exceeds the cache and accesses are scattered. On small, cache-resident structures the extra pass or prefetch is pure overhead, so make it adaptive.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Batched key-value or cache lookups: compute all bucket addresses first, then resolve. In JS, gather-then-process loops over typed-array hash tables (independent loads in the first loop). • Embedded: On cores with a data cache and DMA, start fetching the next table block while processing the current one. • Native / systems: Batched B-tree search that descends all keys one level at a time. Graph traversal that prefetches the next vertices' adjacency lists. Packet classification tables. • Data / databases: Hash-join and aggregation probes, Bloom-filter probes and dictionary gathers with prefetch distance tuned per structure size.
Watch out: Prefetch distance is hardware-specific (too short does not hide latency, too long evicts lines before use). For cache-resident structures it is wasted work. Always benchmark on large and small inputs and gate it adaptively, as DuckDB does.
Seen in DuckDB: f27ec3aef0 2023-12-08 "Trying to vectorize cache misses": a separate `LinearProbe` pass touches all hash slots of a batch before key comparison (merged early 2024 in PR #9957). 33e685b6e9 keeps salt-mismatch probing in a tight inner loop. • 965963f881 2024-12-17 PR #15368 ("vectorize HT lookups", 32cda86440): a branchless count of occupied entries runs before the main lookup loop in `GroupedAggregateHashTable`. TPC-H SF100 `count(DISTINCT l_orderkey, l_linenumber)` went from 4.5s to 4.2s. • 527d0f819f 2026-10-26 Prefetch upcoming buckets in hash-join build/probe and grouped aggregation. • 988d97f315 2026-11-12 Adaptive prefetching for large, scattered bitmap probes in perfect-hash joins. 718716e85f prefetches Bloom-filter probes for large filters. • dd158c1e43 2026-11-16 New `common/prefetch.hpp`: bitpacking prefetches 8 groups ahead with fused frame reconstruction. TPC-H SF1 single-core: Q1 -13.7%, Q6 -18.8%, Q12 -7.2%. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-075): 181ba7fd4d 2021-01-27 adds `emap_edata_lookup_batch`; 9f9247a62e 2021-01-27 restructures it into one loop that does `rtree_leaf_elm_lookup` for every pointer and a second that reads the leaves: "many rtree_leaf_elm accesses are cache misses ... taking more of those misses in parallel".

### duckdb-117 — Plan reads ahead, then fetch them in a few large, coalesced requests
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Before reading, collect every byte range the next unit of work needs. Merge ranges that overlap or sit within a small gap, and fetch them in a few large requests instead of many tiny ones.
Why it is faster: Remote storage charges a fixed latency per request (time to first byte), so ten 4 KiB reads cost about ten round trips while one 40 KiB read costs one. Decoders that pull a few bytes at a time (Thrift footers, page headers) are the worst case. Reading a few extra gap bytes is cheap compared with another round trip. The best gap is about latency × bandwidth: the bytes you could have downloaded while waiting for one more request. Over-reading wastes bandwidth when the gaps are large or the extra data is never used.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Batch S3/GCS range requests for Parquet, PMTiles or Cloud-Optimized GeoTIFFs. A GraphQL DataLoader collects keys in a tick and issues one query. • Embedded: Read a flash page or SD-card sector once and parse many small fields from RAM, instead of one bus transaction per field. • Native / systems: Use `preadv` or io_uring with merged extents. Read ZIP central directories and MP4 `moov` atoms in one range. • Data / databases: Pick the merge gap from measured latency and bandwidth, not from a constant tuned on one machine.
Watch out: On local NVMe the break-even gap is small, so a gap tuned for S3 makes you read a lot of useless data. Measure per device or per endpoint. Readers with broken offsets can turn prefetch into wrong reads, so DuckDB disables it for Parquet files with bad page offsets (8f2e835356).

### duckdb-118 — Remove round trips: reuse connections, cache validators, list lazily
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Every request that does not carry payload you need (HEAD, re-validation, a full listing, a new TLS handshake) is pure latency. Cache or skip it, and make listings streams instead of up-front lists.
Why it is faster: Against object stores, the fixed per-request cost dominates. Keep-alive removes the TCP and TLS setup. A metadata cache removes repeated HEADs. Lazy globbing lets a `LIMIT` stop before the listing finishes, and listing overlaps with processing. The trade-off is staleness: a skipped validation can serve outdated data if the object changes during the cache lifetime.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Use a keep-alive HTTP agent in Node or Go. Use ETag caching with conditional requests only when needed. Paginate `ListObjects` and process each page as it arrives. • Native / systems: Use a lazy `readdir` iterator instead of collecting the whole tree. Do parallel `stat` calls for many-file listings. • Data / databases: Expand file globs only as far as filter pruning and LIMIT require.
Watch out: Skipping last-modified checks trades correctness for speed. Do it only where the files are effectively immutable, or keep an explicit version tag.
Seen in DuckDB: 5e8814e46e 2022-02-09 HTTP keep-alive is enabled, and the Parquet reader issues fewer HEAD requests. • a386815bf3 2022-12-08 `http_metadata_cache` (query-local or global, with expiry and invalidation in 46890acbd7), plus a parallel Parquet metadata scan over many files (25be5a1696). • 953959558c 2024-03-07 The object cache skips `GetLastModifiedTime` (a HEAD) for remote files. 10bd11d203 2024-01-08 uses a thread-local file handle for seekable JSON on S3. 0bbfe09937 2024-10-16 avoids materializing the file list when pruning readers. • 263ef59866 2026-01-21 Lazy `MultiFileList` globbing: `FROM glob('**') LIMIT 10` went from 0.206s to 0.052s. • 9c4e82ecae 2026-03-20 `CREATE VIEW IF NOT EXISTS` skips binding when the view already exists, which avoids network requests against remote catalogs.

### duckdb-119 — Cache remote bytes in fixed, aligned blocks
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Cache remote file data in fixed-size, aligned blocks inside the engine's memory manager, and hand out handles into the cache instead of copies.
Why it is faster: Caching arbitrary byte ranges needs overlap and merge logic and can fetch the same bytes twice. Aligned blocks make a hit a simple lookup and deduplicate concurrent requests for the same region. Handles into the cache avoid a copy. Putting these blocks in the same eviction queue as everything else bounds total memory. The trade-off is fetching some unneeded bytes when a range only partly covers a block.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Put a block cache in front of S3 reads (as s3fs and Alluxio do). Use the browser Cache API for WASM data files, keyed by block. • Native / systems: Use page-cache-style lookups keyed by (file, block number), and coalesce concurrent misses on the same key. • Data / databases: Account the cache in the same memory budget as query memory, so it gives way under pressure.
Watch out: If the block size is much larger than typical requests, every miss amplifies reads. If the cache is unbounded or outside the memory budget, it competes with queries and causes spilling.
Seen in DuckDB: 9e7fedd4c0 2025-04-16 External file cache: `CachingFileSystem::Read` returns a `BufferHandle` into the cached data. 40baf59def shares the eviction queue with database blocks, and e05f4ff81a adds a version tag to validate local files. • b8db3cde7d 2025-12-29 The Parquet metadata cache becomes a memory-capped LRU. a35d8b6d5b emits partition stats from cached metadata with no file-system calls. • c38024fd15 2026-03-26 Reads and caching become block-aligned in the external file cache. • 278d021a04 2026-01-20 The object cache is registered under the buffer pool, so it can be evicted under memory pressure.

### duckdb-120 — Make persistence cost proportional to what changed
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB · Also: TigerBeetle
Principle: On checkpoint, snapshot or save, rewrite only dirty units and point to the previous version of everything else. Skip the flush entirely when nothing changed.
Why it is faster: A full rewrite costs O(database size) for every small change. Reusing block pointers for unchanged segments, tables and metadata turns that into O(changes). Freed blocks go back on a free list, so the file does not grow. The cost is bookkeeping: you need a reliable dirty bit and must keep reachable old blocks alive.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Static-site builds that rebuild only changed pages. Deploys that skip unchanged assets by content hash. • Embedded: Game saves or config stores that write only changed chunks, to cut flash wear. • Native / systems: Incremental backups, copy-on-write B-trees, Git-style content-addressed trees. • Data / databases: Make maintenance triggers count real bytes, so optimistic writes do not look like huge WAL growth.
Watch out: Reused blocks must never be freed while an old version still references them. Incremental schemes also fragment storage over time, so they need occasional compaction.
Seen in DuckDB: 0d2d61be2a 2021-01-28 The WAL is not flushed when no changes were made. 32cc7be05f 2021-01-28 puts a dropped table's blocks on the free list. • bfe318052d 2021-02-03 Incremental checkpointing: `PersistentSegment::HasChanges()` compares block ids, and unchanged segments keep their pointers. • 3085694318 2022-10-14 An index serialized at the last checkpoint is referenced by its stored pointer instead of being written again. 23b94dd87d truncates inside the pending write buffer instead of issuing a file truncate. • df7f4fa0e5 2025-07-24 Reuses metadata of unchanged tables. 8755ee6e1c 2025-07-24 does the same per row group: a TPC-H SF100 single-row-insert checkpoint went from 0.5s to 0.11s. 0769417a29 2025-07-25 flushes only dirty metadata blocks: 0.11s → 0.04s, and open+insert+close 0.63s → 0.07s. a65619811d reuses metadata even when deletes are loaded, and 8f51218753 turns it on by default. • 3f4d7b12fc 2026-01-07 The WAL size estimate counts only block pointers for optimistically written row groups, which stops spurious checkpoints. 6f3da6f556 2026-03-03 keeps the old, wider statistics instead of loading cold column metadata to recompute them. • Also seen in TigerBeetle (tigerbeetle-116): f234461c22 2021-12-13 "Move manifest to SuperBlock trailer, which makes the superblock much smaller ... reducing the latency of checkpointing"; "we only write the bytes that we actually use". • 7553224abd 2022-03-08 The superblock copy field is "excluded from the checksum calculation to ensure that all copies have the same checksum". • bbbb1ffa57 2022-11-15 Removed the secondary superblock copyset, which was "deemed unnecessary". • 4d21e1591f 2023-04-14 "Incremental client table": fixes "Redundant writes, causing a latency spike at checkpoint" and "High RAM overhead per-client". One copy instead of four, updated in place, with no in-memory table. • eb7564a824 2023-12-13 Client sessions moved from the superblock to the grid ("No more trailers in the superblock"). • 8585c35c3f 2024-01-17 Checkpoint trailers encoded directly into grid blocks: "One less copy (of all trailer data) during every checkpoint", with blocks acquired lazily. Enabled by dfbb8abc2c (2024-01-17), which made the EWAH codec resumable across chunks.

### duckdb-121 — Write bulk data as it is produced; commit by linking, log pointers not payload
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: For large writes, persist finished blocks to their final location right away. Commit then publishes references to those blocks, and the log records pointers rather than copies of the data.
Why it is faster: Buffering everything until commit doubles memory and serializes all I/O at the end. Writing the data again into a log doubles the bytes written. Writing optimistically overlaps I/O with production, keeps memory bounded, and makes commit O(blocks) instead of O(rows). Rollback has to free the orphaned blocks.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Use S3 multipart upload during generation, then complete the upload. In event sourcing, log a reference to blob storage, not the blob. • Native / systems: Use LSM memtable flushes during long transactions, and write staging files and then rename them atomically. • Data / databases: In ETL, write partition files as they fill and commit a manifest at the end.
Watch out: Aborts leave garbage blocks that must be reclaimed. Small transactions gain nothing and pay the block-allocation overhead, so gate this on size.
Seen in DuckDB: 91ed0afb2b 2022-10-15 Large transaction-local appends compress and write each full row group to disk immediately. Commit links the blocks in. 23ca324e83 moves whole row groups into the table, and dda01d7468 flushes near row-group size. 84b2449faf and b057d34ec8 extend this to tables with indexes. • a4e5cc9130 2023-05-03 Order-preserving inserts flush full row groups to disk optimistically. • d8a69cc656 2024-08-11 The WAL logs pointers to optimistically written row groups instead of their rows. • 1a9bad7361 2026-08-18 Optimistically written blocks are flushed and fsynced before the WAL lock is taken.

### duckdb-122 — Group commit: one fsync for many committers, with I/O outside the lock
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Do transaction-private I/O before taking the commit lock, append only a small marker under the lock, and let concurrent committers share one durable sync.
Why it is faster: If each commit fsyncs serially under a lock, throughput is capped at 1/fsync latency. If several commits share an fsync, the cost is spread across all of them, and the lock is held only for the marker append. Visibility must wait until the shared sync covers the commit.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: An append-only audit log service that batches fsyncs across requests arriving within a few milliseconds. • Native / systems: Group commit as in Postgres and MySQL. Kafka producer batching. • Embedded: Batch flash commits of several small records into one page program.
Watch out: A commit must never be acknowledged before its covering sync completes. Under low concurrency, group commit only adds latency unless no one waits for followers.
Seen in DuckDB: 5a48da435f 2024-06-20 The WAL write moves out of the global commit lock. 539cbadac5 keeps the WAL size in an atomic, and 55d5d42880 flushes only after a successful commit. • cb82ce96a3 2024-05-03 Checkpoints can run during reads without blocking new connections. • 01305010f8 2026-09-16 WAL group commit: `FlushMarker` runs under the lock, `SyncUpTo(offset)` runs after releasing it, and visibility is capped below the first commit that is not yet durable. • ed7dd79d23 2026-02-13 A commit either skips the WAL write and checkpoints directly, or allows concurrent commits. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-193): 57e0847180 2001-02-18 The default `commit_delay` becomes 0. 9c9936587c applies the delay only when at least `CommitSiblings` backends are active. • 4e9b159484 2002-10-07 `XLogFlush`, after taking WALWriteLock, sets its flush target to the current insert point, which "improves throughput when transactions are small enough that several can be committed per WAL write". • 9b38d46d9f 2012-01-30 `LWLockWaitUntilFree` (later `LWLockAcquireOrWait`, 1a01560cbb): a busy flusher's waiters wait for the lock to be released, re-read the flush pointer and retry only if their WAL is still unflushed. • f11e8be3e8 2012-07-02 "Make commit_delay much smarter": only the first backend ready to flush sleeps, and the others wait for that leader's flush. • 7975c5e0a9 2016-02-15 The WAL writer flushes only after `wal_writer_delay` or once `wal_writer_flush_after` bytes have accumulated. Flushing too often had cost up to about 4x. db76b1efbb lets hint bits be set once the page LSN covers the commit, removing the reason frequent flushes were needed.

### duckdb-123 — Pay for durability only where data must survive
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Logging, checksums and fsync protect data across crashes and restarts. Scratch data, temporary objects and outputs you can regenerate should not pay for them.
Why it is faster: A WAL write doubles the bytes written. Checksums cost CPU on every read and write. fsync and O_SYNC block until the device confirms. Spill files and temp tables live within one process lifetime, so these protections buy nothing there.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Use UNLOGGED tables for caches and staging. Skip fsync for rebuildable cache directories. • Native / systems: Keep "direct" (bypass page cache) separate from "sync" (wait for media). They are separate flags for a reason. • Data / databases: Shuffle and spill files rely on job-level retry, not per-block integrity.
Watch out: Be honest about what "temporary" means. Export files that users treat as final need an explicit sync option, or they can silently lose data on power failure.

### duckdb-124 — Turn random point accesses into computed seeks or sorted batches
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Do not reach item k by scanning items 0..k-1, and do not fetch rows one at a time in random order. Compute offsets from metadata, carry needed values along with the scan that already found them, and sort or batch the rest by location.
Why it is faster: Each point fetch pays block lookup, pin/unpin and often locking. Sorted or co-located batches amortize that over many rows and turn random I/O into sequential I/O. Computing an offset is O(1), where scanning is O(k) with buffer-pool traffic.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Use `DELETE … RETURNING` instead of SELECT-then-DELETE. Sort IN-list lookups by primary key or page. Use cursor pagination instead of OFFSET scans. • Native / systems: Elevator-sort random disk writes. Keep an offset index in log files so you can seek to record k. • Data / databases: Sort keys before bulk B-tree inserts or batched updates.
Watch out: Sorting has a memory and latency cost. Bound the staging buffer, and skip the sort when input is already ordered (DuckDB passes sorted input straight through).
Seen in DuckDB: 740e8e8dd6 2021-08-03 External sort merge does not unpin and re-pin when the next read is in the same block. • c3e3615624 2025-11-14 `TupleDataCollection::Seek()` computes a chunk's position from segment counts instead of scanning earlier chunks. 7292ea53c1 replays WAL deletes in batches instead of row by row. • 175069fd8c 2026-01-14 DELETE RETURNING takes its columns from the scan instead of re-fetching by rowid: 2.2–6.4× faster. c87733c87b scans indexed columns up front for DELETE. 8a3a51774d 2026-06-07 `RowGroupCollection::Fetch` fetches co-located rows in batches (0.85s → 0.79s). • 678f0ebab3 2026-11-17 UPDATE stages out-of-order row ids in a per-thread bounded sort (1–64 MB), so each storage call handles about 21 rows instead of 1. The baseline had 700–1000 s of kernel lock time on 96 threads.

### duckdb-125 — Open lazily: read metadata only when it is first touched
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: On open, read only top-level pointers. Deserialize per-table, per-column and per-segment metadata, delete info, indexes and logs on first use, and skip payloads that a pass does not need.
Why it is faster: Open time and memory then scale with what a query touches, not with database or file size. A query on 1 table of 1,000 never pays for the other 999. The cost is a later, unpredictable first-touch latency and more complex concurrency around load-on-demand.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Lazy-load routes and modules in SPAs. Use lazy relations in ORMs. • Native / systems: Use mmap'd formats that touch only the needed pages. Read a file footer without constructing decoders. • Embedded: Mount a filesystem index and parse directory entries on demand.
Watch out: Operations that need everything (full statistics recompute, export) lose the benefit and pay the indirection. Avoid triggering a full load from a cheap path by accident.

### duckdb-126 — Overlap I/O with compute through async queues with memory backpressure
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Hand writes and reads to an asynchronous queue so compute threads keep working. Bound the queued bytes, and finish finished outputs early so the device stays busy.
Why it is faster: Synchronous I/O idles the CPU while the device works, and CPU work idles the device. Overlapping them approaches max(CPU, I/O) instead of their sum. Backpressure keeps queued buffers from exhausting memory.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Async log appenders with bounded ring buffers. Keep Node's event loop free by offloading file I/O. • Native / systems: Use io_uring or overlapped I/O with a cap on in-flight bytes. • Embedded: Run DMA transfers while the CPU prepares the next buffer (double buffering).
Watch out: Without backpressure, async writes only move the out-of-memory point. Errors also surface late, so a failed async write must fail the whole operation.
Seen in DuckDB: 6ca23878c5 2026-06-16 `AsyncWriteQueue`, `ManagedAsyncWriteQueue` and `AsyncFileWriter`: Parquet row-group flushes no longer write under the writer lock. 15d3f23bdf 2026-06-16 adds async I/O for local Parquet reads. • e1cf6abc4d 2026-06-26 `TryFinalizePartitionWrite` finalizes partition files as early as possible to saturate I/O bandwidth.

### duckdb-127 — Flush in right-sized batches, sized by the expected volume per destination
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Buffer small appends and flush them together. When output fans out to many destinations, estimate how much each will receive and flush only once a destination's batch is worth a file or block.
Why it is faster: Each flush has a fixed cost (locks, transaction bookkeeping, file creation, footer writing). Flushing per chunk or per thread per partition produces many tiny files that are slow to write and slower to read later. Batching spreads the fixed cost. Estimating cardinality (for example with HyperLogLog) sets the threshold before the data has all been seen.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Batched inserts or `COPY` from clients. Browsers batching analytics events. Kafka→S3 sinks with size- or time-based rolling. • Data / databases: Repartition before `partitionBy` so each task writes a few large files. • Embedded: Buffer sensor samples and write full flash pages.
Watch out: Batching adds latency and memory and loses unflushed data on a crash. Bound both, and flush on a timer as well as on size.

### duckdb-128 — Lay out storage blocks by access pattern, and fill them
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Put data that is read together into the same blocks, pack small objects into shared blocks instead of giving each one a mostly empty block, and do not store what can be derived.
Why it is faster: Reading one column should not pull in pages full of other columns. Small segments in fixed-size blocks waste space and I/O. Fewer, fuller, single-purpose blocks mean fewer reads per query and a smaller file. Derivable metadata (start rows, header sizes) costs bytes on every read.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pack small objects into larger archives (the S3 small-object problem). Group assets loaded together into one bundle or texture atlas. • Native / systems: Use slab-style packing of small records into pages. Group chunks per series in time-series stores. • Data / databases: Size Parquet row groups so each column chunk is large enough to read efficiently.
Watch out: Buffering several row groups for column-wise writing costs memory. Shared blocks complicate freeing: a block can be reclaimed only when all of its occupants are dead.
Seen in DuckDB: 5898d10da8 2021-08-10 Multi-use blocks during checkpointing: `GetPartialBlock` picks a best-fit partially filled block with `lower_bound(segment_size)`. ### duckdb-129 — Choose the OS caching layer on purpose: direct, mmap or buffered
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: If the application manages its own cache, bypass the OS page cache (direct I/O with aligned buffers) to avoid double buffering. Where the OS cache is the right cache, use mmap. Large writes should skip the intermediate write buffer.
Why it is faster: Double caching wastes RAM and adds a copy. Direct I/O needs aligned buffers and removes the copy and the page-cache pressure. mmap avoids copies and syscalls on hits. Copying a large write through a small buffer is pure memcpy overhead.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Use O_DIRECT for databases with their own buffer pools, and LMDB-style mmap for read-mostly stores. Reserve virtual address space for growable mappings. • Web / backend: Use buffered streams for small writes and pass large payloads straight through (Java's `BufferedOutputStream` already does this). • Embedded: DMA-aligned buffers for SD or flash drivers.
Watch out: Direct I/O with misaligned buffers fails or falls back silently. mmap hides I/O errors as signals and makes memory accounting opaque. Offer the mode as a choice, not a universal default.
Seen in DuckDB: 13cce32f9d 2019-05-04 Adds a file-system layer with O_DIRECT, F_NOCACHE and FILE_FLAG_NO_BUFFERING, and `AllocateAlignedBuffer` for 4 KiB-aligned I/O. • 582920b4a5 2019-06-24 COPY TO writes through a 16 KiB `BufferedWriter` instead of per-value `ofstream <<`. • 2626cb2b1c 2024-03-21 `BufferedFileWriter` writes directly when the write exceeds the buffer, after flushing the partial buffer. • 17997b74e7 2026-06-02 `ATTACH … (IO_MODE 'MMAP'|'DIRECT_IO')`. mmap reserves `max(256GB, 2×file_size)` of address space so the mapping never has to move.

### duckdb-130 — Ingest in bulk: parse raw, convert by column, never seek backwards, bound detection
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Read input in large sequential buffers, split it into raw fields with a tight state machine, then convert whole columns in bulk. Keep auto-detection (sniffing) to a bounded sample, and do not re-read or seek on streams.
Why it is faster: Per-value conversion interleaves parsing and casting and pays dispatch per cell. A column-wide cast runs one tight loop. Seeks cost syscalls and break non-seekable inputs such as pipes and compressed streams. Sniffing every dialect over many chunks multiplies detection time for little extra accuracy.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Streaming NDJSON or CSV import endpoints that parse into typed column builders. Content-type sniffing limited to the first N KB. • Embedded: Parse serial input with a table-driven state machine over a ring buffer, without backtracking. • Data / databases: Infer schemas from a bounded sample and handle stdin and pipes without seeking.
Watch out: Bounded sniffing can guess wrong on files whose structure changes late. Report the detected dialect and allow overrides.

### duckdb-131 — Stream through bounded buffers instead of loading whole inputs or outputs
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Process files and outputs in fixed-size pieces (hash 8 KB at a time, write one column chunk at a time, flush when buffered bytes pass a per-thread budget), so peak memory is constant.
Why it is faster: Loading a whole file to hash it, or buffering a whole row group in plain memory to write it, costs a full copy and memory proportional to input size. That can cause out-of-memory failures or swapping. Streaming keeps the working set in cache and removes the big copy. Very small pieces add per-call overhead, so pick sizes in the KB to MB range.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Hash uploads with `crypto.createHash().update()` on a stream. Stream CSV or JSON exports row by row to the response. • Embedded: Verify firmware images block by block on an MCU with kilobytes of RAM. • Data / databases: Columnar writers that size row groups in bytes, not rows.
Watch out: Formats that need a full pass first (global dictionaries, footers with offsets) still need either two passes or spillable buffering.
