### duckdb-132 — Decouple logical work units from physical I/O block size
Lens: io · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Keep work partitions large for algorithmic efficiency, but read and write in small physical blocks so several threads can do I/O concurrently. Trim the last block to the bytes actually used.
Why it is faster: Huge blocks serialize I/O on one thread and waste memory on half-filled buffers. Smaller blocks (still at least one storage block) let many threads stream in parallel and reduce pinned memory. That memory can then go to larger in-memory sort budgets.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: External merge sort and LSM compaction page sizes. • Web / backend: Use part sizes for parallel multipart uploads that let many parts go out at once.
Watch out: Blocks that are too small push per-request overhead back up, especially on remote storage. This tuning is for local disks.
Seen in DuckDB: e50755fefa 2021-08-15 External sort separates `rows_per_merge_partition` from `block_capacity = min(…, BLOCK_SIZE/thinnest_row)` ("keep small for better concurrent I/O"). • 38bcbb8271 2021-07-06 Shrinks the final heap block to its byte offset with `ReAllocate`. 2e67ff2595 raises the radix-sort memory budget once blocks are smaller.

### duckdb-133 — Morsel-driven parallelism: thread-local state, merge once
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Split input into small independent work units handed to threads on demand. Each thread accumulates into private state with no locks, and a combine step merges each thread's state into the global result once.
Why it is faster: Locking or atomics per row serializes threads and bounces cache lines. With local state, the shared structure is touched once per thread instead of once per row, so synchronization cost goes from O(rows) to O(threads). Small units balance load automatically. The cost is extra memory for the per-thread copies and a combine phase, and it only works for operations that can be combined.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node `worker_threads` or a Go worker pool where each worker builds its own map and the main thread merges once. • Native / systems: Rayon `fold` + `reduce`. Linux percpu counters summed on read. • Data / databases: Map-side combiners. Build a search-index segment per worker and merge.
Watch out: Per-thread state multiplies memory by thread count, which matters for large hash tables. Non-combinable operations (order-dependent concatenation) must stay serial or be redesigned.
Seen in DuckDB: ebd9140890 2020-07-10 Basic parallelism: `PhysicalSimpleAggregate::Combine` merges local partial states through `aggregate.function.combine` under one lock. 69669e6aee adds parallel scans, and 9317730fca keeps aggregates that cannot be combined (STRING_AGG, LIST) serial. • de6073ed49 2020-07-23 Inter-pipeline parallelism: independent pipelines run concurrently. Windows, cross products and CTAS became sinks too (0482ebb377, 85235f75c1). • 012d99e0e8 2020-09-18 Parallel scan of a single Parquet file by row group. 4983b0089b 2020-12-14 makes parallel pandas scans possible by avoiding any operation that needs the GIL. • e05075834c 2021-02-19 Parallel Top-N keeps only the top N per thread and combines the small buffers. • a0fc1e9cd0 2022-09-24 Parallel CREATE INDEX: each thread sorts locally and bulk-builds a local ART, and Combine merges it into the global index. e4c51a8fb5 2023-01-12 and bfd297cba5 2023-08-07 refine this, skipping the pre-sort for VARCHAR and compound keys where it does not pay.

### duckdb-134 — Partition by key hash so every shared piece has exactly one owner
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Scatter data by a few bits of the key hash into partitions. Merging, building or sorting then happens per partition, one task per partition, so no two threads ever touch the same keys.
Why it is faster: Identical keys always land in the same partition, so the merge needs no locks and no atomic compare-and-swap on hot slots. Each partition's working set is smaller and more cache-friendly. Adding partitions only when the data is large avoids scatter overhead on small inputs. Skew is the limit: one huge key still lands on one thread.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Shard in-memory caches by key hash, one goroutine or actor per shard (Akka and Orleans partition actors by key). • Native / systems: A multi-threaded word count with per-thread maps partitioned by hash, then a per-partition merge. • Data / databases: Shuffle by key hash. Pick the partition count from observed sizes instead of a constant.
Watch out: Too many partitions make tiny, cache-unfriendly fragments and per-partition overhead. A single dominant key defeats the scheme, so detect skew instead of repartitioning forever.
Seen in DuckDB: 69e639af54 2020-07-16 The first parallel aggregate used a global lock on the hash table. f20a1cf94a 2020-10-23 replaced it with radix partitioning: up to 256 partitions from fixed hash bits, used only once groups exceed `radix_limit = 10000`, with one finalize task per partition. 9e8cfda02b forces a single table when not running in parallel. • 6ab86750ac 2021-04-01 Window functions bucket rows by `hash & partition_mask` (1024 bins) and evaluate each bin as a parallel task. • 231dc80cbb 2022-08-23 and 270b5842f0 2022-11-01 add parallel DISTINCT aggregates through radix-partitioned tables. 866efc2db6 2022-11-08 frees each partition's table as soon as it has been scanned. • c1cc21e5bb 2023-05-31 AsOf join partitions both sides by the equality keys and sorts the partitions in parallel. • c54d7f5968 2023-09-04 Adaptive radix partitioning shared across threads. It starts at `min(log2(threads),3)` bits and adds 2 bits when a partition exceeds 1.8 blocks. The global bit count is an atomic raised under a double-checked lock, and lagging threads repartition locally. 9ec59f4088 triggers partitioning on any single thread's group count. • 08fa4cfcbe 2026-03-18 Hash-join build assigns whole radix partitions to threads, so only one thread touches each chain. Building 100 distinct keys over 50M rows went from 5.5s to 4.5s.

### duckdb-135 — Hold locks only to claim or publish; do the slow work outside
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB · Also: Varnish
Principle: Split each operation into a tiny locked part (claim work, publish a result, insert a placeholder) and an unlocked part that holds the expensive allocation, encoding, I/O or construction.
Why it is faster: Lock hold time sets the ceiling on parallel throughput (Amdahl's law). Encoding or doing I/O under a writer lock serializes all threads behind the slowest step. Publishing a future or placeholder lets other threads proceed, or wait on the specific item instead of on the whole map. The risk is races: the unlocked part must work on data no one else can change.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Singleflight or promise caches: store the Promise, not the value, so concurrent requests share one in-flight computation. • Native / systems: Logging libraries that format outside the lock and only append under it. Deferred free and RCU. • Data / databases: Never hold a connection-pool or catalog lock across a query or a network call.
Watch out: A lock that is cheap to take can still be the bottleneck if it is taken per row. Measure wait time, not just hold time. Moving work out of a lock can expose ABA problems or stale reads, so re-validate after reacquiring.
Seen in DuckDB: ed6780b77b 2023-03-26 Moves hash-table `Finalize()` out of the global lock. 81c7b8cfb6 2022-12-22 makes parallel CSV and Parquet writers prepare column writer states before the lock, which is held "only for the final write to disk" (fc8615e5c0). • 839b1cc1cb 2023-10-28 Catalog dependency work runs before global locks are taken. 12782f1a52 2023-12-05 locks all clients only for FORCE CHECKPOINT. • 7732a69486 2024-06-05 `Pin` returns already-loaded buffers without taking the block lock. 4756244efa 2024-10-16 uses table-level checkpoint locks. 96990255e8 opens many JSON files outside the global lock, and 589a7e5800 removes a spinlock. • c677fa112c 2025-07-08 The DB instance cache inserts a future and releases the lock while the instance is created. dbbf3d34da 2025-04-15 runs transaction cleanup outside the transaction lock. 5333f604d5 2025-04-01 reworks Parquet file-rotation locking: 16s → 2.3s for a single output file. • 2a4651376c 2025-11-20 Builds `IEJoinUnion` outside the lock: 8.33s → 3.49s. • beb433b63b 2026-03-18 Parquet `TryInitializeScan` (locked) is split from `PrepareScan` (unlocked). 64f0eae50b 2026-04-09 releases the transaction lock during the fallback WAL write. b5c60cccb2 2026-05-22 stops `pragma_storage_info` from scanning metadata while holding the row-group lock. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-104): 9a788d267d 2006-06-26 `HSH_Lookup` pre-creates `w->nobjhead`/`w->nobj` per worker. They are consumed only if inserted, otherwise kept for next time. cc9c77afaa 2006-09-16 preallocates a srcaddr per worker thread "to speed up locked section". • b20267d1c8 2006-08-22 `hcl_lookup` uses a two-pass loop: miss, unlock, `calloc`, re-lock, search again, then insert or free. "inserts are quite rare and collisions even rarer." • 197f744d65 2010-11-29 adds a hash-slinger `prep()` method "to avoid a malloc in the locked path" in critbit, and preallocates the waiting list per worker. • ca3099c3b6 2012-03-14 adds `OC_F_NOTYET`: the busy objcore is inserted and the lock released before `VBO_GetBusyObj()` and the remaining setup. On a hit, `oc_getobj()` and `o->hits++` also move after the unlock. • 6393f26c38 2012-03-14 passes the digest into lookup "so we don't have to touch the (potential) new objhead, unless we intend to insert it".

### duckdb-136 — Skip locks the data model makes unnecessary
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB · Also: jemalloc, NATS
Principle: If data is immutable after a point (sealed, finalized, read-only), or a cheap atomic flag can show that nothing has changed, read it without the mutex.
Why it is faster: Even an uncontended mutex costs atomic read-modify-write operations and serializes cores on the lock's cache line. When many connections take a database-wide mutex on every statement just to read rarely changed configuration, that mutex becomes the throughput ceiling. An atomic check or an immutability guarantee removes it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Feature-flag checks through an atomic snapshot pointer (RCU-style config). Freeze objects shared across workers. • Native / systems: Use `pread` instead of seek+read under a lock. Use double-checked initialization. Read sealed log segments lock-free. • Data / databases: Mark segments immutable after a flush and serve reads without coordination.
Watch out: "It is never modified" must be enforced, not just assumed. Get memory ordering right, publishing with release and reading with acquire, or readers can see half-built state.
Seen in DuckDB: 53284ccfeb 2021-06-11 Disk block reads no longer take the buffer-manager lock. f727662cb7 2021-09-05 removes an unnecessary lock in ORDER BY. • 4e86594ba8 2023-03-21 Adds a read-only flag to finalized segment trees so reads skip locking. 4058b406a9 adds an atomic `next` pointer so sequential traversal needs no lock or tree search. • f442a9ad30 2024-03-11 `NumberOfThreads` reads a cached value instead of taking `thread_lock`. 91644d27d6 2024-10-31 makes `DestroyBufferUpon` atomic. • f1a924ae29 2025-09-18 Compression-function lookup does one atomic check instead of several locks. 6f6557866f keeps the connection count in an atomic. • 3548e15dba 2026-10-21 A `has_disabled_optimizers` atomic lets `IsOptimizerDisabled` skip the database-wide mutex. 70005f2596 2026-11-02 removes four global locks, one of which was `CreateLogger` locking even with logging off. Mutex wait time with 16 connections went from 34.4% to 0.0%. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-099): e28b43a739 2016-06-01 `large_salloc()` and the profiling getters no longer lock `arena->large_mtx` to read immutable extent fields. • fd5f9e43c3 2015-05-15 `rtree_val_read(..., dependent)`: "Reading a val on behalf of a pointer to a valid allocation is guaranteed to be a clean read even without synchronization." 6c460ad91b 2016-03-22 also skips validity checks for dependent loads. • Also seen in NATS (nats-102): c7ec32b2e0 2013-08-16 "Make checking authTimer cheaper": `authSet := c.isAuthTimerSet()` is snapshotted once before the parse loop instead of a locked call per `OP_START` byte. • c097357b52 2020-01-09 `flushOutbound` captures `wdl := c.out.wdl` before unlocking for the write. 4402a9cca0 2020-10-15 `readLoop` snapshots `acc := c.acc` and uses `acc.hasMappings()` instead of the locking `c.Account()`, re-snapshotting "at end of each readloop since it can change during reload". • 20d250c7cf 2021-02-23 An immutable `stype` field lets `storeUpdates` read the storage type without locking. • 218584d394 2023-10-21 Pinned-account routes resolve the account once at connect. The inbound path uses it directly instead of a lookup per cache miss, and drops the read lock around `acc.sl`, since reload no longer swaps it. • 63204b79ae 2024-05-09 WebSocket client info no longer takes `websocket.mu` and parses `listener.Addr()` per client. Host, port and TLS are set once at startup. • 67d0bc2e64 2025-01-07 Raft `ID()`, `Group()` and `Created()` drop the read lock: "None of these are ever mutated after the group is created".

### duckdb-137 — Keep hot writes core-local: per-thread accumulators, per-CPU counters, padded lines
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB · Also: Kafka, Pebble, Netty
Principle: Do not have every thread write to the same counter or cache line in an inner loop. Accumulate in thread- or CPU-local slots, fold into the shared value rarely, and pad independently written fields onto separate cache lines.
Why it is faster: Each write to a shared line invalidates it in every other core's cache, so a contended atomic costs hundreds of cycles. False sharing does the same to unrelated fields that happen to share a 64-byte line. Local accumulation removes the traffic, at the cost of a slightly stale global value.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Striped counters for metrics (Java `LongAdder`, sharded Prometheus client counters). • Native / systems: Disruptor-style ring buffers with padded sequence numbers, and `alignas(64)` on producer and consumer indices. • Embedded: Per-core packet statistics in NIC drivers, summed on read.
Watch out: Padding wastes memory, so apply it only to measured hot fields. Readers of folded counters must tolerate approximation, which is fine for accounting thresholds but not for exact limits.
Seen in DuckDB: e06e89d336 2024-07-08 Memory-usage accounting keeps per-CPU deltas and folds them into the global atomic only past 32 KB (`kCacheThreshold`), read with relaxed loads. • d107e0cfd6 2026-07-14 Probe-match counts stay local and are flushed once per exhausted chain: JOB 16b 192 → 95 ms, and inner-join `Next` fell from 18% to 3.7% of runtime. 527d0f819f 2026-10-26 isolates the probe counter to avoid false sharing. • 84a00ebe93 2026-11-11 Updates and pads the vendored concurrent queue: 2.55× throughput with 1 producer and 191 consumers, and 22% less CPU. • 300378dec7 2026-02-19 Cached global settings become `thread_local`, removing `shared_ptr` refcount bouncing on many-core machines. • Also seen in Kafka (kafka-113): d9ee9c96dd 2024-05-25 Streams `MeteredKeyValueStore` open-iterator counters moved from `AtomicInteger` to `LongAdder` so interactive-query threads stop contending on a metric. • Also seen in Pebble (pebble-125): 62b926b730 2018-06-30 Added `_ [6]uint64` padding to `commitQueueNode`; `commitQueue` already padded between its read index, write index and nodes. • cd6ba1c8cc 2024-10-23 `shardedCategoryStats`: 16 shards, each padded to 64 bytes. d2480d711e 2024-10-23 resolves the category's stats once per `pebble.Iterator`: the path fell from 3.7% to 2.44% of CPU. • Also seen in Netty (netty-111): 42fba015ce 2016-10-21 rebuilt `ResourceLeakDetector` with `ThreadLocalRandom` sampling and a `ConcurrentHashMap`. Before, a gRPC QPS benchmark spent about 3 CPU-seconds per wall-second in it with 12 threads. 74adbcd25d 2017-09-18 then removed all locks: it records onto a lock-free stack with exponentially falling probability, about 20 records per 1M accesses. • 2791f0fefa 2019-09-24 moved the `ScheduledFutureTask` id from a static `AtomicLong` to a plain per-executor counter assigned at enqueue: scheduleLots(100k) went from 346 to 655 ops/s. • 6a11330061 2022-01-12 switched the pinned-memory counter from `AtomicInteger` to a `LongAdder`-based counter. 80c9a41c25 2025-08-21 added a LongAdder `ChunkRegistry`, and ae6017d555 2025-09-17 removed the duplicate per-magazine `AtomicLong usedMemory`, because chunk creation and destruction already account for it.

### duckdb-138 — Publish to shared structures in batches, or sort first to coalesce lock acquisitions
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB · Also: jemalloc
Principle: Do not take a shared lock per chunk or per row. Buffer per worker and publish whole batches, defer leftovers to a parallel finalize, or sort random-order updates so one acquisition covers many neighbouring items.
Why it is faster: Lock cost is per acquisition, not per item. Batching spreads it over many items. Sorting by location turns many acquisitions on scattered items into a few on contiguous runs. The cost is buffering memory and some extra latency before results become visible.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-thread log buffers flushed periodically. Batched writes to a shared cache. • Native / systems: Sort cache invalidations by shard. Batch inserts into concurrent maps. • Data / databases: Combiners before a shuffle. Sorted bulk index maintenance.
Watch out: Batches delay visibility and need a guaranteed final flush. Unbounded buffering per worker multiplies memory by thread count.
Seen in DuckDB: 0ed5fcceec 2023-03-28 Successive hash tables double in capacity, so finalize steps, which touch shared state, grow only logarithmically with data size. • ee7d48d883 2026-09-01 Recursive UNION buffers keyed rows per worker and pre-aggregates duplicates before the shared hash table. • 9b69a2d305 2026-08-19 INSERT defers sub-row-group leftovers to Finalize and packs them in parallel: about 24–25s → 11–13s on a customer query. • 678f0ebab3 2026-11-17 UPDATE sorts out-of-order updates per thread so each storage call takes its locks once per run of about 21 rows. Statistics are locked once per chunk. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-104): 44d91cf243 2024-02-07 Tcache flush partitions by owner, which "avoids a full array scan (and any attendant branch prediction misses...) while holding the bin lock". • 2707050be8 2026-11-05 Skips the leading run that already matches so items are not swapped with themselves. 4415644451 2026-11-05 compares arena index plus bin shard with one masked `e_bits` compare (`edata_bin_owner_get`). • 7241bf5b74 2018-12-03 The flush path compares arena indices and resolves `arena_get(ind)` once per batch.

### duckdb-139 — Feed workers from one shared queue of long-lived tasks; block, don't spin
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Put every available unit of work (across partitions and pipelines) into one lock-free queue. Let each task loop to grab the next unit instead of spawning a task per item. Idle workers block on a notification instead of spinning.
Why it is faster: A global mutex on dispatch serializes scheduling. One task per item multiplies scheduling and allocation overhead. Processing partitions one at a time leaves threads idle on the small ones, while a single queue keeps all workers busy and allows stealing. Spinning burns a core that a producer could have used.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Worker pools that pull from a queue instead of one goroutine or future per item. `Atomics.wait` instead of busy loops in JS workers. • Native / systems: Work-stealing pools (Rayon, TBB, Java ForkJoin). Game-engine job systems. • Data / databases: Schedule tasks across all partitions and pipelines in one queue so skewed partitions do not stall the job.
Watch out: Blocking adds wake-up latency, and very short waits can still be cheaper to spin briefly. Long-lived looping tasks must check for cancellation.
Seen in DuckDB: ebd9140890 2020-07-10 `TaskScheduler` parks idle threads on `moodycamel::LightweightSemaphore`. 15b6cdaa4f 2020-09-10 switches to `moodycamel::ConcurrentQueue` with producer tokens. a8192a2a90 generates scan tasks incrementally instead of all up front. • 32567fffe8 2021-08-02 Merge tasks loop over the next pair instead of one task per pair ("spawn less tasks, re-use them"). • d58f34a7ef 2022-10-24 One queue holds sorting tasks for all window partitions, so several partitions sort at once. • 31c33ab3a3 2023-08-11 Window work stealing: idle threads take unfinished partitions or blocks. 26185140d2 2023-09-04 splits sorting into multiple tasks per partition, and 7ed219ef3b parallelizes single-partition sorting. • 976bcd29b7 2026-07-08 The task executor blocks on notification. 743c2acb15 actually blocks when no tasks are available, 1956d1b975 2026-07-21 makes `StorageLock` a phase-fair RW lock on a condition variable, and 2dc513f359 makes cancellation wait by notification.

### duckdb-140 — Right-size parallelism to the data and the memory
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Pick the thread count per operation from input size, available memory and plan shape. Run serially (and skip merge phases) when the input is small, when only one thread is active, or when a serial path can stop early.
Why it is faster: Parallelism has fixed costs: task creation, coordination, combine phases and per-thread buffers. For small inputs those costs exceed the speedup. Over-parallelizing a memory-hungry phase forces spilling or out-of-memory failures. A serial scan under LIMIT can stop as soon as it has enough rows, while a parallel one keeps reading.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Send work to a worker pool only above a payload threshold. Do not fan small or LIMIT requests out to every shard. • Native / systems: ForkJoin and parallel-stream sequential thresholds. Size transcoding pools by RAM, not core count. • Data / databases: Size executors from partition memory. Parallelize across files before splitting files.
Watch out: Thresholds tuned on one machine misfire on others, as 192-core regressions showed. Express thresholds in bytes per task and re-tune on many-core hardware.
Seen in DuckDB: e466d90508 2023-05-14 Uses the simpler single-threaded CSV reader per file when `files*2 >= threads`, and parallelizes across files instead. 2f186b8ad5 runs single-threaded when only one thread is available. • 6b317bdfd4 2023-08-02 With one active thread and no spill, the aggregate skips the combine phase and does not reset its table. • a491470b03 2023-12-21 Limits aggregate finalize threads to what fits in 60% of memory, sized from the largest partition. • 03011821d3 2024-02-20 Order-preserving COPY caps threads by memory, sets a minimum memory per thread by column count, and pauses threads. • 5304647326 2024-06-08 Avoids parallel batch LIMIT when the child is a plain scan. a595c8108a 2026-04-28 does use it when the scan has filters, since selectivity is unknown: `LIKE … LIMIT 2148` went from 21s to 1.5s. • 244951e707 2025-02-24 Parallel memset uses `count/threads/4` entries per task instead of a fixed 131072: a 100M-key build went from 0.40s to 0.29s. 300378dec7 2026-02-19 parallelizes destruction only above 1,048,576 entries. • 60e81d3a94 2026-05-15 Recursive CTEs size the thread count to the exactly known working set, single-threaded when it is tiny. • 149e6f0de6 2026-11-05 Hash-aggregate source threads need at least 256 KiB each (DISTINCT over 100 rows had been 2.4× slower multi-threaded). dd7388e121 2026-11-16 tunes aggregate partitions: ClickBench geomean 1.066× at 192 threads.

### duckdb-141 — Preserve order under parallelism with batch indexes and a minimum in-flight watermark
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Tag each input batch with a monotonically increasing index, let threads process batches in any order, and have the sink emit or flush batches in index order once the minimum index still in flight has passed them.
Why it is faster: Without this, order-sensitive queries (insertion order, ORDER BY output, ordered writes) must run serially. With it, the expensive work stays parallel and only a cheap reorder buffer is ordered. The buffer must be bounded or out-of-order batches pile up in memory.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Parallel downloads reassembled by chunk index. Concurrent paginated fetches emitted in page order. • Native / systems: Reorder buffers in network stacks. Parallel video encoders that must emit frames in order. • Data / databases: Ordered parallel map (`par_iter().collect()`). Sequence numbers in stream processors.
Watch out: One slow batch holds back everything after it, so buffered memory grows with skew. Cap it and pause producers. If order is not required, offer a flag to skip this entirely.
Seen in DuckDB: a25b6e3072 2022-05-25 Parallel order-preserving result materialization. 022f2a4bb0 introduces the batch index (also used for parallel LIMIT), and 09cf8f080a 2022-10-25 covers parallel order-preserving CTAS and INSERT. 3c7ecb9473 2022-11-21 reads sorted ORDER BY results in parallel. • 9b1d80a9ee 2023-05-04 Adds the minimum batch index: sinks flush once the global minimum passes them. b0677fb98a 2023-05-07 applies it to parallel Parquet writes, and 0e05f020e0 avoids repartitioning in `NextBatch` once threads are finishing. • 12b4c89827 2024-02-27 Reduces memory and avoids spilling for order-preserving CTAS and INSERT. 1c4775303b 2024-06-14 adds a batched, order-preserving streaming result. • 77f5ade107 2026-08-07 Ordered buffered CTE scans process batches in parallel by batch index. b97d697b02 2026-07-24 runs UNION ALL branches in parallel when order is not required.

### duckdb-142 — Hunt down the remaining serial phases
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: After the main loop is parallel, profile for single-threaded tails (checkpoints, data generation, destructors, memsets, schema inference, mask building, materialization) and turn each one into tasks.
Why it is faster: By Amdahl's law, a 5% serial phase caps speedup at 20× no matter how many cores you add. On 64–192 core machines, freeing a multi-GB hash table or zeroing it on one thread can take longer than the parallel query. Splitting it into a few coarse tasks per thread keeps memory access sequential within each task.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Generate fixtures in parallel. Make C libraries reentrant (`strtok_r`-style context instead of globals). • Native / systems: Game engines that free level assets on worker threads. Release large arenas in background tasks. • Data / databases: Infer schemas over many files in parallel. Run compaction per segment as tasks.
Watch out: Parallelizing small cleanups adds task overhead, so keep a size threshold. Parallel destructors must not race with anything that still holds references.

### duckdb-143 — Push-based pipelines with explicit sinks; stream instead of materializing
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Structure execution as pipelines: a source produces chunks that are pushed through operators into a sink, and each pipeline owns local and global state. Run independent pipelines concurrently, schedule them to bound memory, and stream into consumers instead of materializing where possible.
Why it is faster: Pull-based iterators keep control flow implicit in deep call stacks, which makes parallelism, early exit and batching hard. A central executor makes them easy: it can cache small chunks, stop a source when the sink is done, and schedule pipelines across threads. Materializing an intermediate creates a pipeline breaker plus a write and read. Breadth-first scheduling keeps many intermediates alive at once, while depth-first caps the working set.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pipe Node streams instead of buffering them. Use reactive fan-out (RxJS `share`). Yield cooperatively on the event loop in small quanta. • Native / systems: Game-engine job graphs. Unix pipes. • Data / databases: DAG schedulers that prefer depth-first to cap memory. Build systems that limit memory-hungry jobs running at once.
Watch out: Streaming fan-out couples producer and consumer speeds, so a slow consumer stalls everyone unless buffers are bounded. Depth-first scheduling under-uses cores when individual pipelines are small.

### duckdb-144 — Make sequential-looking work splittable: search for split points, resync at boundaries
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Find independent split points so threads need no coordination during the work. Binary-search the merge-path diagonal of two sorted runs, jump to a byte offset and resynchronize to the next true record boundary, or compute each block's starting ordinal from its row count.
Why it is faster: Merging, text parsing and numbering look inherently serial. A cheap search (O(log n), or a short scan to the next newline) gives each thread an equal, independent slice, so the remaining work runs fully parallel with no locks. Boundary detection must be robust: quotes can hide newlines.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Parallel JSONL or log ingestion from byte offsets. Parallel line counting. • Native / systems: GPU merge kernels (Merge Path). Resynchronizing on frame markers in streaming protocol decoders. • Data / databases: Parallel ID assignment using a prefix sum of per-shard counts.
Watch out: Heuristic boundary detection can mis-split pathological inputs (quoted newlines everywhere). Validate, and fall back to serial when it fails.
Seen in DuckDB: 900ee89604 2021-05-27 Full ORDER BY parallelism with Merge Path: `ComputeIntersection` gives each task an equal output slice of the merge. 47599043f0 2025-05-28 rewrites sorting as a k-way merge, again split with Merge Path: 8-thread scaling 6.5× versus 3.5× before. • 3261e00aa2 2022-11-10 Parallel CSV reader: each thread jumps to its offset, finds the next newline, and test-parses a line to confirm it is a record start, moving on if not. 412d8c5074 2022-12-21 also parallelizes across files. • 336509ed7a 2024-10-08 Robust newline finding: try at most three start states (STANDARD, QUOTED, ESCAPED) and take the earliest consistent boundary, which bounds the work on invalid data. 31a2467e30 adds validation. • 161b745400 2026-04-09 `row_number() OVER ()` becomes a virtual column computed per row group from its visible-row count, so numbering runs in parallel: 1.6s → 0.12s.

### duckdb-145 — Replace locked ordered structures with a concurrent queue plus lazy invalidation
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Instead of keeping an exact LRU list under a global lock, push (weak handle, version) entries onto a lock-free queue on each use. Skip stale entries when popping, and purge dead entries opportunistically.
Why it is faster: An exact LRU needs a lock on every access to move a node. With versioned entries, an access becomes a lock-free enqueue, and eviction validates entries lazily by version. The order is approximate and the queue can hold dead entries, so it needs periodic purging to stay bounded.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: CLOCK or lazy-deletion LRU in HTTP caches. Timer wheels with lazy cancellation. • Native / systems: Page-cache eviction in embedded databases. Priority queues with tombstones (Dijkstra with lazy deletion).
Watch out: Without purging, a hot set of re-pinned blocks floods the queue with stale entries and memory grows. Approximate order can evict slightly wrong victims.
Seen in DuckDB: 7251a55379 2020-12-08 Buffer eviction pushes `BufferEvictionNode{weak_ptr, timestamp}`. `CanUnload()` checks that the timestamp matches and no readers remain, and pinning takes only the block's own lock. Merged as 13b80d7042. • d48e5c34d0 2022-06-17 `PurgeQueue()` runs on enqueue and dequeues dead entries until it finds a live one, so the queue does not grow without bound. • 8c40a99d4d 2025-06-19 The eviction queue sorts purged nodes and re-adds them in bulk.

### duckdb-146 — Copy-on-write snapshots so maintenance does not block readers or writers
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Do not mutate a shared structure in place under a lock. Build a new version that reuses unchanged nodes, swap a pointer at the end, and let readers keep their snapshot.
Why it is faster: Readers never wait for the writer, and the writer never waits for readers to drain. The only synchronized step is one atomic pointer swap. The cost is allocating new nodes for the changed parts, plus reclamation (here, reference counting) of old versions.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Hot-reload config by swapping an atomic pointer. Immutable state updates (Redux, Immutable.js). • Native / systems: MVCC B-trees (LMDB). RCU in kernels. • Data / databases: Snapshot-isolated metadata trees for background compaction.
Watch out: Long-lived readers pin old versions and their memory. Large changes copy a lot. Reclamation must be safe (refcounts or epochs).
Seen in DuckDB: 1f90f7d393 2025-11-21 A checkpoint builds a new `RowGroupSegmentTree` that references unchanged row groups, while readers hold `shared_ptr` snapshots. This removes the table lock in `TableScanInitGlobal`. • 880ae8d1f5 2025-12-05 Commits continue during a checkpoint: the checkpoint is pinned to a commit id and new commits go to a fresh WAL. f38e0ff7c3 has inserts take a shared table lock instead of the checkpoint lock.

### duckdb-147 — Summarize concurrency metadata per block so the common case is one check
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Keep per-block summary flags (anything deleted? all rows written by one transaction?) so visibility for a whole batch is decided with one comparison instead of per-row version checks. Collapse uniform per-row version arrays to a single constant.
Why it is faster: Most blocks are untouched or were written by one transaction, so per-row checks repeat the same answer many times. A summary flag answers for all rows at once. A constant replaces an 8-byte-per-row array, which saves memory and scan time.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: An all-visible bit per page (as in Postgres's visibility map). A "no tombstones in this block" flag in SSTables. • Web / backend: Dirty bits on UI tree nodes or cached aggregates, so clean subtrees skip re-validation.
Watch out: Flags must be maintained on every write path. A missed update gives wrong visibility, which is a correctness bug, not just a slowdown.
Seen in DuckDB: 8338db9943 2019-05-27 A dirty flag on segments skips version-info lookups for unchanged data. • aef4f15d7f 2020-09-10 `any_deleted` and `all_same_id` in chunk info, with a four-way fast path in `GetSelVector`. • 929a0e336b 2026-07-22 `constant_delete_id`: deleting 100M rows had retained about 815 MB of per-row ids. 1da80a4a3a 2026-08-06 collapses fully visible partial deletes to a bitmask of about 256 B: 82 MiB → 0.75 MiB.

### duckdb-148 — Give each thread its own copy of internally locked or shared objects
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Library objects that lock internally (regex engines, RNGs, formatters) or are shared through atomic refcounts serialize threads. Build one per thread-local state instead.
Why it is faster: The hidden lock or refcount inside a "thread-safe" object becomes a contention point that does not show up in your own code. Per-thread copies cost a little memory and setup and remove that contention completely.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Thread-local `DateFormat`, `Random` or `MessageDigest` in Java. Per-worker compiled regexes in Node workers. • Native / systems: Release the GIL (`Py_BEGIN_ALLOW_THREADS`) in long native extension calls. Use per-thread allocators.
Watch out: Heavy objects multiplied by many threads cost memory, and their per-thread warm-up is repeated. Releasing the GIL requires that no Python objects are touched while it is released.
Seen in DuckDB: 0596e4fe1f 2021-09-28 Regex functions get a `RegexLocalState` holding a private RE2 instance, through a new `init_local_state` hook. • 8e3c36cc85 2021-02-09 The Python client releases the GIL around query execution, so Python threads run alongside DuckDB's workers. • 300378dec7 2026-02-19 Settings are cached per thread instead of through a shared `shared_ptr`.

### duckdb-149 — Cache- and NUMA-affine work assignment
Lens: concurrency · Source: DuckDB
Principle: On many-socket machines, give each worker the morsels whose data sits in its caches or on its memory node. Keep per-worker scan state, and steal only to balance load.
Why it is faster: Remote-node memory access is slower and uses interconnect bandwidth. Re-reading blocks another core just cached wastes the cache. Affinity keeps most accesses local, and stealing keeps load balanced.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Thread-per-core servers (Seastar). NUMA-pinned shards in Redis or Kafka deployments. • Native / systems: Locality-aware task schedulers with steal-on-idle.
Watch out: This only pays on large multi-socket machines, and strict affinity hurts under skew. Keep stealing enabled.
Seen in DuckDB: 527d0f819f 2026-10-26 NUMA-aware join and aggregation align buckets with radix partitions on nearby CPU groups: Q09 -36.1% and Q18 -16.1% at 192 threads. • 1305195445 2026-11-17 Scan affinity following the morsel-driven design of Leis et al.: TPC-H SF704 geomean 1.067× on 192 cores, Q06 1.37×.

### duckdb-150 — Profile first, then attack the top line with the right counter
Lens: observability · Source: DuckDB · Also: curl, Netty, PostgreSQL
Principle: Before optimizing, measure where the cost actually goes, and choose the metric that matches the suspected bottleneck: CPU share per function, instructions retired, allocation count, lock-wait time, page faults or kernel time.
Why it is faster: Intuition picks the wrong target. A 5% function rewritten 2× faster saves 2.5%, while a hidden 30% hot spot saves far more. Different bottlenecks show up in different counters. Allocator contention appears as allocation count, not bytes. Lock problems appear as wait time, not CPU. OS overhead appears as minor faults and system time. Instruction counts are less noisy than wall time for micro-changes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Flame graphs (perf, pprof, clinic.js) before touching code. Track allocations per request (Go `-benchmem`, Node heap snapshots). • Embedded: Cycle counters (DWT on Cortex-M) around candidate hot loops instead of guessing. • Native / systems: Use `perf stat` instructions and cache misses for micro-changes. Use off-CPU or lock profiling for contention. Watch `minor-faults` for allocator and mmap churn. • Data / databases: Report the per-operator share of time from the engine's own profiler.
Watch out: Fewer instructions do not always mean less time, because memory stalls dominate many loops. Confirm with wall time on a realistic workload. Profiles taken at low thread counts miss many-core contention.

### duckdb-151 — Commit a reproducible benchmark with each optimization
Lens: observability · Source: DuckDB · Also: NATS, Tokio, Netty
Principle: Every performance change ships with a small, named benchmark that exercises exactly the targeted path, kept in the repository and re-run later. Report standard-suite numbers (TPC-H, TPC-DS, ClickBench, IMDB/JOB) next to the micro result.
Why it is faster: A committed benchmark turns a claim into something anyone can re-run. It documents which workload a change was meant for and lets future changes see what they break. Micro-benchmarks isolate the mechanism, and suite geomeans catch side effects elsewhere. Benchmarks also supply constants, so tuning knobs come from data instead of guesses.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Keep `benchmark.js`, Go `testing.B` or JMH files next to the code. Load-test scripts (k6) live in the repository. • Embedded: Cycle-count tests for ISR and DSP routines, run in CI on hardware or an emulator. • Native / systems: Use criterion or google-benchmark suites, and report geomeans across a standard set. • Data / databases: Pair each optimizer rule with a query that triggers it.
Watch out: Micro-benchmarks overfit: a loop that wins in isolation can lose inside a real pipeline. Always check a broad suite too, and keep benchmark inputs realistic in size and distribution.

### duckdb-152 — Treat measured regressions as bugs: detect, bisect, revert or gate
Lens: observability · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Run suites continuously, and when a change slows a workload, fix it, revert it, or gate the optimization behind a condition. Say in the commit which cases regressed and choose tuning constants by where regressions begin.
Why it is faster: Optimizations often trade one workload for another, and heuristics produce cliffs. Without regression tracking, gains erode and slowdowns pile up unnoticed. Explicitly gating an optimization (do X only when it helps) usually keeps most of the win without the loss.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Performance budgets in CI (Lighthouse, bundle-size checks). Track p95 latency per release. • Embedded: Gate firmware merges on binary size and worst-case loop timing. • Native / systems: Benchmark dashboards per commit, with bisection on slowdowns. • Data / databases: Add guard conditions (thresholds, statistics checks) so an optimizer rule fires only where it wins.
Watch out: Noise creates false alarms. Use repeated runs, stable machines and relative thresholds. Old tricks stop paying when hardware or compilers change, so re-validate them instead of keeping them forever.

### duckdb-153 — Measure at runtime and adapt (explore / exploit)
Lens: observability · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Where the best choice depends on the data or the environment (predicate order, compression level, whether a filter pays off), time or count the alternatives while running, keep the winner, and keep exploring occasionally.
Why it is faster: Static estimates of selectivity, I/O contention or bandwidth are often wrong, while live measurements are accurate for this input on this machine. Periodic exploration lets the choice track changing conditions. The overhead is timing calls plus some exploration runs that lose, so measure per batch, not per row.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Bandit ordering of middleware checks. Compress responses only when measured bandwidth is the bottleneck. Adaptive batch sizes for queue consumers. • Native / systems: Pick the fastest codec per stream at runtime. Carry tuning state across similar inputs, the way a JIT keeps profile data. • Data / databases: Runtime filter on/off switches. Adaptive join or probe order in search engines.
Watch out: Timing noise can cause oscillation, so use moving averages and hysteresis. Adaptive behavior makes performance harder to reproduce, so expose the chosen strategy in diagnostics.

### duckdb-154 — Keep instrumentation always available but free when off; expose engine decisions
Lens: observability · Source: DuckDB
Principle: Build profiler names, debug strings and loggers only when profiling or logging is enabled. Surface the engine's internal choices and I/O counters in its own diagnostics, so tuning works from facts.
Why it is faster: Eager instrumentation costs on every query even when no one is looking: string formatting, allocations, and locks in a logger that is switched off. Lazy construction removes that cost. Exposing request counts, chosen codecs and exact structure sizes makes regressions and misconfiguration visible without a debugger.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Lazy log formatting (`logger.debug(() => …)`). Build metric labels only when the metric is enabled. Expose cache hit rates and upstream call counts per request. • Embedded: Compile-time trace levels, with format strings stored as IDs. • Data / databases: Show chosen plans, codecs and I/O counts in EXPLAIN-style output.
Watch out: A check for "is tracing enabled?" taken under a lock is itself a contention point. Make it an atomic or immutable read.

### duckdb-155 — Prove fast paths equal the slow path
Lens: observability · Source: DuckDB
Principle: When a specialized or closed-form implementation replaces a general one, assert in debug builds that both give the same result, or test the replacement exhaustively over its parameter space.
Why it is faster: Fast paths are where subtle bugs hide (edge cases, overflow, encoding assumptions). Checking equivalence lets you be aggressive with confidence, and avoids the expensive pattern of shipping a shortcut and then removing it after a bug report.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Property-based tests (fast-check, Hypothesis) comparing an optimized function with a reference implementation. • Embedded: Exhaustively test lookup tables and fixed-point approximations over every input. • Native / systems: Differential fuzzing of SIMD or SWAR kernels against scalar code.
Watch out: Debug-only assertions do not run in production, so pair them with tests that cover the real input distribution, including empty, maximum-length and non-ASCII cases.
Seen in DuckDB: 219bafadb7 2025-02-18 The branchless short-string hash asserts that it equals the generic `Hash(data,len)`. • 2569474e67 2026-10-19 The closed-form `FixedSizeAllocator` layout (1,161 → 14 loop iterations) was verified exhaustively over 8.1M parameter combinations. • e3740f5b40 2020-06-30 Removed an incorrect ASCII shortcut in the pandas scan (Latin-1 is not ASCII). This is the failure mode that equivalence checks prevent.
